# Plan: real-time 3D EM wave simulator as a cross-platform KiCAD plugin

Status: **working prototype on macOS** (GPU-drawn views and UI since 2026-11-20, see "GPU rendering"; CI green on `c91367f`; later commits checked locally with `scripts/check.sh`, see Next steps 18) (snapshot -> voxelizer -> CPU and Vulkan solver with CPML and lumped ports -> S11 and an animated field view in a standalone window; KiCAD plugin entry point written, untested inside KiCAD). Plan restarted 2026-11-19 and updated through the same session with measurements, findings from the pg-strom and mongo sessions and probes of the Windows box. Scope is this repository only. The sections below were written as a plan; "Measured so far" and "Next steps" record what has been built.
Example board: the user's own KiCAD design in `../bomu-hardware/bomu`, used as read-only test input. By the user's instruction nothing from that repository is copied or committed here.

## Focus
**Make it work on this Mac first** (Apple M1 Pro, MoltenVK, local KiCAD). Windows and Linux support, the Windows eGPU, CI, packaging, signing and PCM distribution come later. Keep the code portable (no Apple-only shortcuts), but do not build, test or tune for other platforms yet.

## Goal
Simulate electromagnetic waves on a KiCAD board in 3D on the GPU and show the fields live in 3D, packaged as a KiCAD plugin that runs on macOS, Windows and Linux.
**First target:** the 2.4 GHz (WiFi band) antenna path on the example board.

## Decisions so far
- Solver method: **FDTD** (finite-difference time-domain, Maxwell's equations stepped in time on a 3D grid).
- Compute and graphics API: **Vulkan 1.2 baseline** (a Vulkan-on-Metal driver on macOS: MoltenVK today, possibly KosmicKrisp later; see "GPU rendering"). Not CUDA, so that Apple, AMD, Intel and NVIDIA GPUs all work.
- Cross-platform is the eventual goal (macOS, Windows, Linux), but macOS comes first.
- License: **MIT** (decided 2026-11-19). See Licensing below for dependencies, the clean-room rule and open patent items.
- Plugin type: **KiCAD IPC API plugin**. Legacy SWIG `pcbnew` plugins are deprecated; do not use them.

**Copyleft projects added to the clean-room list (read behaviour and documentation only, never source):** EMerge (FennisRobert, GPL-2.0) and kicad2ems (lowprocess, GPL-3.0), alongside openEMS, MEEP and gprMax above. Palace (Apache-2.0) has lumped L and C in a finite-element code: its documentation may be read for behaviour only. Their published behaviour (kicad2ems dumps E, H and J fields and computes far-field patterns, per its forum post) is a feature reference, not a template.

Not yet checked: fonts or other assets if the viewer bundles any (record their licenses when added), KiCAD itself, and transitive dependencies of kipy (protobuf, pynng and others).
All of the above are compatible with distributing this project under MIT. Apache-2.0 components bring a notice and patent-grant obligation when redistributed (keep their license and NOTICE files in any bundle).

**Clean-room rule for algorithms and code.** The mature open-source FDTD and EM solvers are GPL: openEMS (GPL-3.0), MEEP (GPL-2.0) and gprMax (GPL-3.0), as detected from their repositories. We may run them for validation and read their documentation for behaviour. We never copy, translate or paraphrase their source, and we do not use it as an implementation template. Kernels are written from the published papers in References and from first principles. The same applies to any other copyleft or unlicensed code, including the EM tools in sibling projects on this machine.

**KiCAD PCM license requirement (from the official addons documentation, dev-docs.kicad.org/en/addons).** Packages in the official repository must be under an open-source license; packages containing code must use one compatible with the GNU GPL. MIT satisfies this. The `license` field in `metadata.json` is validated; the documentation says the v2 schema accepts any non-empty string but the official repository still enforces the open-source requirement. Open item: the documentation says official packages must be open source "to allow for code review". Whether a prebuilt native binary inside the package is acceptable to the maintainers is not stated there; ask the KiCAD addons maintainers before relying on bundling binaries. Third-party repositories and direct builds have no such review.

**Patents (open).** Conformal and subcell treatments of curved metal have been commercialised (for example CST's "perfect boundary approximation"). Whether any patent covers the specific method we would implement has NOT been checked. This is not legal advice. Rule: the first versions use staircased geometry plus a published thin-sheet model [8], with no conformal scheme. Before implementing any conformal scheme (the Dey-Mittra method [9] is the candidate), check Google Patents or the USPTO for patents naming the technique, and have the result reviewed by someone qualified. A paper's age does not decide patent status. **Check run 2026-11-24 (not a clearance):** general web searches for Dey-Mittra, CST's perfect boundary approximation (partially filled cells; Krietenstein, Schuhmann, Thoma, Weiland 1998), Yu-Mittra and Yee thin-wire methods returned only papers and no patent. Patent full-text search was not reachable: freepatentsonline.com refused automated fetches (403) and the Google Patents query endpoint was refused by the permission layer (not worked around). Four FDTD patents were read on their Google Patents pages: US7089130B2 (Fujitsu, boundary on a cell face approximated by centre-line segments, covers PEC/dielectric and dielectric/dielectric interfaces), US6772076B2 / US20030204343A1 and JP3703812B2 (a thin conductor as a line splitting cell faces; Yamamoto, Iki), US6711511B2 (net smoothing for EMI analysis, off topic). All four show as expired for non-payment of fees; none claims a Dey-Mittra or PBA update. *What can be said without legal judgement:* a 20-year term from filing means a patent filed on a method first published in 1997 would have run out by about 2018, and a later patent by others cannot validly claim the published method itself, only improvements or implementations; whether a given implementation infringes something newer is exactly what has NOT been established. The rule above stands: no conformal scheme before review by someone qualified. *Consequence for this project:* none is needed so far, because the TI antenna's mesh drift came from a missing snap (see the iteration below), not from curved metal.

**Data we read.** The example board in `../bomu-hardware/bomu` is authored by this project's user, so they decide its license. Your instruction stands: it is read-only input and nothing from that repository is copied or committed here. Third-party files inside that design (vendor 3D models such as the antenna STEP, datasheets, library footprints) carry their own licenses, which the board author does not control, so they would need checking before any of them were ever shipped with this project. If test boards are wanted in this repo, author new ones here (or add a deliberate, explicitly licensed copy later, only on your say-so).
**Reference antenna board.** `boards/w3008-testboard/` is our own KiCAD design of the 80 x 37 mm datasheet test board for the Pulse/Larsen W3008, authored from dimensions only (sources, derived coordinates, assumptions and the DRC result are in its README). The Pulse datasheet pages carry a confidentiality and no-reproduction notice, and whether publishing or fabricating a derived layout is permitted has not been confirmed with Pulse, so it is a private validation reference that the project owner will not publish. It must be excluded from any public release or public push of this repository. Datasheets and the vendor 3D model are not stored in this repository.

## Product requirement: works on the user's own design
The plugin is a tool for anyone to run on **their own board in KiCAD**. The example board and the W3008 reference board are only test cases; nothing in the plugin or solver may be specific to them (no hard-coded part, net, layer, board size or antenna).
- **Input comes from the open board:** outline, stackup (layers, thicknesses, er, tan delta, copper weight as set in KiCAD's board setup), copper (tracks, pads, vias, filled zones), and footprint positions, all read through the IPC API. When the stackup lacks material values, use documented defaults and show the user what was assumed.
- **User choices in the UI, with sensible defaults:** the frequency range; the excitation (pick a port as two pads or pad and net, with a reference impedance, or a plane-wave or dipole source); the region of interest (crop to a box or selected footprints plus margin, since simulating a whole large board at fine resolution does not fit in memory); which components count as metal or dielectric bodies; boundary distance.
- **The user selects which components take part, separately for the model and for the view.** From the footprints, nets and layers of their own board the user chooses (a) what is in the electromagnetic model and (b) what is drawn in the 3D view. These are two independent selections, so a part can be modelled but hidden, or shown for context but left out of the model. Each included component gets a treatment the user can override: metal body, dielectric block, lumped element (a resistor, inductor or capacitor with its value), or ignored; defaults depend on the footprint type, and a part without a 3D model falls back to its courtyard box with a warning. Selections can start from what is selected in the PCB editor, from a list with filters (by reference, value, net, layer, region), or from presets such as "everything within the region of interest". The choice is stored in a sidecar file next to the board, never in the KiCAD design files, and the run summary lists what was included and how. Whether `kipy` 0.8.0 exposes the editor's current selection is unverified and part of the probe step.
- **Resource limits are reported, not hidden:** estimate cells and memory from the chosen region and resolution before running; coarsen automatically or tell the user how to shrink the region; refuse with a clear message rather than crash.
- **Unsupported features are detected and named** (for example blind and buried vias, curved or complex zones, component bodies without 3D models), with the simplification used. Nothing is silently dropped.
- **Results go back to the user:** viewer for fields, S-parameters exported as Touchstone, and a summary of the assumptions used so results are reproducible. Suggestions are shown, never applied to the design automatically; the plugin never modifies the board.
- **Version tolerance:** target the KiCAD 10 IPC API through `kipy`, report the version in use, and fail with a clear message on an unsupported version.
- **Testing consequence:** besides the W3008 board and the user's example board, add at least one board of a different shape, layer count and part (for example a plain 2-layer board with a microstrip line), so we do not tune the pipeline to one design.

## Defaults and editable attributes
Every physical attribute the model needs has a default and the user can change it. Nothing is hard-coded in the solver or the plugin.
- **One data file holds the defaults:** `data/fab_profiles.json` (materials, and fabrication profiles with their limits). The solver app, the plugin and the board generators read it; no second copy in code. Each value records its `source`, and values no fab publishes are marked `assumed`.
- **Built-in profiles (fab pages read 2026-11-19 and 2026-11-20, linked in the file), one per copper-layer count, all ENIG FR4-class:** OSH Park 2-layer (one 60 mil 175Tg FR4 core, Dk 4.5 at 10 MHz), 4-layer (FR408HR: 7.87 mil prepreg, 39 mil core, 7.87 mil prepreg, Dk 3.61 at 1 GHz) and 6-layer (FR408HR: 4.36 mil 106 prepreg, 4 mil 2113 core, 36.44 mil core, 4 mil 2113 core, 4.36 mil 106 prepreg; Dk 3.23 to 3.66 and Df 0.009 to 0.011 at 1 GHz where published); PCBWay 2-layer (a single 1.53 mm core, derived: PCBWay publishes no 2-layer stackup) and 4-layer (entry 1 of its stackup table: 1.6 mm, 7628 prepreg 0.1855 mm Dk 4.74, core 1.03 mm Dk 4.6, no frequency stated); and **`common_denominator`**, the default for the reference boards. Laminate values come from the datasheets OSH Park links (read 2026-11-20): Kingboard KB-6167F (OSH Park 2-layer: Dk 4.6, Df 0.016 at 1 GHz; the OSH Park page itself says Dk 4.5 at 10 MHz) and Isola FR408HR (Dk 3.69, Df 0.0091 at 1 GHz, used for the cores whose Dk the page omits and for the prepreg Df). Still assumed and named in each layer's `assumed` list: the FR408HR prepreg loss tangent (laminate value used), and all PCBWay values that are not Dk (Df 0.016, the KB-6167F figure; the 2-layer thickness and Dk). PCBWay's page counts 112 four-layer stackups of 298 but the 6-layer tables could not be read (its 6-layer example is an image), so there is no PCBWay 6-layer profile; PCBWay publishes no 2-layer stackup, no Df and no Dk frequency, and lists ENIG with no layer-count restriction. Fab limits (track, space, drill, ring) stay in each profile.
- **Assumed material values:** FR-4 er 4.5 and tan delta 0.02 (neither fab states them; FR408 is given the same until a datasheet figure is entered), solder mask er 3.5 and 0.01 mm thick, bulk copper conductivity 5.8e7 S/m (real PCB copper is lower), copper-to-board-edge clearance 0.3 mm, absorbing-boundary distance a quarter wavelength (to be confirmed by convergence tests). These are labelled assumed in the file and in the run summary.
- **Precedence (highest wins):** a value the user sets in the plugin dialog, then a value in the board's own KiCAD stackup (read through the API, used when it carries a real number), then the selected fabrication profile, then the built-in default. The user's overrides are stored in the sidecar file next to the board (never in the KiCAD design files), so they travel with the project.
- **What the user can edit (built 2026-11-20, Settings, group PCB):** "PCB stackup" (the board's own stackup, the default, or any fab profile) and, for up to 7 dielectric layers counted from the top, thickness, permittivity and loss tangent (0 keeps the board's value), plus outer and inner copper thickness (recorded only: copper is a zero-thickness sheet in the model). A profile starts the values; typing one overrides it. A profile for another copper-layer count is refused with a note, not applied silently; typed values still apply. Every change is listed in the run notes with its origin (your value, profile, assumed). Still to do from the list below: solder mask, finish, board-edge clearance and the layer materials by name, and a per-layer table in place of the slots. Original list: the profile choice; per-layer thickness, copper weight, material name, er, tan delta and conductivity; solder mask; finish; the board edge clearance; the boundary distance; the grid resolution rules. Edits apply to the run only, and the run summary lists every value used and where it came from (user, board, profile or default).
- **Why a fab profile matters to the simulation:** the stackup and copper weight set the dielectric heights and conductor thickness in the model, and the fab limits tell the voxelizer which features are real (for example a gap below the profile's minimum is flagged), so results match the board the user would actually order.
- **Open items:** the er and tan delta values to use for a given fab's FR-4 and FR408 (get the laminate datasheets and add them with sources), and whether to add more fab profiles (for example JLCPCB) later.

## Architecture
```
KiCAD PCB editor -> thin Python IPC plugin (kipy) -> board snapshot -> native C++/Vulkan app
                                                     (voxelizer -> FDTD compute (SPIR-V) -> 3D viewer GLFW + ImGui)
                                                     backends: Vulkan GPU | CPU fallback
```
- The plugin is a separate process (`plugin.json`, toolbar action) that exports a snapshot of the board.
- The solver and viewer is a separate native app with its own window (a plugin cannot draw inside KiCAD's window). It must also run standalone without KiCAD.
- One snapshot format (for example JSON plus a binary blob) is shared by the plugin and the C++ app.
- A CPU backend sits behind the same interface as the GPU one, for CI, machines without a GPU, and cross-checking GPU results.
- Shaders are GLSL compiled to SPIR-V at build time with `glslc`.
- Build: CMake and C++20. Dependencies (GLFW, Dear ImGui, VMA, volk) come from vcpkg or FetchContent.

## FDTD design
- Yee grid [1]: E and H update kernels (3D dispatch, shared-memory tiling), absorbing boundaries (CPML [4][5]), a lumped port or soft source (3D lumped-element formulation [7]; the 1992 paper [6] is 2D), running DFT accumulators at chosen frequencies.
  A Gaussian pulse drive plus a DFT of port voltage and current gives S11 over the whole band from one run.
- **Graded (non-uniform) grid:** the grid is made of small boxes (cells). A uniform 0.1 mm grid over a whole board is about 160M cells (about 4 GB in fp32, about 12 steps/s): too slow.
  A uniform 0.25 mm grid is about 10M cells (roughly 200 steps/s) but is too coarse for thin (about 0.1 mm) board layers. A graded grid uses fine cells where detail matters (through the
  stackup in z, and near the antenna in xy, about 0.1 to 0.2 mm) and grows to about 1 mm toward the boundary. Thin copper is modelled as PEC or as thin conducting sheets with a subcell model [8].
  Timing figures are estimates from memory bandwidth and must be measured.
  Accuracy: on a non-uniform Yee grid the local truncation error drops to first order where neighbouring cell sizes differ, but convergence analysis exists for the scheme [10]. We have NOT verified a numeric grading-ratio limit from the literature (only the paper's bibliographic record was checked, not its contents). Rule until then: grade smoothly (neighbouring cells differ by a small factor), never jump in size at a material interface, and decide the ratio from our own convergence tests (halve the grid and compare S11 and resonance).
- **Time step (correction):** the explicit scheme is stable only if the time step is below the 3D Courant limit, dt <= 1 / (c * sqrt(1/dx^2 + 1/dy^2 + 1/dz^2)) [2][3][15], and with a graded grid ONE global step is set by the smallest cell. So fine z cells through the stackup slow every cell in the domain, not just the thin region. Computed here at 2.44 GHz (period 410 ps): uniform 0.1 mm cells give dt about 0.19 ps (about 2100 steps per cycle); 0.1 mm in xy with 0.05 mm in z gives dt about 0.14 ps (about 3000 steps per cycle); uniform 0.25 mm gives dt about 0.48 ps (about 850 steps per cycle). Total cost is cells times steps, so the earlier steps-per-second estimates understate the run time. Local time stepping or subgridding could remove this penalty but is a research item whose stability is not verified here.
- Real-time expectation: at 2.4 GHz a wave period is about 2000 to 3000 time steps (see the time-step item above), so a continuous-wave view runs well below wall-clock speed. S11 ring-down needs tens of thousands of steps (50 000 steps is about 7 ns of simulated time at dt 0.14 ps), which takes seconds to minutes of GPU time depending on the measured step rate.
- Batch many time steps per command-buffer submission, keep fields in GPU memory, and read back only probes and accumulators every N steps.
- Sub-cell features (for example a 0.07 mm gap) need a conformal or subcell treatment; see the patent item in Licensing before choosing one [9]. fp32 fields are fine; DFT accumulation needs compensated (Kahan) summation [14] or fp64 on the CPU.
- Stackup and material defaults come from `data/fab_profiles.json` and are user-editable (see Defaults and editable attributes). Material parameters missing from datasheets (for example ceramic permittivity) make absolute resonance uncertain without a measurement. The viewer is for field patterns and trends.

## Performance strategy
Run time = cells x steps / cell-updates per second. FDTD is bandwidth-bound: the update does 7 floating point operations per field component against 24 bytes moved, far below a GPU's compute-to-bandwidth ratio [16], so memory traffic sets the speed and the roofline view applies [13].

**Source-backed and derived figures**
- Floor per cell-step: 6 field components each read once and written once in fp32 is 12 x 4 = 48 bytes (derived; ignores material coefficients and halo re-reads).
- Measured elsewhere: on a GTX 1080 Ti, gprMax's free-space cubic models reached up to 3405 Mcells/s [16] at a measured 360 GB/s, which is about 106 bytes per cell-step effective (derived from those two numbers). Free-space cubes have trivial materials, so a board with many materials will be slower.
- Updated estimate from the measured 182 GB/s copy bandwidth (kernels typically reach 85 to 90 % of it): about 155 to 165 GB/s, so roughly 1.5 Gcells/s at 106 bytes per cell-step, up to about 3.4 Gcells/s at the 48-byte floor. A 10M-cell grid would then run about 150 to 340 steps/s, and 50 000 steps would take about 2.5 to 5.5 minutes. Still an estimate until the FDTD kernel is benchmarked (step 3).
- Budget placeholder: set a target (steps/s for a given cell count on the M1 Pro) after the step-2 benchmark, then track it so a regression is visible.

**Levers, in priority order**
1. Fewer cells: region-of-interest crop, graded grid, automatic coarsening, memory estimate before the run (see Product requirement).
2. Fewer steps: one broadband pulse gives S11 over the band; stop when the field energy has decayed; do not over-resolve thin layers because the smallest cell sets the time step (see Time step).
3. Fewer bytes per cell: compact material indices instead of per-cell coefficients; fp32 fields; later fp16 storage and temporal blocking [12] only if measurements justify it.
4. Keep the GPU fed: fields stay in GPU memory, short submits (watchdog), no per-step host work; absorbing boundary only on boundary slabs.
5. Display cost: the multi-frequency DFT accumulators at display resolution add about 1 % of solver traffic per field component at 10M cells and S = 100 (derived, see Viewer design: N = 15, 128^3), growing with display voxels over solver cells; the per-frame reconstruction and ray march are separate render costs. N, the number of components and the display grid are tunables.
6. Startup: several small kernels, a persistent pipeline cache, parallel CPU voxelization.
7. Measure with timestamp queries, warm runs, medians, against the measured copy bandwidth, under the GPU lock.

## Parallelism in the GPU design
FDTD is data-parallel: within one time step every cell's update depends only on its neighbours' values from the previous half-step. The cost is dominated by memory traffic, not arithmetic, so the design aims to keep every GPU core busy and every byte loaded once.
- **One invocation per cell (or small cell group).** The E update and the H update are two 3D dispatches per step. Between them is a barrier, since H needs the finished E and the reverse. Nothing else in the step needs ordering.
- **Memory layout for bandwidth.** Store each field component (Ex, Ey, Ez, Hx, Hy, Hz) as its own array (structure of arrays) with x fastest, so neighbouring invocations read neighbouring addresses (coalesced). Material coefficients are stored per cell in compact form (a material index plus a small table) rather than six floats per cell.
- **Workgroup tiling (corrected after reading [11]).** The established GPU method is not a 3D tile in shared memory. Each workgroup loads a 2D tile plus a one-cell halo of the x-y plane into shared memory and marches along z, the slowest axis, keeping the neighbouring slices in registers [11]. Read redundancy is (n*m + k*n + k*m) / (n*m) with k the stencil width; the Yee update reads only +-1 cell, so k = 2 and a 16 x 16 tile gives at most 1.25 and a 32 x 32 tile 1.125 (derived here; the staggered stencil may do better). Tile shape is a specialization constant chosen per device. **But start simple:** the gprMax GPU engine uses plain 1D-indexed, coalesced kernels with no tiling and reports near-peak bandwidth with similar performance to tiled designs [16]. So version 1 is the simple coalesced kernel, and tiling is added only if the benchmark shows it helps on the M1.
- **Fuse where possible.** Apply the source term and the PEC and conductivity-sheet corrections inside the update kernel instead of separate dispatches. Absorbing boundary (CPML) work runs only on the boundary slabs, in its own small dispatches, so the interior stays a simple stencil.
- **Cheap reductions.** DFT accumulators and probes use subgroup operations and a per-workgroup partial sum, then one small final pass, instead of atomics on every cell.
- **Many steps per submission.** Record N time steps into one command buffer, with barriers only between E and H passes, so the CPU is never in the per-step loop. Readback is limited to probes and accumulators every N steps.
- **Compute and rendering.** The viewer does not read the solver arrays directly; see Viewer design. MoltenVK exposes four queue families (probe below), but whether the Apple GPU runs work from them concurrently is unmeasured, so do not rely on async compute until measured.
- **Graded grid and parallelism.** Keep the grid structured (a regular index space with per-axis cell sizes), so the kernels stay branch-free and coalesced. Cell sizes are 1D lookup tables per axis.
- **CPU backend parallelism.** Same update structure, split across threads by z-slabs, with vectorized inner loops over x. It doubles as the correctness reference.
- **Later options (only if measurements justify them):** fp16 field storage to halve bandwidth, temporal blocking (several steps per tile) [12], splitting the domain across two GPUs with halo exchange, and a CUDA backend.
- **Measure first, against a measured ceiling.** gprMax reached about 320 GB/s of the 360 GB/s that the BabelStream copy benchmark measured on a GTX 1080 Ti (about 89 %), and BabelStream itself reached only 66 to 75 % of the theoretical peak on the NVIDIA cards tested [16]. So our denominator is a copy-kernel bandwidth measured on this machine, not the spec-sheet number, and the target is about 85 to 90 % of it.

## Viewer design
**Display path (decision).** The solver's field arrays and the viewer's data are separate. A compute pass writes the quantity to display into a separate 3D image: reconstructed each animation frame from the N-frequency phasor accumulators (see Animation), or, for a live preview, resampled every M steps (R8_UNORM or R16_SFLOAT, normalised), at a size capped for display (proposal: at most 256 voxels per axis by default, about 17 MB at 8 bits; larger on request). The viewer ray-marches that image in a fragment shader over a cube proxy [19][18] and draws the board geometry (outline, copper polygons, component boxes) as ordinary triangles. This replaces "the viewer reads the solver buffers".
- **Ray marching:** accumulate colour and opacity front to back along each ray, stop early once the opacity is high enough, and skip empty space using a coarse min/max grid [18]. Krüger and Westermann implemented this with multiple passes and the early depth test on 2003 DirectX 9 hardware; modern shaders can loop and exit early inside one shader, so the techniques carry over but the mechanism does not [18]. The field is concentrated near the antenna and tiny elsewhere, so a log transfer function and a user threshold matter more than the marcher's speed. Slice planes are a cheap second view.
- **Graded grid:** resample to a uniform display volume inside the snapshot pass, fetching the solver arrays through per-axis coordinate tables. Why: the ray marcher stays simple and hardware trilinear filtering works, and the cost is one pass per M steps. The alternative, marching in the non-uniform index space with per-axis lookups on every sample of every pixel every frame, costs more.
- **Synchronisation:** two display images. The solver writes one while the viewer reads the other; swap at an M boundary with a single compute-to-fragment barrier. All four MoltenVK queue families here support graphics, compute and transfer, so one queue family is enough and no ownership transfer is needed.
- **Frame budget:** a long solver submit also starves the swapchain. The "tens of ms" submit rule from the watchdog lessons is also the frame budget: submits are sized so a display frame fits between them (assumption: about 8 ms; measure).
- **Snapshot cadence M** is a user-visible performance knob (smooth view versus solver speed), exposed with a default.

**Animation (requirement: the 3D view is an animation of the waves).** The render side is settled above. The open question is how to represent time. Candidates, compared on four axes: (a) extra solver traffic, (b) display-data memory, (c) per-frame cost, (d) temporal fidelity. Reference case for the numbers: 10M solver cells, a 128^3 display grid (2.1M voxels, which is 0.16 mm voxels over a 20 mm region of interest), sampling every S = 100 solver steps (3000 steps per cycle, dt about 0.14 ps), N = 15 frequencies. All figures are derived here, not measured.

| Representation | (a) Solver overhead | (b) Display memory | (c) Per-frame cost | (d) Fidelity |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Single-frequency phasor** (standard steady-state animation) | about 0.1 % (20 B per voxel per sample against S x 48 B x cells) | 17 MB per field component (fp32 complex) | read 8 B per voxel and rotate phase: 17 MB | converged CW pattern at one frequency; loops and scrubs for free; no transient |
| **2. N-frequency phasor, reconstruct per frame** | about 1.1 % per component, 3.2 % for three E components (244 B per voxel per sample) | 0.25 GB per component (fp32 accumulators), 0.13 GB if stored as fp16 after the run; 0.75 GB for three components | reconstruct pass: 2.1M voxels x 15 complex multiply-adds, reading 0.13 to 0.25 GB per component; about 4 to 8 GB/s at 30 fps | full transient within the run window, any time instant, free loop and scrub, any playback speed; exact only for a band-limited signal that has decayed by the end of the run |
| **3. Frame store** | about 0.1 % (one 12 B read per voxel per frame) | 2.1 MB per frame at 128^3, 8 bit: 2.1 GB for 1000 frames; 17 GB at 256^3 | read one frame | transient, but time resolution fixed at the recording interval M; scrub only over recorded frames |
| Live (no storage) | none | none | none | 2 to 6 frames per second at the estimated 120 to 300 steps/s, so not smooth |

**Decision (proposed): representation 2 as the single mechanism, with representation 1 as its N = 1 case and representation 3 only as a live preview.** The same DFT accumulator path then serves S11, steady-state animation and transient playback. The three checks the choice depends on:
1. **N (number of frequencies):** a signal confined to a window T is fully described by its DFT at spacing df = 1/T. For T = 50 000 steps x 0.14 ps = 7 ns the spacing is about 143 MHz; a 2 GHz-wide excitation band (for example 1.5 to 3.5 GHz) needs N about 14, so use N = 15 to 20. This needs the excitation to be band limited and the fields to have decayed by the end of the run (otherwise the animation wraps around at T); an antenna with a narrow resonance rings long, so the ring-down time sets T.
2. **Memory:** accumulators live at display resolution, not solver resolution. At 128^3 with N = 15 they are 252 MB per component in fp32 (fp32 because summing thousands of samples in fp16 loses precision, the reason compensated or wide accumulation is already in the plan). At 256^3 they would be 2 GB per component, and the traffic below would be about 8 times higher, so the display grid is capped at about 128^3, which the region-of-interest crop makes adequate.
3. **Traffic:** per sample per display voxel per component: one 4 B read of the field plus a read and write of 15 complex fp32 accumulators (240 B) = 244 B. Against S x 48 B x cells of solver traffic this is 1.1 % per component at 10M cells and S = 100. It scales with display voxels over solver cells: for a 2M-cell solver it would be about 5 % per component, so the number of components and N are exposed as knobs. The sampling interval S x dt must stay below half the period of the highest frequency present (about 700 steps for 5 GHz content at dt 0.14 ps); desampling the solver's oversampled time series this way is standard [22].
Both are under the 10 to 15 % threshold, so the single-mechanism design stands. If a run ends with a lower cell count and more components, the plan falls back to representation 1 plus 3.

The sources behind this: running-DFT field conversion for multiple frequencies from one pulsed run, with storage of 2 x points x parameters x frequencies values and a cost of one complex multiplication per time step, point, parameter and frequency, is the standard FDTD approach and desampling is a known saving [22][23]. An alternative that solves N equations for N unknowns from 2N time samples (NENU) needs a source built from a sum of sine waves and can be ill-conditioned, so it does not fit a pulse excitation [22]. The survey of time-varying volume rendering reviews the same trade-off (store or compress many time steps, or reduce the data) and treats time separately from space as the usual choice when temporal and spatial coherence differ [20]; compressed time-varying approaches (palette and transform coding, time-space partitioning trees) exist for very large simulations [24][25][26] and are not needed at our sizes.

- **Reconstruction runs in a compute pass per animation frame into the display image, not per ray sample in the fragment shader:** millions of voxels x 15 multiply-adds per frame, against billions of ray samples. The ray marcher then sees an ordinary scalar volume, as designed above.
- **Per-frame render cost (formula):** texture fetches per frame = pixels x samples per ray x rho, where samples per ray = volume depth in voxels x oversampling and rho is the fraction left after early termination and empty-space skipping [18]. At 1920 x 1080 (2.07M pixels), 128 voxels deep, oversampling 3 (384 samples) and rho = 1 this is about 0.8 billion fetches per frame, 48 billion per second at 60 fps. The M1 Pro's 3D-texture fetch rate has not been measured: it is a step-6 probe, and the numbers above are an upper bound. Mitigations in priority order: early ray termination and empty-space skipping [18]; pre-integrated classification, which gets equal image quality with fewer samples but needs its lookup table rebuilt when the colour map changes [21]; reduced resolution and fewer samples while the camera moves; slice planes.
- **Slice planes first.** A 2D slice costs one fetch per pixel (2M per frame), so it is the cheapest view and the first thing step 6 builds, before the ray-marched volume.
- **Signed field:** a wave only looks like a wave if the sign is kept, so show a signed component (diverging colour map) or the instantaneous real field. Time-averaged |E| hides the propagation and is for the static pattern only. Transfer-function design is surveyed in [27].
- **Controls:** play and pause, loop, playback speed against physical time (picoseconds, for example "1 s = 20 ps"), a time or phase slider, frequency selection, field component, colour map, threshold, slice planes, the component selection described under Product requirement.
- **Render rate is independent of solver rate:** the viewer renders at the display refresh rate; provide a resolution scale while the camera moves.
- **Consequence for the plan:** the solver carries a multi-frequency DFT accumulator at display resolution from the start (a knob for N, components and region), and the viewer needs only the reconstruction pass and the ray marcher.

**Probed on this Mac (2026-11-19, `vulkaninfo` under the GPU lock):** four queue families, each graphics + compute + transfer with 1 queue, 64 timestamp bits and present support; `VK_EXT_metal_surface` and `VK_KHR_surface` available; R8_UNORM, R16_SFLOAT and R32_SFLOAT all support sampled image, storage image and linear filtering with optimal tiling; `maxImageDimension3D` is 2048; shared memory per workgroup 32 KB; subgroup size 32.
**Still to probe:** presentation through GLFW (not installed; its MoltenVK surface path is unverified), whether work on different queue families actually overlaps on this GPU, the cost of writing a 3D storage image versus a buffer, and the image layout transitions needed for storage write then sampled read.

## GPU rendering (decided 2026-11-20: move the 2D and 3D drawing from the CPU to Vulkan)
**Today.** The solver and the phasor accumulators are on the GPU, but every picture is made on the CPU: the slice and the 3D ray marcher (`viz/slice.cpp`, `viz/volume.cpp`), the board overlays and the ImGui widgets (`viz/imgui_soft.cpp`), composited into an RGBA frame that `viz::Window` uploads to the swapchain. The solver then competes with the renderer for CPU time and every frame crosses the CPU.

**Is MoltenVK deprecated? Yes (the user said so; read on the primary page github.com/KhronosGroup/MoltenVK on 2026-11-20).** Its README opens with "Khronos has deprecated MoltenVK", says the repository "is now maintained by the community on a best-effort basis", that "Khronos now recommends KosmicKrisp on Apple Silicon Macs" and that MoltenVK "remains the recommended option for Intel-based Macs and for iOS". It gives no date (Wikipedia's 2026-10-13 is secondary) and shows no release. It says KosmicKrisp comes from "the latest macOS Vulkan SDK from vulkan.lunarg.com" or a Mesa source build, which also settles that the SDK alone is enough. KosmicKrisp is LunarG's Vulkan-on-Metal driver inside Mesa, reported conformant to Vulkan 1.4 (Phoronix, 2026-10); it needs Metal 4, so macOS 26 or later, and is macOS only. (An earlier version of this section said no deprecation was found; that was wrong, from a first search that missed the notice.) MoltenVK 1.4.2-rc1 (2026-07-19, from a news report) is the last release seen. **Performance is disputed:** Phoronix says good relative to MoltenVK, a vkQuake issue from 2025-12 found it much slower on an M1, before later work. **Not verified here:** whether KosmicKrisp installs on this Mac (an M1 Pro; the OS reports Darwin 27), its speed on our FDTD kernels, and its licence (Mesa; check the file before bundling).
**Decision.** KosmicKrisp is the target driver on macOS, MoltenVK the fallback until the measurements below say otherwise. The code stays driver-neutral: core Vulkan 1.2 only, portability enumeration as an optional instance extension, no driver-specific paths, SPIR-V from `glslc`; the Vulkan loader picks the driver (`VK_ICD_FILENAMES` forces one). **Dependency (user suggestion): the LunarG Vulkan SDK alone**, since it bundles the loader, `glslc`, the validation layers and the Apple drivers; the README says the latest SDK carries KosmicKrisp. Today the build uses Homebrew's loader and `glslc` while the SDK at `~/VulkanSDK/1.3.283.0` (older than the deprecation) sits unused. **Next:** (1) install the latest SDK with the user's go-ahead (a system change, not done yet); (2) run `scripts/check.sh --gpu` and `apps/bench` against both drivers under the GPU lock and record step rate, copy bandwidth, shader compile time and any failures here; (3) decide the default. Do not use Metal directly: it would fork the renderer per platform.

**Steps, each with a BDD scenario written first, `scripts/check.sh --gpu` green, and a line in Measured so far.**
- **R1. GPU volume renderer behind `viz::VolumeRenderer`** (`src/viz_vk`, `shaders/volume_render.comp`): phasors uploaded once, a frame is a camera and a phase; compute pass for the ray march; result read back as RGB8 into the existing compositor. The CPU marcher stays as the reference and the fallback (`makeVolumeRenderer(nullptr, ...)`). Scenario in `tests/gpu/volume_gpu_test.cpp`: same picture as the CPU marcher (mean channel difference under 0.5 of 255 levels; under 0.5 % of pixels off by more than 8), phase applied, empty field is exactly the background.
- **R2. No readback:** the renderer writes into a storage image that the window samples, so frames stay on the GPU. Needs the swapchain image to be a transfer destination or a full-screen draw. Measure the readback cost from R1 first and do R2 only if it matters.
- **R3. UI on the GPU:** replace `SoftImGui` with Dear ImGui's Vulkan backend (`imgui_impl_vulkan.cpp`, MIT, already in the fetched source; no new licence) drawing into the swapchain image after the volume. `SoftImGui` stays for the headless screenshot and accessibility-dump path, so `--ui-screenshot` keeps working without a window.
- **R4. 2D slice, overlays and legends on the GPU:** slice as a texture sampled through the colour table, copper, vias and the port as triangles or lines, the Smith chart and plots through ImGui draw lists.
- **R5. Measure** frame time against the CPU path at 1080p on this Mac (warm, under the GPU lock), including the cost while the solver is running; record the result here.

## How other tools show and drive EM simulation (researched 2026-11-20; proposals, nothing decided)
Sources are vendor documents and forum posts found by web search, read as search summaries; none were read in full. Commercial products are described only by what their public documentation says.

**What the commercial tools show.** HFSS field overlays cover far-field radiation patterns, E, H and current density; an E-field plot animates over phase, and surface current (Mag_Jsurf) animates the same way; the 3D far-field pattern can be overlaid on the antenna geometry ([HFSS post-processing course](https://innovationspace.ansys.com/courses/wp-content/uploads/sites/5/2021/07/HFSS_GS_2020R2_EN_LE4_FEM_PostProc.pdf), [far-field on CAD](https://courses.ansys.com/index.php/courses/post-processing-in-aedt-hfss-ii/lessons/including-cad-model-in-far-field-plots/)). The Fields Calculator integrates the Poynting vector through a surface to get radiated power. CST records E and H on a box around the antenna and computes the far field from it, offers more than 200 result templates plus scripting, and uses an isotropic reference for gain ([CST antenna note](https://www.slideshare.net/slideshow/cstantennacst-studio-suite-2006bpdf/252616276), [post-processing whitepaper](https://www.scribd.com/document/135318014/Postprocessing-Whitepaper)). Open tools (openEMS, kicad2ems) dump fields for ParaView and compute far-field patterns from a closed surface of recorded fields ([openEMS NF2FF](https://wiki.openems.de/index.php/NF2FF.html)).

**"Mesh Fusion" and system-level simulation.** Ansys says HFSS 3D Layout reads ECAD layouts of boards, packages and connectors, assembles them, and solves a fully coupled matrix; Mesh Fusion lets a design that large be meshed with the same adaptive technology. Its published example is eight BGA packages on a SODIMM board in a connector on a motherboard, 64 nets and 128 ports, solved in 34 hours against weeks for a component-by-component flow (vendor claim, 2020 to 2021, [blog](https://www.ansys.com/blog/system-level-em-simulation)). How it partitions meshes is not documented in anything found, so **we do not claim or plan an equivalent.** The nearest FDTD ideas are subgridding and local time stepping, already listed as unverified research items under "FDTD design". What this project can honestly offer, using what exists: one board-level run in which each part is a black box (chip antenna as a dielectric block, lumped R, L, C, a connector or module as a body), which is the test-matrix and "component bodies" work already planned. A later step, S-parameter-defined blocks (a Touchstone file for a filter or module, stamped in as a port network), is a proposal that needs its own method review.

**Parametric tools.** HFSS and CST both sweep and optimise named parameters; that is the feature engineers expect after the first run. The primitive already exists here: the settings registry and `--set KEY=VALUE`. **Hard constraint:** the plugin never modifies the board, so geometry parameters (patch length, feed offset, trace width) cannot be swept by us. What can sweep without touching the design: stackup values (dielectric thickness, er, loss tangent, via the PCB settings), port reference impedance, mesh preset, boundary margin, and a chip antenna's body permittivity. A geometry sweep needs the user to edit in KiCAD and re-export per point, or a solver-side geometry override (a snapshot-level offset of a named footprint or net), which is a larger design and an open question below.

**Feature gaps against what those tools show, ranked by cost on this code.** What exists today (read from `src/viz`, `src/fdtd`): a CPU ray marcher (`viz/volume.cpp`), a CPU `PhasorAccumulator` and a CPU `RunningDft`; the GPU DFT accumulator at display resolution, the GPU reconstruction pass and the GPU ray marcher are not built (Next steps 3). Costs below are relative, not estimates.

| # | Feature | What an antenna engineer gets | Exists | Missing |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Surface current density on copper** | where current flows, so where to cut, add a stub or move the feed | H field and the voxelised copper masks | J_s = n x (H_above - H_below), the jump in tangential H across the zero-thickness copper sheet (both faces summed; one face only for a solid body with no field inside), with a defined rule for the staircase, accumulated as a phasor on the copper cells only; drawn on the copper polygons |
| 2 | **Phase animation of one frequency** | the standard "wave moving" view | the N = 1 case of the planned phasor path; CPU version exists | expose a phase control and a play loop that is exactly periodic |
| 3 | **Smith chart and VSWR, impedance** | match at a glance, what to add to tune it | S11 and Touchstone output | a plot (implot or our draw list), markers, band shading from the profile |
| 4 | **Poynting vector on a slice** | where power flows and where it leaks | E and H | time-average of E x H* from phasors, arrows or line integral convolution on a slice |
| 5 | **Threshold isosurface** of the phasor magnitude | cheaper and clearer than a fog volume for hot spots | display volume | marching cubes or a ray-hit shader, one level the user sets |
| 6 | **3D far-field pattern, gain, directivity, efficiency, axial ratio** | what the antenna does outside the board | RunningDft, the display DFT plan | near-to-far-field transform: DFT of tangential E and H on a closed box in the air, equivalent currents, radiation integral (method: Luebbers et al. [28], Taflove and Hagness [15]); a pattern mesh drawn at the board's phase centre, plus a gain-versus-angle plot. CPML must sit outside that box |
| 7 | **Total radiated power and radiation efficiency** | tells loss from mismatch | port power, field energy | from the same box (Poynting flux), and from S11 and dissipation |
| 8 | **Sweep overlay** | compare variants in one plot | one results folder per run | a sweep driver and an overlay of S11 curves (see parametric above) |
| 9 | **Export for ParaView** | users keep their own tools | frames as PNG | VTK image data (`.vti`, XML, no VTK library needed) of the phasor volumes and a pattern mesh; the format is open |
| 10 | **Current-flow and E-field animation overlaid on the board in KiCAD colours** | recognisable to a PCB designer | board geometry in the view | copper layer colours taken from the editor's theme if the API exposes them |

Reading across: items 1, 2, 3 and 4 need no new solver physics and are where an engineer's first questions are answered. Item 6 is the largest and the most valuable for an antenna tool, and it is the same box-of-DFTs machinery the display path already needs, so the accumulators should be designed once for both (see Next steps 11).

**Improvements to the existing view (proposals).**
- Put the density of colour where the data is: the volume uses a dB scale with one colour pair; add a named sequential map for magnitude (open item 2 in `docs/ux.md`). Perceptual maps exist as data (cmocean MIT; colorcet CC-BY-4.0 with attribution), but the current two-colour scheme needs no dependency, so adopting one is a user decision.
- Freeze-and-compare: pin a run's slice or S11 as a reference and overlay the next run (cheap, helps every tuning loop; same code as the sweep overlay).
- Probes: click a point on the slice to read |E| and phase at each frequency; a time-domain trace at a probe.
- Show the model as the solver sees it: the voxelised copper and dielectric on a toggle, so a user sees a staircased edge or a widened port before trusting a result (error prevention, `docs/ux.md` section 2).
- Keyboard camera control and a visible focus ring (already in Accessibility follow-ups); any new plot needs a spoken summary like the S11 plot has.

**3D view controls (researched 2026-11-20; requested by the user: a control to snap the volume camera to the +X, -X, +Y, -Y, +Z and -Z views).** Search summaries only; no page was read in full.
- **Prior art.** The widely copied pattern is a small orientation widget in a corner of the view that shows the current orientation and snaps the camera to standard views; Autodesk's ViewCube has 26 clickable regions (6 faces for orthographic views, 12 edges, 8 corners), drag-to-orbit with optional snapping to the nearest standard view, arrows to step between adjacent views, roll arrows, and a highlighted outline when on a standard view ([Maya help](https://help.autodesk.com/cloudhelp/2023/ENU/Maya-Basics/files/GUID-C1861E55-85FA-47F9-B4D2-71366875E56D.htm), [Autodesk Research paper](https://www.research.autodesk.com/publications/viewcube-a-3d-orientation-indicator-and-controller/)). Blender's navigation gizmo (axis balls) and FreeCAD, KiCAD and Rhino view controls were not read here.
- **Patent risk, open.** Autodesk holds patents on this widget: US 7,782,319 B2 "Three-dimensional orientation indicator and controller" (issued 2010-08-24) and US 9,043,707 B2 "Configurable viewcube controller" (whose claims, as the search summary describes them, cover a 3D representation comprising a cube whose faces correspond to viewpoints). The claim text of 7,782,319 was not retrieved, its expiry was not verified (a forum post says 2029), and this is not legal advice. **Rule, same as for conformal schemes:** do not implement a clickable orientation *cube* until the claims have been read and the result reviewed by someone qualified. "ViewCube" is also Autodesk's name for it; never use that name in the UI or code.
- **Licences of readable prior art:** ImGuizmo MIT (has a view-manipulation gizmo; not adopted, our UI is drawn by our own rasteriser), FreeCAD LGPL-2.1, KiCAD GPL-3.0, Blender not reported by the GitHub API. Copyleft sources are behaviour references only (clean-room rule).
- **Options that avoid the cube claim (proposals):** (1) an axis triad gizmo (three labelled axis arms with opposite-end markers, click an end to snap to that axis view), a different shape from the patented cube; (2) a row of six labelled buttons (+X, -X, +Y, -Y, +Z, -Z) plus "isometric", which also serves keyboard and screen-reader users (every control is a named node, `docs/ux.md` Accessibility), and number-key shortcuts; (3) smooth animated transition to the chosen view, skipped under Reduce Motion. Whichever is chosen, show the current orientation (labelled axes in the corner) and keep drag-orbit, pan and zoom as they are.
- **Decided 2026-11-20 and built: the axis triad** (user's choice; no cube). Six labelled axis ends in the volume's top-left corner snap the camera to the +X, -X, +Y, -Y, +Z, -Z side views ("+X" = camera on the +x side), an Iso button gives the isometric view, keys 1-6 and 0 do the same, and the move glides unless Reduce Motion is on. Pose and screen layout are pure code in `src/viz/view_axes.hpp` (scenarios in `tests/cpu/view_axes_test.cpp`: named end at full depth within 1e-3, glide ends within 1e-6, short way round the azimuth). Each end has an accessibility node ("View from +X"). The top and bottom views stop 1e-3 rad short of the pole because the camera's up vector is +z. Not built: edge and corner regions, a drag-to-snap, and a roll.

## Reference-guided antenna tuning (researched 2026-11-21 with the advisor; proposals, nothing decided)
**Problem.** The W3008 reference board (`boards/w3008-testboard/`, 2 layers, 1.6 mm) follows the datasheet. The user's own board uses the same chip antenna on a multi-layer HDI stack (through vias and laser vias) and reaches only about 2 to 3 ft by Bluetooth. The wanted feature: use a reference design to point out what differs in a user's design and what to change, as suggestions only (the plugin never edits the board).

**The constraint that shapes everything (now a hypothesis under test, see "Is the W3008's radiator just its visible Ag metallization?" below).** The W3008 has been treated as not simulable (no published internal geometry, see Measured so far, 2026-11-19), so we can never compare the antenna's own resonance by FDTD. What we *can* compare is everything around the chip: ground plane, keepout, feed line, stackup, matching, placement, nearby metal. Vendors say the same: a chip antenna's tuning and pattern follow the ground plane's size and shape, the datasheet figures hold only for the vendor's test board, and the datasheet matching parts "are only valid for a chip antenna implementation that follows the datasheet exactly: same ground, same keep out, same orientation" ([Ezurio/Laird white paper](https://www.ezurio.com/resources/white-papers/how-to-implement-chip-antennas)). So the reference can say "your surroundings differ here", and the match must always be re-derived for the user's board, never copied.

**The reference cannot ship.** The W3008 reference board folder is untracked and git-ignored (`.gitignore`; its datasheet carries a no-reproduction notice and the repository is public), so a public build has no bundled reference. So the design is *any two snapshots or runs* plus antenna integration rules held as data; "the reference" is whatever the user points at (their own board, a vendor evaluation board, a golden run). Nothing here may name W3008 or the example board in code.

**Sources (search summaries only; no page read in full).**
- Ezurio/Laird, element14, Analog Devices [AN-1370](https://www.analog.com/en/resources/app-notes/an-1370.html), Microchip, Digi, Antenova: keepout dimensions come from the datasheet; no copper on *any* layer of the host board under the antenna; vias along the edge of the keepout; a pi or T match close to the antenna; short feed; metal structures at least 25 mm from the antenna; re-matching needs a network analyser ([Digi notes](https://docs.digi.com/resources/documentation/Digidocs/90002002/Reference/r_design_notes_pcb.htm), [Antenova](https://www.antenova.com/storage/media/documents/Antenova-Antenna-PCB-Integration_1767721973.pdf)).
- Ground plane: a University of Valencia study of an 868 MHz LTCC chip antenna reports about 3 dB less radiation efficiency when the board area halves ([paper](https://riunet.upv.es/bitstream/10251/177476/3/Molins-BenlliureCabedoAntonino%20-%20Effect%20of%20the%20ground%20plane%20in%20UHF%20Chip%20antenna%20efficiency.pdf)); a larger ground lowers the resonant frequency and widens the bandwidth, and gains level off above about half a wavelength of side ([Digi-Key](https://www.digikey.com/en/articles/understanding-antenna-specifications-and-operation-part-2)). Not a universal formula; the vendor's own board is the reference.
- Nordic DevZone replies (forum posts, mixed age): the reference match is tied to the reference layout (pad size, line lengths are part of the match); reference designs assume 1.6 mm FR-4; tune the radio side for output power on a spectrum analyser, check the antenna side with a VNA; include the enclosure when tuning; isolate the SoC with a U.FL switch connector to measure the antenna alone ([threads](https://devzone.nordicsemi.com/f/nordic-q-a/115718/nrf52840-rf-wireless-tuning)).
- TI [SWRA726](https://www.ti.com/document-viewer/lit/html/SWRA726/GUID-D538E56B-24ED-47B7-AD45-DA62211DFB6F): measure the impedance at the plane where the match will sit; port extension for one-port antennas; remove the matching parts and the DC block while measuring; an in-device check shows resonance, match and bandwidth only.
- **Part variants differ.** The W3008, W3008C and W3008G datasheets give different clearances (4.00 x 4.25, 4.00 x 6.25 and 11.00 x 6.25 mm per the search summaries). The checker must read the keepout from the rule data for the exact variant, and warn when the part number on the board is ambiguous.
- TI SWRA117 and Silicon Labs AN1088 for stackup dependence of PCB antennas were already listed under "Sample designs"; they were not re-read for this section.

**What the example board is, in general terms (nothing from that repository is copied here).** It is a multi-layer HDI design with through vias and laser vias (the user's description), so thin dielectrics and a total thickness well below the reference's 1.6 mm are expected. The checks below are the ones to run on it; its raw findings stay in `out/`, not in this file. Probable causes, by what the sources and simple physics say, in order of how cheaply they can be checked (hypotheses, not findings):
1. **Copper on an inner layer inside the keepout** (a ground or power plane that is not notched on every layer). HDI stacks make this easy to miss because the planes are 0.1 mm apart. *Checked on the example board 2026-11-21 (read-only, `out/w3008/keepout_check.py`; details in `out/example-board-notes-2026-11-21.md`): the keepout holds nothing but the pads' own connections on every layer (zones were refilled on a copy to be sure); a routed cutout inside it is empty.*
2. **Smaller ground plane than the vendor's test board, or ground cut up by routing and anti-pads**, so the counterpoise is short or interrupted (efficiency and tuning shift). *Checked on the same board: its ground planes are a small fraction of the area of the vendor's test board. By the vendor's warning (performance depends on the test-board size) and the published trend (one 868 MHz study, not a 2.4 GHz law), this is the leading candidate, and the simulator can test it: the example's geometry and the reference's with the same chip model, compared.*
3. **Feed line sized for the wrong stack.** A 50 ohm line needs about the same width-to-height ratio; with thin dielectrics the line to the next plane is much closer, so a 50 ohm line is far narrower than on a 1.6 mm two-layer board (a copied or reference-sized feed is then far from 50 ohm). The CPWG closed form already in `boards/.../cpwg.py` (and the planned validation) is the tool; it takes the board's own stackup.
4. **Matching network left at the datasheet values**, which belong to the test board; with a different ground and stack the antenna's impedance differs and the pi network needs re-fitting.
5. **Nearby metal and cables** (battery, USB shell, shields, a plane under the chip on another layer) detuning it; the enclosure and a hand also matter (Nordic advice above).
6. **Not the antenna:** TX power setting, crystal or clock problems, firmware (advertising interval, PHY), RF filtering, or a receiver-side issue. 2 to 3 ft against the 10 m or more a chip antenna normally gives is about 20 to 30 dB (free space: 20 log of the distance ratio, about 22 dB for 0.75 m against 10 m; an estimate), which is more than most antenna mismatches cost, so a gross fault (a short, an open, an unfitted part, a wrong net) is as likely as a mistuned antenna.

**Proposal in three tiers.**
- **Tier 1: rule and geometry diff, no solve (cheapest, most useful first).** From each snapshot, compute and show side by side, each with the rule value and its source: copper area inside the keepout per layer (any non-zero inner-layer value is an error); ground plane length and area along and behind the antenna and its distance to the board edge or corner; whether the ground under and beside the keepout is continuous (via stitching along the keepout edge, anti-pads, slots); feed line Z0 from the closed form with the board's own stackup, and the length and bends from the antenna to the first part; the matching network topology and values against the *rule's* recommended starting values and against the reference run, labelled "starting point, not transferable"; nearest metal bodies to the antenna; stackup summary (layers, total thickness, height to the nearest plane under the feed). Output is a table of OK / differs / unknown with a one-line suggestion each, written to the run folder and the window, never applied to the design. Needs the exporter to report layers per copper item (it does) and **blind/buried via spans and microvias** (open: the exporter notes unsupported blind and buried vias; the checker must at least know which layers each via joins, or it will wrongly report a notch as continuous).
- **Tier 2: same-setup simulation of both boards, then a diff.** Run the same profile, port definition, mesh preset and boundary on the reference and the user's board (one `emfdtd` run each, or a sweep entry). Show: S11 and impedance overlaid on one Smith chart and one plot with the difference marked (this is the "freeze-and-compare" and "sweep overlay" items already proposed, so build it once); surface current on the ground plane side by side with the same colour scale (shows a counterpoise that is cut, shorted or cut off by a plane); |E| inside the keepout and the fraction of energy that stays near the chip; the far-field pattern and radiated fraction once item 11(f) exists. Honest limit: the chip is a dielectric block (or not modelled), so S11 here describes the feed, ground, stack and match, not the ceramic's resonance; the report must say so on the same screen.
- **Tier 3: calibrated black-box chip (proposal; needs a method review and a cited source before any code).** Treat the chip as an unknown one-port Z_chip(f). From the reference board, where a measured S11 or impedance exists (datasheet plot, or the user's own VNA file), solve for the one-port that makes the simulated reference match the measurement, then place that one-port on the user's board and simulate. This transplants the chip's behaviour but assumes the chip's coupling to the ground does not change, which is exactly what changes when the ground changes; so treat the result as indicative, not a prediction, and say so. Whether it adds anything over Tier 2 needs a trial on two boards with known measurements.

**Closing the loop on the match (the part the user can act on).** Given a measured or simulated impedance at the match plane, compute pi and L networks over the user's candidate E-series values and show S11 and the loss in each part's ESR, as a list of suggestions (the matching is best done with a VNA at the match plane, per TI and Nordic). That is Smith-chart item 3 plus a small solver of network values; the design must put the plane where the user will measure. Suggest a VNA test point (a U.FL or a cut) as a layout recommendation.

**What the simulator cannot split, and what to ask the user to measure.** A VNA reading at the feed (S11 of the antenna with the radio disconnected) separates antenna and feed faults from radio, firmware and receiver faults; a spectrum analyser reading of the transmit power at the connector separates the radio from the rest. Without them the tool can report only the geometry differences and a simulated impedance.

**Data (nothing in code).** `data/antenna_rules.json` (new), same pattern as `radio_profiles.json` and `fab_profiles.json`: per antenna part number (variant-exact): keepout box and the layers it applies to, the vendor test-board size, the reference position, minimum recommended ground, a recommended match topology and starting values on the vendor's board, nearby-metal distance; each entry with `source`, and `verified` or `unverified`. Generic entries (any 2.4 GHz chip antenna: copper-free keepout on every layer, ground continuity, a pi footprint) are marked as general guidance from the vendor notes above. Values come from the datasheets, read and cited, never from memory.

**BDD scenarios, tolerance in the THEN (to write first).** (1) Two small fixture boards, one with an inner-layer pour inside the keepout: the checker reports that layer and the copper area within 1 % of the fixture's known area, and none for the clean board. (2) Ground length and distance-to-edge metrics against hand-computed values on the fixtures, 1e-6 mm. (3) Feed Z0 from the closed form against the published CPWG/microstrip formula for a given stack within 1 %, and the width a 0.1 mm stack needs against a stated example. (4) A blind via spanning the keepout layers is counted as joining only the layers it spans. (5) The S11 overlay delta of two identical runs is 0 within 1e-6 and of two different runs matches a hand computation. (6) A match solver reproduces a known pi-network example within 0.5 dB.

**Open questions for the user** (nothing blocks the Tier 1 start): (1) Which outputs first? Recommendation: Tier 1 table, then the Tier 2 overlay (it reuses item 11 and the sweep). (2) Is "the reference" a user-supplied run or board (recommended, since the W3008 board cannot ship), with the vendor's rules as data? (3) Do you have a VNA, or can you take one reading at the feed? It decides whether the match step is possible. (4) ~~Which W3008 variant is on the example board?~~ Answered 2026-11-21: the plain **W3008**, so the keepout to check is 4.00 x 4.25 mm on every layer (datasheet v2.7), not the W3008C's 4.00 x 6.25 mm. (5) Should the checker stay in the plugin flow (a list of findings after Simulate) or live only in the standalone window? CLAUDE.md says to discuss new plugin UX first, so none is designed here.

### Alternative chip antennas for a tiny board (researched 2026-11-21; datasheets read, facts only; nothing decided)
**Constraint (from the user's board, read-only):** the island is about 13 x 9 mm with a small ground (the examined board's ground planes total under 100 mm2), the antenna sits on an edge, and the land pattern is the W3008's: one 0.8 x 1.6 mm pad and two 0.8 x 0.65 mm pads (feed and ground) at 3.2 mm pitch. The previous work in `../bomu-hardware/em` studied only the W3008 (and a TI inverted-F demo); it holds no list of alternatives.
**Datasheets read (local copies in `out/antennas/`, git-ignored; paraphrased):**
- **Johanson 2450AT18A0100001E** (legacy 2450AT18A100; Rev 4.0, 2024): 3.2 x 1.6 x 1.3 mm (EIA 1206), LTCC, "corner mount"; **two terminals, feed and NC** (the second is for anchoring, not ground, so the W3008's G2 pad would have to be left floating); 2.4 to 2.5 GHz, return loss at least 9.5 dB, peak gain 1.5 dBi, average gain 0.0 dBi, efficiency 74 % on its 39.5 mm wide evaluation board with ground vias recommended and a pi or tee match whose values change on another board; its clearance drawing (a few numbers near 6.5, 4, 4.2 and 2.6 mm) could not be tied to dimensions from the extracted text. **Internal structure:** "LTCC ... embeds low and high dielectric constants inside"; no permittivity, layer count or electrode metal is given. STEP: only on request through a form.
- **Antenova Abdita SRCW090:** 1.0 x 0.5 x 0.5 mm ceramic, clearance 7 x 7 mm (larger than the W3008's 4.00 x 4.25), 2.8 dBi peak, 60.5 % efficiency on its evaluation board, "operates on various ground plane sizes"; pads 0.5 x 0.5 mm at 0.9 mm pitch (not the W3008 footprint); internal structure not stated.
- **Ignion NANO mXTEND NN02-101:** 3.0 x 2.0 x 0.8 mm non-resonant booster "built on glass epoxy substrate" (the one stated material), two pads (feed and a mounting pad that must not be grounded), clearance 5 x 5 mm, a three-element match, 55 % (corner) or 65 % (edge) efficiency on an 80 x 40 mm board; HFSS and CST files are published (`ignion.io/files/HFSS_NN02-101.zip`, `CST_NN02-101.zip`; their terms were not read). Not a drop-in: different pads.
- **Abracon AMCA31 (3.2 x 1.6 x 1.2 mm), Yageo W3008C and W3008G, Taoglas, TE:** datasheets could not be fetched (timeouts, HTTP 403, a script-rendered page). Land pattern, clearance and structure are unknown; the W3008C and W3008G have larger clearances per earlier search summaries.
**What the application note shows (Ignion, April 2024, NN02-101 on 1 mm FR4 boards; a chip antenna of a different design, so a trend, not a prediction for any other part):** total efficiency about 59 % on 80 x 40 mm, 60 % on 40 x 20 mm, 77 % on 40 x 5 mm, **14 % on 15 x 10 mm**, 13 % on a 15 mm disk and 78 % on a 30 mm disk, each with a match re-fitted to the board. The user's island is in the 15 x 10 mm class, so a loss of this size comes from the ground, not from which chip is fitted; no alternative chip is a drop-in cure. What can help is more ground (or a longer one): a plug-in USB board borrows its host's ground when plugged in.
**Which candidates need internal structure to simulate:** all of them have some. LTCC parts (Johanson, Abracon, W3008-class) hide embedded conductors and mixed dielectrics; the glass-epoxy booster (Ignion) is a printed pattern on a known material, the most simulable of the set, and it has published HFSS and CST models to check an FDTD model against. For none of them could the permittivity, layer count or electrode metal be found, so each needs the same treatment as the W3008: a body-plus-conductors model with its dielectric constant fitted to a datasheet S11, labelled fitted. See the chip-model status above: sub-cell gaps decide that result, so a fitted model is not yet trustworthy.
**Open:** read the Johanson Rev 4.0 clearance drawing and the Abracon PDF by hand; ask Johanson for the STEP and CAD; check the Ignion model terms; check the user's board edge (curved) against corner or edge mounting rules.

**Enclosure (told by the user 2026-11-22; no dimensions yet):** the board is designed to fit inside an Anker USB A to USB C adapter. Its real surroundings are a small plastic housing with metal shells at the USB-A plug and the USB-C receptacle, plugged into a host whose ground acts as the counterpoise. This probably matters more than the choice of chip: the island-only model and the ground-size ratios above ignore the shells, the housing and the host. **The adapter (user's link, 2026-11-22):** Anker USB-C to USB 3.0 Adapter, 2 pack; the Anker page itself gives no model, size, material or teardown. Retailer listings (search summaries, not read in full) give model A8731 (B8731, SKU B87310A1), about 26.2 x 14.5 x 7.5 mm, 3.8 g, **an aluminium shell**, 5 or 10 Gbps (listings disagree). If the antenna end of the board sits inside or against that shell, the shell shields and detunes it; whether it does depends on where the antenna end lies when plugged in (told by the user 2026-11-22: **the antenna end sticks out of the adapter and is never inside**, so it is in free air and the shell is behind it, acting as part of the ground, not as a cage). No teardown found. **Photo of the board seated in the adapter (user, 2026-11-22; the photo is private and not in the repository):** with nothing but the board in the adapter, only a small end of the board shows past the aluminium body, about 3 to 4 mm by my scale estimate from the adapter's 14.5 mm width (a rough photo estimate, not a measurement), and the chip antenna is on that protruding end, its long side along the adapter's end face. So the metal face is a few millimetres from the chip, inside the 4.25 mm keepout depth the datasheet asks to be free of ground. The user sometimes adds a piece of credit card to make the fit snug; it never covers the antenna, so it is not modelled near the chip. Modelling needs: the shells as metal bodies (supported: `--body REF=metal`), the housing as a dielectric body, a host ground plane beyond the plug, and the adapter's dimensions or a teardown. Not started.

### Is the W3008's radiator just its visible Ag metallization? (tested 2026-11-21, partly; the user's hypothesis)
The earlier conclusion "the chip cannot be simulated" assumed hidden internal geometry. The user's hypothesis: the radiator is the Ag metallization seen on the outside (the caps wrap round the sides to the top, and with the pads form the antenna), on a plain ceramic body.
- **Evidence read.** Datasheet v2.7 pages 1 to 4 (read in full, 2026-11-21): the only metal drawn is "Ag metallization contact pad area (2x)", 0.62 x 1.36 mm on a 3.2 x 1.6 x 1.1 mm body; "antenna is symmetrical, either terminal can be feed/GND"; the photo shows two Ag ends and bare ceramic between; it calls the part a "loaded PIFA" (the user says monopole; see below). The **vendor's STEP model** (SolidWorks 2017, three solids; found in the user's own KiCAD project and read in place, nothing copied) is more detailed than the datasheet: the ceramic body (dark) and two white Ag caps, each **0.05 mm thick, 1.36 mm wide**: an end wall over the full 1.15 mm height, a bottom contact pad 0.62 mm long, a top strip about 1.09 mm long, and from it a **half-width tab running past the middle of the top** (about 0.47 mm beyond the centre). The two tabs overlap along about 0.95 mm side by side, in opposite halves of the width, **0.04 mm apart**: an interdigitated gap on top of the chip, the sort of structure that makes a loaded or coupled radiator out of two terminals and a plain block. A CAD model is a mechanical drawing, so it shows only what the vendor chose to model; it does not prove the inside is empty, and the dielectric constant of the ceramic is not in it.
- **What the datasheet cannot tell.** Whether the terminal at the feed end touches both pad 1 (feed) and pad 2 (ground) (a PIFA-style short: the datasheet's land pattern and a 1.36 mm wide cap suggest it does) or only the feed pad (monopole-like, the user's reading): two variants, `drawn` and `feedonly`, test it.
- **Test, built and not finished.** Private generator `boards/w3008-testboard/make_snapshot.py` (git-ignored with the folder) writes a snapshot of the reference board with the chip as the STEP geometry (metal parts of 0.05 mm, a ceramic box, one unknown: its permittivity, plus the lossless-ceramic assumption) and a fixed `--flip` for the chirality of the tabs; `compare_s11.py` compares a result with the three datasheet |S11| points (-8.19, -18.31, -8.23 dB at 2.400, 2.440, 2.4835 GHz; the impedance markers 27.6+j22.4, 62.1+j6.9, 45.0-j40.9 ohm check against them within 0.2 dB). **Status 2026-11-21: every run before the fix below was invalid; the defect was in my snapshot generator, not the solver or the chip.** The 50 ohm feed line is wider (half-width 0.509 mm) than the narrow corridor cut in the top copper pour around the feed stub (half-width 0.5 mm), and the corridor widened at exactly the u where the line starts, so the line's corner touched the pour by 9 micrometres and the grid connected them: the feed was grounded at the pad in all runs, with or without a chip (so the chip, its permittivity and the `drawn` short could not change anything). Found by known-load controls (no chip; the phase of S11 extrapolated to zero frequency should be 0 degrees for an open end and 180 for a short, whatever the line length): ties across the line's gap far from the pad read as clean shorts (183 and 186 degrees) but the supposedly open line also read as a short (181 degrees on a 5 mm line), and a flood fill of the generated copper showed the RF and GND copper as one piece. After moving the corridor's widening one gap back from the line, the controls pass: open 357 degrees, tied at the stub 184 degrees. Lesson: a generated snapshot needs a geometry check that nets which should be separate are separate, and every simulated case needs a known-load control first. Chip runs after the fix are recorded below. **After the fix, screening results (30 x 22 mm crop, coarse mesh, 1 % stop, `feedonly`, lossless ceramic).** With the grid snapped to the chip's body corners (a second defect: the 0.04 mm gap between the two silver tabs was sampled by chance, and before it was resolved the response did not depend on permittivity at all) the response now moves with the ceramic's permittivity: the deepest dip sits at 2.856 GHz for 10, 2.823 for 20, 2.497 for 30, 2.326 for 40 and 2.130 for 60, so a resonance crosses 2.44 GHz near a permittivity of 30 to 35. But it is shallow: about -1.1 dB at best, against the datasheet's -18 dB at 2.44 GHz, so the model as built is badly matched or poorly coupled. Not yet separated: the screening shortcuts and the small crop (a ground plane of 30 x 22 mm, not the vendor's 80 x 37), the lossless-ceramic assumption, missing internal structure, and the tab gap still being only one cell. Nothing here is a fit; the next step is the permittivity 30 to 35 case on the full board at default settings (35 to 50 minutes each), then a halved-cell check. **Full reference board, default mesh, boundary and decay (2026-11-21, `feedonly`; each run took 9 to 17 minutes, not the 40 to 50 estimated).** A resonance sits in band for permittivity 35 to 37 (dip -4.1 dB at 2.464 GHz for 35, -4.5 dB at 2.473 GHz for 37; -3.1 and -3.1 dB at 2.44 GHz) but the datasheet has -18 dB there, so **the visible-metallization model does not reproduce the datasheet match**. Loss does not close the gap (loss tangent 0.002: -4.15 dB; 0.01: -4.33 dB; lossless -4.11). The impedance shows why: the model is capacitive with a small resistance over the band (R 13 to 27 ohm, X -50 to -70 ohm at permittivity 35) where the datasheet's reactance crosses zero near 2.44 GHz (+22 ohm at 2.4 GHz to -41 ohm at 2.4835 GHz, R 28 to 62 ohm), so the model has no sharp series resonance. Not separated: the one-cell tab gap, missing internal structure in the ceramic (a CT scan would show it), the lossless-ceramic assumption, and the chip's true tab chirality (`--flip` not tried on the full board). Consequence for use: the chip model is a rough stand-in, so comparisons between boards that carry the same chip model are more trustworthy than any absolute S11, and no impedance from it should be quoted as the part's. Each run costs about 7 minutes of the shared GPU at these settings; the 35 to 50 minute figure applies to the full-board default settings.
- **Solver changes made for it (general, tested; plus a fix found on the way: a via narrower than the local grid cell used to vanish, it now falls back to the nearest node, scenario `A via narrower than the grid cell still joins the layers`):** `--body REF=ignore|metal|dielectric:EPS` (a part's body treatment from the command line; the sidecar was only read by the window); settings `air_near_cell_mm` and `air_near_height_mm` (a fine layer of air next to the board, so a body thinner than the coarse air cells is modelled instead of silently left out; default off); an optional `offset` of a snapshot body above the board surface (a metal strip on top of a part). Scenarios in `tests/cpu/board_test.cpp`.
- **Board outline, cutouts and via spans are respected (2026-11-21, on the user's instruction).** The snapshot now takes optional `cutouts` (holes in the board: no dielectric inside) and optional `from`/`to` layers on a via (blind and micro vias join only those layers); the voxelizer, the plugin exporter (outline read from Edge.Cuts with arcs, circles, beziers and holes, `outline.py`) and the documentation follow, with scenarios in `tests/cpu/board_test.cpp` and `plugin/tests/`. Before this the exporter replaced any outline by its bounding rectangle and every via ran through all layers, which is wrong for a rounded board, a routed slot or an HDI stack.
