A board drawn as a panel with routed slots and breakaway tabs exports as drawn (the whole panel, slots as cutouts); to model only the board proper a script has to cut the tabs, as a private analysis of a real board did.
- **Decision rule.** A null result is not conclusive on its own: the 0.04 mm gap is one cell and each metal part one cell thick, so under-resolution can mimic a missing structure. Before concluding hidden structure, halve the cell at the best permittivity and see whether the response moves. If one permittivity (and one variant) reproduces the datasheet's -18 dB dip at 2.44 GHz and the two -8 dB edge points within about 2 dB, record the chip as a body-plus-caps model, with the fitted permittivity labelled as fitted, and use Tier 2 as written. If none does even after that check, the interior probably has structure the vendor model omits and Tier 3 stays the route (a CT scan is the way to see it). Either way a fitted model is ours, not the vendor's.

## Plugin lessons from the antenna study (2026-11-21)
What the reference-board work taught, as items for the plugin and the solver. Status as of the date; "notes" means the run-summary and Preview notes, no new widgets.
1. **Short check before a run (building, scenario first).** Copper of different nets that touch at the grid's resolution is reported in the notes ("nets A and B touch on layer L near x, y"). Reason: a 9 micrometre overlap in a generated board grounded the feed in every run for hours and nothing said so.
2. **Port connectivity check (building).** Static, no run: the voxelized copper is followed from the port's two terminals; the notes say when they are joined by copper (a DC short between the port's terminals: fine for a shunt-fed antenna, otherwise the feed is grounded) or when the driven terminal reaches almost no copper (floating port). This would have flagged the bug above at once. The run-based known-load self-check (open and short at the pad, phase at zero frequency) stays a proposal for an Advanced action because it needs geometry only the user can supply; the method is the `fdtd-control-check` skill.
3. **Unresolved thin features (building).** A modelled body or gap thinner than about two local cells is reported with its size and the cell size, next to the existing "covers no cell" note. Reason: a 0.04 mm gap between two silver tabs was sampled by chance and the response did not depend on permittivity until it was resolved.
4. **Panel outlines and breakaway tabs (slot-end bridging built in the exporter 2026-11-21; perforation rows, the opening fallback and the switch are not).** The exporter now writes Edge.Cuts as drawn, so a board drawn inside a panel exports with its frame. See "Excluding breakaway tabs".
5. **Keepout, ground and feed checks (queued, no change of plan).** This is Next steps 15 (a) and (b); the study supplies its first fixtures (a board whose ground is a small fraction of the vendor board's area, an HDI stack with micro vias, a keepout crossed by ground micro vias). Order after item 4's tab exclusion, because every check is measured on the board proper, not the panel.
6. **Model status labels (building, small).** A part model may carry a label saying whether it was fitted to a datasheet and how well; the notes print it, so a rough stand-in does not look authoritative. The W3008 body-and-caps model reads "not fitted: does not reproduce the datasheet match". Unlabelled treatments print "assumed, not fitted".

**Findings from building items 1 to 3 (2026-11-21).** (a) The new checks work and found real defects at once. The body-touch note showed that on a real multi-layer board, with the many round pads near the chip, grid snapping could close the chip's 0.04 mm tab gap (two lines closer than a cell: the second target's nearest edge was the first's, so its gap line was lost); the snapping now takes the nearest unfixed edge (scenario `Two snap positions closer than a cell both become grid lines`) and snaps only to shapes of 8 vertices or fewer (round pads have no corners worth a line). It still closed on the user's board, so the chip generators take `--gap` (default 0.04, the part's value) and a 0.10 mm gap (two cells at 0.05 mm) removes every feed-to-ground contact between the caps on both boards tested. (b) The port-path note then showed a second defect on the real board: with the chip fitted the port's terminals are joined by copper, without it they are not, and dropping the footprint's G1 pad (as the reference test did with its second pad) did not clear it, so the chip's feed-end cap probably also touches the ground tracks around that pad. What the real part's feed-end terminal touches is the open question; until it is settled the chip run on that board (an impedance of about j10 ohm, a short) says nothing, and the earlier reference-board chip results should be treated as unconfirmed until a rerun with the corrected grid passes these notes (queued as `rc`). (c) `scripts/check.sh` must be rerun after these changes (queued).

**Update (same day, 2026-11-21).** The corrected-grid rerun of the reference board (`rc`, permittivity 35, tab gap 0.04 mm) reproduced the earlier result (-4.112 dB at 2.4643 GHz), so the reference-board findings stand. A fourth note was added: a via whose column ends on copper of another net is reported ("via of net X ... lands on copper of net Y"), with its scenario. On that board it reports about a dozen such vias, and the port-path note says the feed is joined to ground whenever the chip is present, even with the G1 pad dropped and the feed cap's wall and foot cut down to the FEED pad's size; the join appears with only the ceramic block present, so it depends on the grid, not on a metal contact. Neither the via notes nor the join is believable for a finished design: they point at the private board generator (a private script kept in `out/`), most likely at pad or zone placement for footprints other than the antenna (positions were verified against the datasheet for the antenna's pads only; zone nets are read as numbers where the pads and vias use names). Next step, before any more chip runs on that board: check the generator's pads, zone nets and via pads against the KiCAD file (for example by exporting the same board through the plugin path) and clear every note. The reference-board chip model needs no change for this.

**Resolved (same day): the via notes and the "joined" note on the real board are grid-level shorts, not a generator error.** Cutting a small window around a reported via and re-running with different refinement showed that the set of flagged vias changes with the cell size. A copper edge is claimed when its midpoint is inside a shape, so copper within half a cell claims a whole edge, and a board with clearances near 0.1 mm shorts neighbouring nets at 0.15 mm cells; the notes are right that the model, at that resolution, is shorted. Consequence: the whole neighbourhood of the antenna feed (track, match pads, ground vias) needs cells of about 0.05 mm or finer, not only the chip's box. Widening the 0.05 mm box to cover the feed path did not yet clear the port note, because the snapping then squeezes some cells to 0.02 mm (the smallest cell sets the time step, so a run grows to about 400 000 steps and an hour of GPU) with so many nearby pad and body corners as snap targets. Next: limit how far snapping may shrink a cell (a floor of about 0.7 of the local cell, accepting that a gap narrower than that is widened and reported) and make the port-path and via notes name the first shorting contact, then rerun. Not done; the chip run on that board stays untrusted until the notes are clean.

**Chip-model status (2026-11-21, end of the study).** With the corrected grid (squeeze floor 0.6, tab gap 0.10 mm) the reference board's in-band response no longer resembles the datasheet (2.44 GHz about 335-j227 ohm; deepest dip -10.6 dB at 2.04 GHz), while the 0.04 mm gap gave -4.1 dB in band: a sub-cell gap decides the result, so permittivity cannot be fitted to the datasheet. The user's board with the same chip reads as a near-short (|S11| -0.03 to -0.21 dB, about 1 ohm plus a reactance) with clean notes, cause not found. Decision: the chip body-and-caps model is not used for tuning advice. Valid outputs for the user's antenna are the rule checks (keepout copper, ground size, feed impedance, via spans) and the reference comparison, not simulated S11 of the chip.

## Excluding breakaway tabs (researched 2026-11-21; a recommendation, nothing built)
**Problem.** A board drawn inside a panel (a frame, routed slots, tabs, perforation holes) exports as the whole panel, so the frame's copper and dielectric change the ground and the radiation. The simulation must use the board proper.
**What fabricators publish (numbers only, read for ranges; no code or text copied):** tabs 2 to 5 mm wide (2 to 3 mm for small boards), routed slot gaps about 1.6 to 2 mm (1.2 mm minimum), perforation holes 0.5 to 1 mm in diameter at about 0.75 to 0.8 mm pitch, three to eight holes per tab, often set slightly inside the board edge; sources: the JLCPCB mouse-bite panelization guide, the SparkFun mouse-bite whitepaper and Altium's depaneling article. They are guidance, not a standard (one source says so outright), so any rule must be a detection with a user check, not a constant.
**Measured on a real panelised board (details private, kept in `out/`):** the board proper was joined to the frame by a few tabs a little over 2 mm wide across slot gaps under 2 mm; width alone separated it: a morphological opening by a 1.6 mm disk left two pieces and the board piece held about 95 % of the board's true area (only convex corners rounded); a 1.2 mm disk left everything joined (tabs wider than the disk survive), a 2.0 mm disk started to eat the board's own features. So the disk must be matched to the tab width, which can be measured.
**Recommended order of cues** (first that works wins, always reported in the notes and overridable):
1. **Perforation rows.** Small holes in a row at roughly 0.6 to 1.2 mm pitch across a tab mark the cut line exactly: the board is the side of that line that holds the antenna footprint. Most reliable where present; the exporter already sees holes as Edge.Cuts circles or as drilled pads.
2. **Slot-end bridging.** Slots are long, thin Edge.Cuts loops (aspect ratio above about 4, width under about 3 mm) that form a ring around the board; each tab is a gap between two slot ends. The board outline runs along the slots' board-side edges and crosses each gap by the straight segment joining the two end corners on the board side. Exact (it reproduces the hand-cut island), no thresholds beyond "is this loop a slot".
3. **Opening by width.** When neither cue exists: erode and dilate the material mask by a disk of 0.6 times the measured tab width (the narrowest neck between large regions), keep the piece that holds the antenna, restore corner slivers within half a disk of it. Fragile for tabs wider than the board's own narrowest feature, so it is the fallback and says so.
4. **Confirm in the Preview:** one line in the notes ("board proper N mm2, panel frame and K tabs excluded by slot-end bridging") and a switch "Use the whole Edge.Cuts as drawn". The switch is new UX, so it waits for the user's decision, as does where it lives (exporter option or window setting).
**Built (2026-11-21):** `outline.board_proper` finds slots (width up to 3 mm, aspect 4 or more), bridges each slot end to the mutually nearest end of another slot within 6 mm on the seed's side, and takes the face of slot edges and bridges that holds the seed (must be at least 20 % of the drawn outline and have 2 or more tabs, else the outline stays as drawn). Copper, vias and footprints off the board proper are dropped. Tested on a synthetic panel only: not yet run on a real panelised board through KiCAD, and perforation-only panels are not handled. Convex corners at the tabs are cut by the bridge, as measured in the prototype.
**Decision (user, 2026-11-21): the switch lives in the window's settings** as `board_outline` (proper or whole, default proper, group PCB, Essential). So the exporter writes both rings (`outline` as drawn, `board_proper`) and `applyPcbSettings` picks; built with a scenario.
**Where it runs:** in the exporter, before the snapshot, so the solver never sees the frame; the snapshot may also carry the panel outline so the window can show both. Items beyond tabs, such as a region of interest around the antenna on a very large board, are a later setting (not started).
**Not used:** the full advisor review was skipped to save spend; the method rests on the prototype above and on the fabricators' published ranges.

## Lessons from other GPU projects on these machines
Reported by the pg-strom session (Vulkan GPU offload) and the mongo session (Vulkan mesh work), both on the same M1 Mac and Windows RTX 4060 Ti. They come from those projects' experience and are unverified for this one. Details: `/Volumes/CrucialX9/Projects/mongo/poc/gpu/extension/PLAN.md` and `poc/gpu/README.md` (branch `vulkan-gpu`), read-only references.
- **Watchdog and the viewer:** a long-running dispatch can trigger `VK_ERROR_DEVICE_LOST` through the macOS GPU watchdog and also freezes UI interactivity. Keep each submit short (tens of ms) and split a batch of time steps into several submits. This directly limits the "many steps per submission" rule above, and matters because the viewer shares the GPU.
- **Shader compile time:** one huge SPIR-V kernel can take minutes on Metal. Prefer several small kernels (E update, H update, CPML, DFT) over one ubershader. Persist a `VkPipelineCache` and cache SPIR-V by source hash.
- **Feature tiers:** M1 has no fp64 and no 64-bit buffer atomics. Do not depend on them; query caps with small probe kernels as well as feature flags. Enable storage8/16, int64 and subgroup ops explicitly when needed. Our fp32 plan with Kahan summation or CPU fp64 fits this.
- **Differential testing:** compare GPU against the CPU reference on small grids. It caught an NVIDIA driver bug in 64-bit signed remainder, and is the main correctness tool here too.
- **Process model:** Metal's shader compiler cannot run in a forked process. Do not `fork()` after Vulkan initialization; spawn a separate process instead (relevant to the plugin launching the solver app).
- **Benchmarking:** wall time from submit to fence is CPU-side, so use timestamp queries for device time. Pipeline creation costs 400 to 700 ms on first use: warm up, report best of 3, and measure on an otherwise idle machine. Unified memory on the M1 gave about 31 GB/s effective in their workload (data-dependent), so measure our own bandwidth first. Concurrency knobs (queue depth, chunk size) gave 8% or less; kernel specialization mattered more.
- **GPU sharing and locking (Mac):** never run two GPU programs at once on the M1, and run tests at `nice -n 10`. Existing wrappers (verified present): `/Volumes/CrucialX9/Projects/pg-strom/test/gpuserv/gpu_lock.sh` and `/Volumes/CrucialX9/Projects/mongo/poc/gpu/tools/with_gpu_lock.sh`. Read them before use; do not copy them in.
- **GPU sharing and locking (Windows, later):** the FIFO lock is `/Volumes/CrucialX9/Projects/pg-strom/test/gpuserv/win_gpu_lock.sh` (`hold <label> <min>` in the background and wait for the line HOLDING, `status`, `release <label>`). Only the lock holder asks the Windows agents to run GPU programs, one at a time. Windows GPU runs need this project's user's approval; peer sessions cannot give it.
- **Metal compiler bug:** spec-constant-sized arrays can be miscompiled. Use a fixed maximum array size and test every parameter dimension (loop bounds, sizes), not just one. This matters because the plan tunes tile size through specialization constants.
- **Floating point:** GPU compilers may fuse multiply-adds. Keep a CPU oracle and test it with `-ffp-contract=off` and `=fast`; compare results on every device.
- **Measure warm, not cold:** the first run includes device and pipeline creation (about 40 ms on the M1, 100+ ms on Windows). Report the second run or medians of 5, and measure the submit floor by dispatching one workgroup.
- **GPU is not automatically faster than the 8-core M1 CPU** (unified memory): copies and data movement around the kernel can cost more than the kernel. For FDTD the fields stay on the GPU, but budget the viewer snapshot and probe readbacks up front.
- **Toolchain:** `glslc -O` vs `-O0` made no difference for them. Use the Vulkan SDK's `glslc` and `volk`, and set `VK_ICD_FILENAMES` to the SDK's `MoltenVK_icd.json` if the loader does not find MoltenVK. SPIR-V checked in as a generated header is an option.
- **Device robustness:** use a finite fence wait, never free buffers after a failed or timed-out wait (work may still be running), and treat a failed submit as poisoning the context.
- **Windows box (later):** native Vulkan through NVIDIA's driver works for compute; check `nvidia-smi` first (the eGPU dropped out once). Mesa Dozen under WSL is non-conformant, so its results are not representative. The box has had two blue screens (not caused by GPU runs; one involved exFAT on a USB SSD), so keep irreplaceable data off that drive. For Windows builds, the mongo project cross-builds with mingw in a Debian container and delivers over the SMB share the user mounts on the Mac (only the user can enter the password; verify sha256 on both sides). Here the simpler route is probably building natively on the box with Visual Studio (see Hardware), to be decided when Windows work starts.
- **WSL2:** Mesa `dzn` lacks 8-bit storage and 64-bit atomics, and there is no NVIDIA Vulkan driver under WSL. Use lavapipe (CPU Vulkan) on Linux for host-code and CI checks, and Windows-native for the NVIDIA GPU.
- **Housekeeping:** keep scripts in the repo, not `/tmp`; keep big data off the internal disk. macOS has BSD `sed`, `awk` and `wc`, `echo -n` is not portable, and the system GNU make is 3.81.

4 GB storage buffer range, 32 KB shared memory per workgroup, `VK_EXT_shader_atomic_float`. Tools: `glslc`, `glslangValidator`, spirv-tools, spirv-cross, DXC, cmake, ninja, clang++, Xcode.
Not installed: GLFW, SDL, Dear ImGui, VMA, Slang, Rust.
KiCAD 10.0.7 with `kicad-cli`. IPC API server enabled 2026-11-19 (`"enable_server": true` in `~/Library/Preferences/kicad/10.0/kicad_common.json`; it takes effect at the next KiCAD launch; original file backed up at `/private/tmp/claude-501/-Volumes-CrucialX9-Projects-kicad-em-fdtd/78a0cdfa-15a5-47c5-a6ab-78fcfa8ed0cd/scratchpad/kicad_common.json.bak`). `kipy` (`kicad-python` 0.8.0, MIT, requires Python 3.9 or later) is installed in the git-ignored repo `.venv` (Python 3.12) for development and probes only; KiCAD runs plugins with its own bundled Python 3.9.13 and its own managed plugin environment, so the shipped plugin must stay 3.9-compatible. Compatibility of kicad-python 0.8.0 with KiCAD 10.0.7 is unverified until the first `KiCad()` connection succeeds.

- Windows 10 22H2, build 19045. RTX 4060 Ti **16 GB**, driver 621.24, eGPU over Thunderbolt (confirmed by the user), PCIe link width x4 (`nvidia-smi` 2026-11-19; the generation read gen 1 only because the GPU was idle and downclocked, so the loaded link speed is not measured yet). Also an internal Radeon Pro 555X (Vulkan 1.1 only: too old for our kernels).

  Select by vendor/device ID, never rely on enumeration order.
- WSL2 Ubuntu-24.04: Vulkan sees ONLY `llvmpipe` (CPU, Mesa 25.2.8), no NVIDIA device. WSL is for Linux builds and CPU-Vulkan correctness tests only, NOT GPU runs.

  Visual Studio Community 2026 18.12 at `D:\VS2026` (checked 2026-11-19): MSVC 14.52.37025 (`cl` 19.52.37029, C++20 headers such as `<format>` and `<span>` present), Windows 11 SDK 26100 only (no Windows 10 SDK), workload Native Desktop with the VC CMake and vcpkg components. NOT installed: clang-cl (`Component.VC.Llvm.Clang`), so no clang.
  Not on PATH: `cl`, `cmake`, `ninja`. Enter an x64 developer shell first (both ways verified to work non-interactively): PowerShell `& 'D:\VS2026\Common7\Tools\Launch-VsDevShell.ps1' -Arch amd64 -HostArch amd64 -SkipAutomaticLocation` (run it in a child `powershell -NoProfile -Command {...}`, since the environment persists only for that session), or cmd `"D:\VS2026\VC\Auxiliary\Build\vcvars64.bat"`. A harmless "'vswhere.exe' is not recognized" line prints. In the dev shell: cmake 4.3.1 (bundled, under `D:\VS2026\Common7\IDE\CommonExtensions\Microsoft\CMake\`), ninja 1.13.2, `link.exe` from `D:\VS2026\VC\Tools\MSVC\14.52.37025\bin\Hostx64\x64`.
  CMake generators: "Visual Studio 18 2026" (default), "Ninja", "Ninja Multi-Config". Vulkan: `C:\VulkanSDK\1.4.363.0\Include\vulkan\vulkan.h` and `Lib\vulkan-1.lib` exist, so linking against the SDK works. Windows build plan: MSVC with CMake and Ninja inside the dev shell, no clang needed.
- Disk: D: 465 GB free of 931 GB (USB SSD, exFAT; two BSODs on 2026-11-17 involved the exFAT driver, so keep irreplaceable data off it). B: 50 GB free of 150 GB (NTFS, a .vhdx file stored on D:). C: 19 GB free of 94 GB. Existing builds use B: and D:.
- Measured by an earlier agent: Vulkan compute runs correctly on the 4060 Ti. About 17 ms per submit+wait (about 6 ms kernel time); allocation and copy cost 8 to 9 ms per batch.
  Thunderbolt dispatch and transfer latency is a visible cost, so a one-dispatch-per-step loop would cap at about 60 steps/s.
- **GPU access rule:** other agents share that GPU. Use the lock script described under "Lessons from other GPU projects" before any GPU run there. Read the script first.

Cross-platform rules (design constraints now, verification later):
- Kernels run on MoltenVK, NVIDIA and Mesa without extension tricks. Use the `volk` loader and enable `VK_KHR_portability_enumeration` on macOS. Detect optional features (fp16, float atomics) at run time.
- No hard-coded hardware numbers: tune workgroup size with specialization constants (subgroup 32 on Apple and NVIDIA, 64 on AMD), size the grid from the queried memory heaps, coarsen automatically when the model does not fit.
- Device selection by command line and UI. Default: prefer discrete, Vulkan 1.2 or later, most VRAM; allow a vendor/deviceID override. Never take the first enumerated device.
- CI (later): GitHub Actions matrix (macOS arm64, Windows, Ubuntu). Hosted runners have no GPU, so run the CPU backend plus a software Vulkan (SwiftShader or lavapipe). Real-GPU runs are manual.

## KiCAD plugin and distribution (distribution is later)
- Uses the IPC API through `kipy`. The API returns the live board, so no file export is needed; keep a file-based path (`kicad-cli`) for headless use.
- Unverified: which stackup, zone-fill and footprint data the KiCAD 10 API exposes. First task is a read-only probe plugin that dumps stackup, zones and pads to a file.
- Distribution is undecided: KiCAD's Plugin and Content Manager (PCM) store versus direct builds from GitHub releases. Nothing to decide until packaging. Per-platform binaries need code signing (Apple Developer ID, Windows certificate).

## Open questions for the user
**Answered 2026-11-20** (from "How other tools show and drive EM simulation"):
1. Build all the visual features, in the order under Next steps 11.
2. Geometry parametrics are wanted and must be configurable. Scope: a solver-side override carried in the snapshot (a named footprint offset or rotation, a named net's trace-width scale), the spec stored in the sidecar `em-fdtd/settings.json`, one `results/run-<time>/` per sweep point, the KiCAD design never touched. Known cost: a second source of truth for geometry, so the run summary prints every override and its value.
3. The user wants a colour map; the project is open source and non-commercial, so licence is not the deciding factor. Decision: no colour-map library. A map is a 256 x 3 table in `data/colormaps.json`, each entry with `source`, `licence` and a colour-vision note, chosen by a View setting ("Volume colour map") the way `ui_defaults.json` works. The matplotlib family (viridis, magma, inferno, plasma) is stated CC0 in the header of its source file (github.com/BIDS/colormap `colormaps.py`, read 2026-11-20: "released under the CC0 license / public domain dedication", credit appreciated; credit goes in `CREDITS.md` when the tables are added). cmocean is MIT (verified). Crameri's maps stay unverified until the Zenodo record is checked. Selection is on merit: a sequential map for the dB magnitude of the volume, since the volume is glow on dark and absorption on light, one default per theme; the signed slice keeps the two-colour diverging pair.
4. The far-field pattern is in the first release.

No blocking questions. Later: PCM versus direct builds, and whether KiCAD's addons maintainers accept prebuilt native binaries inside an official package (see Licensing). Open before any conformal scheme: the patent check.

## Measured so far
- 2026-11-19, `build/hello_compute` (cold, single run, so not a benchmark): Apple M1 Pro, vendor 0x106b, Vulkan 1.2, 17.2 GB reported as device-local (unified memory), compute queue family 0 of 4, 64 timestamp bits. 1M-element dispatch passed with 0 mismatches; wall 2.6 ms, GPU 0.5 ms.
- CMake found the Homebrew Vulkan loader (`/opt/homebrew/lib/libvulkan.dylib`, 1.4.363) and it found MoltenVK without `VK_ICD_FILENAMES`. `VULKAN_SDK` is unset in the shell, so the SDK at `~/VulkanSDK/1.3.283.0` is not what the build uses; `glslc` came from `/usr/local/bin`.
- 2026-11-19, `bench` (release, warm, medians of 9 and 21, under the GPU lock): **copy bandwidth 182 GB/s** (512 MiB buffers, read + write, 3 passes per submit; about 91 % of the 200 GB/s spec) and **submit-to-fence floor 0.28 ms** for a one-workgroup dispatch. A 3-pass copy submit takes about 18 ms wall. Memory type was host-visible and coherent, which on this unified-memory machine is the real bandwidth (flags are recorded in `out/bench.json`). The GPU test scenario passed with the validation layer confirmed active and zero validation errors.
- 2026-11-19, **IPC probe** (`plugin/probe/dump_board.py`, read-only, run against a copy of the W3008 test board in pcbnew 10.0.7): `kipy` 0.8.0 connects to KiCAD 10.0.7 over the default socket after launching `pcbnew` with the board. Exposed and working: version, board name, copper layer count, net list, footprints (position, reference and value fields, a `definition` with a `models` list that was empty for our model-less footprint), pads (number, position, net, padstack, `get_pad_shapes_as_polygons`), tracks, vias (position, diameter, drill, net), zones, the current selection (`get_selection`, readable, empty here), item bounding boxes, and the stackup (`get_stackup`: layers with type and thickness in nm, `edge`, `finish`, `impedance`; dielectric layers carry sub-layers that hold the material values, whose attribute names still need to be confirmed). Zone `filled_polygons` came back empty because the zones had not been filled in that session; `refill_zones` exists but changes the open board in memory, so it must be explicit and never saved. Not yet confirmed: whether 3D model references come back for a footprint that has one, and the dielectric sub-layer attribute names.
- 2026-11-19, **first GPU FDTD kernel** (`shaders/fdtd_step.comp`, simple coalesced version, no tiling, per-node coefficient arrays): **1845 Mcells/s** on a 160^3 PEC cavity, 22 ms per 10-step submit. That is about 99 bytes of traffic per cell-step if the kernel sits at the measured 182 GB/s copy bandwidth, close to the 106 bytes derived from gprMax and consistent with being bandwidth-bound. The kernel moves roughly 72 bytes per cell-step (6 fields read and written plus 6 coefficient floats), so the main lever is replacing the six per-node coefficient floats with a one-byte material index and a table (about 25 % less traffic), before any tiling. A 10M-cell grid would run about 185 steps/s today (50 000 steps in about 4.5 minutes). The GPU matches the CPU reference within 1e-4 of the peak after 200 steps on a uniform and on a graded grid (float rounding only), with zero validation errors.
- 2026-11-19, **material palette** (one packed word per node holding three 1-byte palette indices into a (ca, cb) table, replacing six coefficient floats): **2290 Mcells/s** on the same 160^3 case (+24 %, 17.9 ms per 10-step submit), GPU still within 1e-4 of the CPU reference on a uniform grid, a graded grid and a cavity with dielectric, lossy and PEC regions. The palette is limited to 256 distinct edge materials; the host throws if a model exceeds it.
- 2026-11-19, **CPML and lumped port** (CPU reference and GPU): CPML (kappa = 1, order 3, target 1e-6 reflection, 8 layers) leaves 1.5e-10 of the PEC-box energy on a uniform grid and 7e-11 on a 3 %-graded grid after 600 steps (test threshold 1e-6). A 50 ohm lumped port conserves energy within 5 % (stored = delivered - dissipated; the energy estimate itself is approximate). GPU: CPML runs as extra dispatches of the same shader (per derivative axis, slab-compact psi arrays), ports use a device-side step counter with a per-submit waveform table and an E log. GPU matches the CPU within 1e-4 of the peak with CPML only and with the port only, and within 1e-3 with both (the field left after absorption is small, so the same float noise is a larger share).
- 2026-11-19, **end-to-end checks:** the snapshot -> voxelizer -> solver -> S11 chain reproduces an analytic load: a 50 ohm port across a 2 mm gap with a passive 100 ohm port beside it gives |S11 - 1/3| < 0.002 over 0.3 to 0.6 GHz (S11 = 2 V / Vs - 1, source in series with R = Z0). The KiCAD exporter ran against the W3008 board in pcbnew 10.0.7 (14 copper polygons, 66 vias, stackup 1.53 mm FR4 er 4.5 tan 0.02 between two 35 um copper layers). **Dielectric sub-layer attributes confirmed:** `layer.dielectric.layers[i]` has `epsilon_r`, `loss_tangent`, `thickness` (nm), `material_name`; stackup layer types are named `BSLT_COPPER`, `BSLT_DIELECTRIC` (also silkscreen, solder mask, paste). Pads on a layer come from `get_pad_shapes_as_polygons` per pad (the batch call drops pads that have no shape, so results cannot be matched); filled zones need `refill_zones` in the open board.
- 2026-11-19, **the W3008 chip antenna cannot be simulated from public data.** It is a ceramic loaded PIFA whose radiating structure is inside the body; the datasheet gives the land pattern, the clearance and measured S11 but no internal geometry (the vendor's STEP model later showed the outer Ag caps; see "Is the W3008's radiator just its visible Ag metallization?"), so a footprint (pads only) has no antenna in it and the coarse run shows |S11| of about 0 dB. What can be simulated on that board is the feed line, the ground plane and clearance effects, with the chip as a black box. A user whose design has a PCB-trace antenna (inverted-F, meander, patch) is fully modelled. Validation therefore uses a rectangular microstrip patch whose resonance follows the standard cavity-model equations (`plugin/tools/make_patch_snapshot.py`, design 2.45 GHz on 1.6 mm FR4, er 4.5). Results: 1 mm cells on the CPU, dip near 2.4 GHz, -3.6 dB; 0.5 mm cells on the GPU (1.1 M cells, 23 392 steps, 34 s, 766 Mcells/s with CPML, port and a host read every 16 steps), dip at 2.4 GHz, -3.8 dB. So the resonance is about 2 % below the 2.45 GHz design value and stable between the two resolutions; the match is poor (-4 dB) because the probe position was a first guess. Not yet a convergence study: margin and CPML thickness differed between those runs (the CLI now adds the CPML thickness outside the margin).
- Grading is now the blocker for real boards: the 0.3 mm feed gap and 0.15 mm CPWG gaps need cells of 0.1 mm or less near the feed, while the rest of the board can use 0.5 mm or more. A uniform grid fine enough everywhere is tens of millions of cells. The voxelizer widens a port narrower than a cell to one cell and says so. Next: lateral grading (fine cells only near fine features, smooth ratio), then the viewer.

- 2026-11-20, **GPU phasor accumulator, kernel and host API (Next steps 11a, first half).** `shaders/phasor_accum.comp`, `GpuSolver::addPhasorList / accumulate / phasor` (`src/fdtd_vk/gpu_phasor.cpp`). One kernel over a list of channels (a field component, E or H, at a node) and N frequencies, Kahan-compensated in fp32 (`precise`), E sampled at n dt and H at (n - 1/2) dt. Scenario "GPU phasor accumulator matches a double-precision DFT" (700 mixed E and H channels on a graded cavity, 3 frequencies, 60 samples every 10 steps): every phasor within 1e-4 of the peak magnitude, passed on the M1 under the GPU lock. Caveats: the validation-layer count was 0, but I did not confirm the layer loaded in that run (VK_LAYER_PATH was guessed); the run did not time the kernel. **Update, same day (11a finished):** the accumulator is behind `sim::Engine` (`addPhasorList`, `accumulate`, `phasor`, `copyPhasors`; CPU `fdtd::PhasorBank` in double as reference, GPU forwarding), and `app::PhasorFeed` feeds the slice and the 3D volume from it, refreshing the viewer's copy about once a second and once at the end (`RunOptions::onFinish`). The per-sample CPU work over the display voxels (nearest-node sampling and 25 complex multiply-adds per voxel) is gone from `apps/emfdtd/ui.cpp`. Checked: a CPU scenario (engine phasors against a DFT of recorded fields, 1e-9; bulk copy), a GPU scenario (GPU engine against CPU engine, 1e-3 of peak), the whole GPU suite, and the patch sample end to end (`--set mesh_preset=coarse`, 17 952 steps): GPU and CPU runs give the same best match (-5.433 dB at 2.1461 GHz) and **pixel-identical slice frames** (mean absolute difference 0 of 255 over 3 frames); the GPU run took 15.4 s against 42.1 s on the CPU (272 against 99 Mcells/s, includes the accumulators). The UI path (`--ui --ui-run`) finished and drew the 3D volume from the GPU list. Memory: the Kahan arrays cost 4 x N x channels floats, about 105 MB at N = 25 and the default 64^3 display grid, and about 840 MB at 128^3 (derived, not measured). The sampling interval is 16 steps (about 16 ps here), far inside the half-period rule at the top of the excitation band. Not measured: the accumulator's own share of the step time.
- 2026-11-20, **Surface current on copper, computation only (Next steps 11b, first half).** `src/fdtd/surface_current.hpp`: `surfaceCurrentList(grid, pecEdges)` turns every forced x or y E edge (z edges, the via barrels, are left out) into one cell with two H channels, the samples half a cell above and below the sheet at the same lateral position, so the staircase needs no rule beyond "the edges the solver forces"; `surfaceCurrent()` gives J_s = n x (H_above - H_below). Border edges of a sheet count half a cell of width on the open side, so widths along a cut add up to the sheet width. The lists feed the same accumulator as everything else (`PhasorChannel`, `Engine::addPhasorList`); it is not yet wired into a run or drawn. Scenario (release, `[slow]`): a strip over a ground sheet, shorted at the far end, fed by parallel lumped edges whose conductances add up to 50 ohm; at 1 GHz the current through the first cut beside the feed is within 8 % of the port current in magnitude and 0.1 rad in phase. **Finding:** the shortfall depends on strip width, because current crowds at a strip's edges and a staircase cannot resolve it: 28 % at 4 cells wide, 14 % at 8, 8.7 % at 12, 5.6 % at 16 (measured, 1 mm cells, strip 1 mm over ground). So the magnitude on traces narrower than about 8 cells is a lower bound and only its pattern should be trusted; the viewer must say so. Not done: running it on the GPU in a run, the drawing on the copper polygons (needs a UX decision: in the 3D view or in the slice tab), and via barrels.

Caveat: during a run each layer's viewer copy refreshes on its own timer (about once a second), so for a moment they can come from slightly different sample counts; at the end all are synced. A still image picks its phase from the field's strongest moment, not the current's. Full colour is the 92nd percentile of |J_s|; the legend states it in A/m. 3D tab: checkboxes `copper`, `field`, `surface current` (all copper layers). Slice tab: `board outline`, `field`, `surface current`, `bottom layer` (the slice itself sits between the copper layers, so the current is drawn for the top copper layer, or the bottom one). Field off draws an empty background; the colour scale is not rescaled by the toggles. Layer state is not saved between sessions yet. `--ui-layers field,nofield,current` sets the layers for screenshots. Checked on the patch sample on the GPU (`--ui --ui-run`): the current shows on the patch in both tabs, the field layer switches off, `scripts/check.sh --gpu` is green. Not checked: the visual of animation over time, the cost of drawing very large cell counts (one quad per cell above 4 % of the scale), a board with several copper layers carrying current.

- **Gap found 2026-11-20 (user question): series parts that join an antenna line to an antenna (0 ohm links, matching inductors and capacitors) are not modelled.** Their pads are copper and are in the model, but nothing spans the gap between the pads, so the matching network is an open circuit. A metal body would short everything under the footprint, which is only a crude 0 ohm link. Needed: lumped R, L, C between a part's two pads (detect the pads of a two-terminal part, stamp an edge or a strip of edges across the gap like a port without a source), with R as a conductance on the edge, C as a permittivity or an added capacitance, and L as a current-state update (a solver change, needs its own scenario against the analytic impedance and a cited method). Values come from the part's value field when it parses (0R, 2.2nH, 1pF), else a user entry. Until this is built the run summary must say that two-terminal parts are not modelled; it does not yet.

- 2026-11-20, **Item 12 step (3) done: fitted resistors and 0 ohm links are modelled across their gap.** `board/parts.hpp` reads the value field (RKM code: 0R, 4k7, 2R2, 220 ohm, "10k 1%"; anything else, such as 2.2nH, is not a resistance) and decides **by value, not by reference** (user's note, checked read-only against the bomu board: L3 is an `Inductor_SMD:L_0201` footprint on B.Cu with the value `0R`, while L1 is 3.9nH, L2 4.7nH and C1 1pF): a mark (R, k, M, G), an ohm word or sign, or a plain zero says resistance whatever the reference, and the notes record when the reference disagreed; a bare number such as `47` counts as ohms only on an `R` reference. The footprint library name (`Inductor_SMD:`) is a further hint that the exporter does not send yet. For a fitted, selected, two-pad part the voxelizer finds the two pads' copper (the owner's shapes on the part's layer that contain the pad positions), takes the gap between the inner edges of the two copper areas and the overlap of their widths, and expands it with the existing port code (`expandGap`, refactored out of the port loop): a value above 0 becomes a passive lumped load appended after the real ports (the solver already treats ports after the first as undriven loads, so no solver change), and 0 forces the gap edges as a short. DNP parts, parts outside the selection, inductors and capacitors stay open and the notes name them. Scenarios (board_test): 0R makes the gap edge a forced edge, 100R makes a 100 ohm lumped edge (within 1e-9), DNP and unselected parts leave the gap open, an inductor is still reported as not modelled; parts_test: 13 value forms and 7 non-values. Not checked: a whole-run result with a resistor or 0 ohm link on a real board (for example S11 with the link fitted against open), a pad that is not a simple polygon, parts with the two pads on different copper layers, a gap that is wider than a few cells (the resistance is spread over the cells by the port rule). Next: step (4), the capacitor.
- 2026-11-20, **Item 12 step (4) done: fitted capacitors are modelled across their gap.** `fdtd::LumpedPort::capacitance` (F per edge) adds permittivity C dl / area to the edge in `Solver::buildCoefficients`, so the GPU, which takes its coefficient arrays from that solver, needs no change; a pure capacitor has infinite resistance (no conductance). `board::parseCapacitance` reads 1pF, 100nF, 0.1u, 4p7, 2n2, 10 pF, 1 uF (a prefix letter p, n or u is required; a bare number, 2.2nH and 0R are not capacitances). A part whose value says capacitance gets per-edge C = C * series / parallel, so the gap presents C overall whichever way the port code splits it; as for resistors the value wins over the reference letter, and the notes say when they disagree. Scenarios: solver, a source of 50 ohm into one edge carrying 10 pF in a PEC cavity gives V/Vs = 1/(1 + j w R C) within 4 % and 0.04 rad at 318 MHz and 1 GHz (passed; margins not measured); voxelizer (2 pF gives 2 pF within 1e-9, no conductance, not a short; value wins over an R reference); parts (10 value forms, 8 non-values). `scripts/check.sh --gpu` green. Not checked: a capacitor on the GPU in a whole run, a gap wider than one cell, the part's own parasitic inductance and ESR (an ideal capacitor, stated in the notes). Next: step (5), the inductor, which needs a solver current-state update and a read of reference [7]'s inductor section.
- 2026-11-20, **Capacitor accuracy measured (advisor review of step 4; table in `out/convergence/lumped_cap_rc.txt`, produced by the hidden test `Lumped capacitor accuracy table`, run with `tests_cpu "[.measure]"`).** One Ez edge, 50 ohm source, 24 mm PEC cavity, band 0.1 to 1.5 GHz, C = 0, 1, 3, 10, 30 pF, cells 1 mm and 2 mm, runs of 3000, 6000 and 12000 steps. Taking C_eff = Im(1 / (V/Vs)) / (w R) (V and Vs both at (n + 1/2) dt: the port couples the mean of E^n and E^(n+1) to the source evaluated at that time; stamping E at n dt gave a phase error that was partly this sampling): **the capacitor adds exactly its capacitance plus a constant stray.** C_eff - C is 26.6 fF at 1 mm cells and 53.2 fF at 2 mm cells for every C (spread under 0.1 fF for C up to 3 pF, 0.3 fF at 10 pF, 3 fF at 30 pF and 2 mm), flat across frequency (spread of C_eff over the band 0.01 to 0.4 fF once the run has rung down), and the real part of 1 / (V/Vs) stays within 1e-4 of 1 (no spurious loss). The stray is about 3.0 eps0 dl (eps0 dl = 8.85 fF per mm), grows with the cell size, and is the capacitance the surroundings add at the port edge; Demir [29] reports the same kind of parasitic capacitance and gives C_z = eps dx dy / dz per edge (his structure differs, so the factor does not carry over). Operational consequence: a 1 pF capacitor on 1 mm cells behaves as 1.027 pF (a 3 % effect), so the gap needs the finest cells and the run summary should state the stray; correcting it (Gillard [30]) is not done and would need a model of the surroundings. The earlier 9 % at 30 pF and 3000 steps was the run not having rung down (RC = 1.5 ns), not a capacitor error: runs need to be long compared with R C. The scenario now checks C_eff(10 pF) - C_eff(3 pF) = 7 pF within 0.05 % (measured 0.001 %), no dispersion between 318 MHz and 1 GHz within 0.1 %, and a stray of 0 to 5 eps0 dl (measured 3.0).
- 2026-11-20, **Latent wrong-answer path fixed (advisor):** lumped parts were appended to `model.ports`, so a board with a resistor or capacitor part and no defined port would have been driven through the part and reported as S11 of "R6". `PortPlan::lumped` now marks them and `sim::buildSolver` refuses with the existing "the model has no port to drive" message when the first port is lumped (scenario in `sim_test.cpp`). Parked, not blocking: every lumped load also gets a per-step E-log slot (2 x kMaxStepsPerSubmit words) in the GPU port buffer that it does not need.
- 2026-11-20, **Item 12 step (5), solver and GPU halves done: the lumped inductor** (`LumpedPort::inductance`; the voxelizer wiring, `parseInductance` and the bead are not done). Method, derived here and consistent in spirit with the ADE approach of Demir [29], whose update differs in form (a second-order auxiliary variable): a trapezoidal auxiliary current, I^(n+1/2) = I^(n-1/2) + dt dl / (2 L) (E^n + E^(n+1)), drawn from the edge as J = I / A. The E^(n+1) half is folded into the edge's conductivity (sigma += dt dl / (2 L A), exactly as the resistor is) and the E^n half comes from the stored current, so the update stays explicit in E and is the semi-implicit form; the stability literature [31] is not read. GPU: the port table grew from 4 to 8 words per port (gl = dt dl / (2 L), 1/area, the inductor current and the previous-step E as device state). Measured (`out/convergence/lumped_ind_rl.txt`, hidden test `Lumped inductor accuracy table`; 50 ohm source, 24 mm PEC cavity, L = 1, 3, 10, 30 nH, cells 1 and 2 mm, 6000 and 12000 steps, band 0.1 to 1.5 GHz, the capacitor table's stray capacitance removed): L_eff is within +0.001 % (1 mm cells) and +0.006 % (2 mm cells) of nominal, spread over the band at most 0.008 nH (30 nH, 2 mm), spurious conductance at most 4e-8 S. Stability (scenario, 99 % of the CFL limit, 20 000 steps, no resistor, field energy plus 1/2 L I^2): after the pulse the total never exceeds its settled value by more than 0.05 % at 0.01 nH, 1 nH and 100 nH on 1 mm cells (test bound 0.2 %). GPU against CPU on a 50 ohm port plus a parallel 600 ohm, 5 nH, 0.5 pF part: fields within 1e-3 of the peak and the part's voltage within 1e-3 (passed under the lock, validation layer clean). Not measured: an inductor in series with other lumped parts, a graded gap, more than 256 distinct edge coefficient sets (the GPU material palette is a byte index; the code does not yet say what happens beyond that).

Checked that the scenario discriminates by mutation: with the compensation term replaced by 0 the GPU returns 1e8 and the scenario fails (shader restored afterwards). Used 20 000 rather than 100 000 small samples to keep the run near 10 s. Not done from item 13: printing the device's float-control properties in the run summary; the CPU control run.
- 2026-11-20, **Item 13 baselines measured.** (1) **Accumulator cost** (`apps/bench`, GPU lock, release, 160^3 cells, step 1.75 ms = 2336 Mcells/s, copy 185 GB/s on the M1 Pro): one accumulate call (wall, includes the submit and wait, so an upper bound on kernel time) takes 0.71 ms for the display volume (64 x 64 x 32 voxels, 13 frequencies) and 0.52 ms for a far-field box (150 000 channels, 5 frequencies), i.e. 2.5 % and 1.8 % of a run that samples every 16 steps on that model size; the window runs six lists (slice, volume, surface current, power flow, far field, probes), so the total is of order 10 % there and larger on smaller models where a step is cheaper (not measured per model). (2) **Float controls** printed in the run header and in `summary.txt` (Vulkan 1.2 `VkPhysicalDeviceFloatControlsProperties`): on the M1 Pro through MoltenVK, fp32 denormals neither preserved nor flushed (implementation-defined), signed zero, infinity and NaN preserved, round-to-nearest-even not guaranteed. (3) **Known-correct values for the DFT**: `tests/cpu/dft_known_test.cpp` checks the running DFT of the project's own source pulse against its closed-form transform, and that closed form against 50-digit values from `scripts/make_golden.py` (mpmath 1.4.1, BSD-3-Clause, used only to generate the constants): the double transform is within 1.6e-8 of the in-band peak at 2.0, 2.3, 2.4, 2.45, 2.5, 2.6 and 3.0 GHz (the limit is the pulse being cut at 8 tau, where it is still 1.1e-7 of its height, the same abrupt start a real run has), the float Kahan transform within 4.9e-8, the closed form within 1e-12 of the golden values (the double rounding of a 680 rad phase), and out-of-band bins (exact values below 1e-20) within the same bound, i.e. no leakage. Not done from item 13: the CPU control for the compensated-sum scenario, pairwise sums in the far-field transform (its sums are in double on the host), a ULP table for the trig factors, fp32 against double solver drift over 100 000 steps.

The default stays `signed` for both themes, so nothing changes until chosen; which map suits which theme is for the user to judge from the screenshots (`out/magma.png`; `--set volume_colour_map=viridis` etc.). Scenarios: the four maps have 256 entries, their end colours match the published ones within 1 level (viridis 440154 to FDE725, magma 000004 to FCFDBF, inferno 000004 to FCFFA4, plasma 0D0887 to F0F921), luma never falls by more than 2 of 255 and rises by at least 150 along each map; a table colours the volume by magnitude whatever the sign (a green table gives identical green for positive and negative blocks), blue-to-red gives a redder strong block. Not done: a quick picker in the 3D tab (the Settings dialog has it), a light-theme-specific map, colour-blind simulation of the maps (the authors' statement about viridis is quoted, not verified), the slice tab does not use the maps.
- 2026-11-20, **Item 11(e) built: Smith chart, impedance, VSWR.** `fdtd/impedance.hpp`: Z = z0 (1 + G) / (1 - G), VSWR = (1 + |G|) / (1 - |G|), return loss and mismatch loss. Scenario against closed forms: G = 0 gives 50 ohm and VSWR 1; G = 1/3 gives 100 ohm, VSWR 2, return loss 9.5424250944 dB and mismatch loss 0.5115252245 dB (each within 1e-9 relative); G = -1/3 gives 25 ohm; G = j/3 gives 40 + j30 ohm; a short is 0 ohm and an open infinite with infinite VSWR; Z to G round trip within 1e-12. The S11 tab has three views as toggle buttons (each a named, checkable accessibility node): the dB plot (default, unchanged), a Smith chart normalised to the port impedance (constant-resistance circles, constant-reactance arcs, the trace over the sweep, orange dots at the ends of the band, a white dot and a text block for the best match: frequency, Z, VSWR, return loss) and impedance and VSWR (R and X clipped to +/- 5 z0 above, VSWR up to 10 with the 2:1 line below, the band shaded). Every view has a spoken summary (Image node). Checked by screenshot on the patch sample on the GPU: the best match at 2.187 GHz reads Z = 17.5 + j20.6 ohm, VSWR 3.39, return loss 5.3 dB, consistent with the dB plot's -5.4 dB best match (|G| = 0.54 gives VSWR 3.35 and RL 5.3 dB; the small difference is the grid of frequencies). Not done: marker interaction (hover or click to read a point), a normalisation other than the port impedance, Touchstone export of the impedance, a check of the chart against a reference tool. Next: (f) the near-to-far-field transform.
- 2026-11-20, **Item 11(f), core built and validated: the near-to-far-field transform.** `fdtd/ntff.hpp` and `ntff.cpp`: a closed box of Yee samples (faces on node planes), surface currents J = n x H and M = -n x E read from accumulated E and H phasors (H as the mean of the two samples half a cell either side of the face; each tangential component is a current element placed where the grid holds it, with its dual-cell area, half cells at the box edges), the radiation integrals N and L with exp(+j k r_hat . r') (the running DFT of a real signal is the exp(+j w t) phasor) and U = k^2 / (32 pi^2 eta) (|L_phi + eta N_theta|^2 + |L_theta - eta N_phi|^2), integrated on a theta x phi grid on all hardware threads; `surfaceFlux` (1/2 Re of E conj(H) through the same box) as an independent check; `antennaFigures` (radiated power, available power |Vs|^2 / (4 z0), accepted power times 1 - |S11|^2, radiation efficiency, directivity, gain, realised gain; power-like quantities are in transform units, doubled against the phasor formulas, so only ratios are physical). Written from the textbook formulation; the paper [28] (Luebbers et al.) has not been read, so the sign and ordering of the terms stand on the checks below, not on a citation. Known-correct values and results: (1) analytic element: a z-directed current element of 2 A m at 1 GHz gives U = eta k^2 |I l|^2 sin^2(theta) / (32 pi^2) within 1e-12 relative at every sampled angle, radiated power eta (k |I l|)^2 / (12 pi) and directivity 1.5 within 2e-4 on a 180 x 72 grid; a magnetic element radiates the same with M / eta; two elements a quarter wavelength apart follow the array factor within 1e-9. (2) FDTD short dipole (0.1 wavelength wire, 50 ohm port, CPML box, 1.5 GHz; table `out/convergence/ntff_short_dipole.txt`, 8 configurations: cells 2 to 4 mm, CPML 8 and 12 cells, Huygens box 1, 3 and 6 cells inside the CPML, 3500 to 6000 steps): far-field power over surface flux 1.0001 to 1.0012, twice the far-field power over the port's input power Re(V I*) 0.9994 to 1.00005, directivity 1.5059 to 1.5071 (1.5 for an infinitely short dipole; the scenario asserts 0.3 %, 0.3 % and 1 %). The input power needs V at (n + 1/2) dt, as the port current is the mean of two steps: with V at n dt the dipole's mostly reactive impedance made the real power wrong by a factor 7.6 (a sampling skew of 0.025 rad against a reactance to resistance ratio of about 500). (3) FDTD half-wave dipole (33-cell wire, 0.495 wavelength, 3 mm cells, n 84): directivity 1.663 = 2.21 dBi against 2.15 dBi for the ideal thin dipole (a 31-cell wire, 0.465 wavelength, gives 2.153 dBi), power balance 1.00006 and 1.00028, radiation efficiency from S11 and the source alone 1.0008 (lossless wire), |S11|^2 0.23 against 50 ohm; the scenario asserts 2.15 dBi within 0.1 dB and the efficiency within 0.5 %. Not done: refining the half-wave case beyond 3 mm cells; a dielectric-loaded or board-mounted antenna against a reference tool; the run-time wiring (the box needs a free-space margin inside the CPML, the phasor list is large: about 230 000 channels on the patch sample's grid, so fewer frequencies than the animation's) and the pattern view and numbers in the window. Next: that wiring.
- 2026-11-20, **Item 11(f) wired into the window: the Far field tab.** The run builds a Huygens box two cells inside the absorbing layer when it clears the board (the outline laterally, the copper planes vertically; otherwise the tab says "increase the air margin" and no far field is computed), accumulates its E and H phasors on the backend at up to five frequencies of the sweep (a sixth `PhasorFeed`; 149 252 channels and 100 592 current elements on the patch sample, 5 frequencies, synced every 4 s and at the end), and after the run integrates the pattern on a 36 x 72 grid (5 degree steps, all threads, about 1 s per frequency) and makes the figures from S11 and the source spectrum (`fdtd::sourceSpectrum` now includes the sample interval; a first version without it gave efficiencies of order 1e8). Tab: a frequency slider, three polar cuts (x-z, y-z, x-y) in dBi over 30 dB with rings every 10 dB, a switch between realised gain (default) and directivity, and the numbers (peak value and direction, directivity, radiation efficiency, S11, gain and realised gain) in text and in the accessibility description. Checked on the patch sample on the GPU: far-field power equals the surface flux at all five frequencies (4.22e-24 and 4.22e-24 at 2.04 GHz); at 2.04 GHz directivity 6.00 dBi at broadside (a patch antenna is typically 6 to 7 dBi), radiation efficiency 40 % with S11 -0.4 dB (the FR4 substrate is lossy; the figure is the model's own, not a measurement). Limits: no 3D pattern surface (only cuts); the 5 degree grid makes the cuts slightly jagged; five frequencies only; efficiency counts only losses inside the model (dielectric loss tangent, port resistance excluded as it is the source); the patch result is not compared with a reference tool, only with the closed-form dipole checks and the flux balance. Next: (g) sweeps.
- 2026-11-20, **Item 11(g) built: sweeps, with overlaid S11, and geometry overrides.** `app::runSweep` (`src/app/sweep.*`) runs the ordinary pipeline (prepare, `runOnePort`, `writeResults`) once per value of one settings-registry key, in its own folder `<results>/sweep-<time>/<key>-<value>/` (Touchstone, summary naming the changed setting, notes), and writes a combined `sweep.json` (value, folder, best match, frequencies and the S11 real and imaginary parts, or the error). A point that fails (value out of range, model too large) is recorded with its message and the sweep goes on; values are a list `a,b,c` or a range `start:stop:count`, at most 25 points. Geometry overrides (`src/board/geometry_override.*`): `NET.x=MM` or `NET.y=MM` scales all copper and vias on a net along that axis about the net's low edge, so its extent changes by MM millimetres (a feed line on the same net scales with it); applied to a copy of the snapshot, the board file is never touched; every override is a line in the run notes and summary. Sweep key `geom.NET.x` sweeps a geometry override. Command line: `--sweep KEY=values` (headless table, or with `--ui` the overlay), `--geom NET.x=MM` (standing override). Window: Preview tab header "Sweep a setting (optional)" (setting name, values, Run sweep, status) and an S11-tab view "Sweep overlay" (Okabe-Ito colours and alternating dashes, legend with each point's best match, a spoken summary); the sweep runs on its own thread with the run's backend. Scenarios: value parsing (list, range, empty, bad, too many); a CPU sweep of `sweep_points` 11, 21 and 1 (one point per value in order, the bad one carries its error and does not stop the sweep, point sizes 11 and 21, finite and passive S11, a folder with Touchstone and summary per point, the combined file, the summary naming the changed setting; the runs are cut at 150 steps to stay near 20 s, so the physics is not checked there); a GPU sweep of `fine_cell_mm` 1.0 and 0.5 mm on the 100 ohm load, coarse mesh, band 0.3 to 0.6 GHz (hidden test `[.sweepgpu]`, run from the release build because the debug build with the validation layer needs many minutes): |S11| within 0.06 of the analytic 1/3 at every in-band frequency (measured 0.345 to 0.377) and the two cell sizes agree within 0.01 (measured 0.002); geometry overrides: a 10 x 8 mm patch stretched by +1 mm along x is 11 mm long from the same start and keeps its width, the via at its middle moves to 0.0155, other nets are unchanged, shortening along y, composition in order, refusal for an unknown net or a stretch that removes the extent, and the NET.axis=mm parser (dots in net names, bad axis, bad number). Observations made while building it, not resolved: on the same load board with the default Wi-Fi band the fine preset gave a best match of -1.7 dB against -10.5 dB for coarse and medium (the structure is not electrically small there, so the analytic value does not apply, but the spread between presets is large and unexplained); with band 0.3 to 0.6 GHz the S11 magnitude is within 3 % of 1/3 but its phase turns by 0.1 rad across the band (an electrical offset the closed form does not include, not examined); stretching the patch sample's RF net by -1, 0 and +1 mm along x moved the best match from 2.146 to 2.146 and 2.138 GHz at the coarse mesh (the resolution is too coarse to resolve a 1 mm change of a long patch; the sweep tool works, the sensitivity of that design to this knob is not established). Not done: persisting a sweep definition in the sidecar file, a sweep over two keys, parallel points, comparing a sweep with a reference tool. Next: (h) VTK export, threshold isosurface, probes.

- 2026-11-20, **Validation finding, resolved: wide ports read an apparent loss; narrow ports are exact (Next steps 2: microstrip).** Boards from `scripts/make_validation_boards.py` (3 mm trace over a ground plane on 1.6 mm FR-4, vertical 50 ohm port, via short; `microstrip_short.json` and variants in `out/samples/`). A lossless shorted line must reflect everything, and with the first boards it did not: with a 3 mm port on the 3 mm trace the 39.3 mm line gave |S11| from 0.69 to 0.23 to 0.85 across 1.35 to 2.75 GHz (GPU, medium mesh, 1.5 to 2.5 GHz), a 4 mm shorted stub gave 0.38 to 0.48, even a bare 3 x 3 mm pad on a closed PEC box gave 0.98 at 1 GHz down to 0.886 at 3 GHz. Ruled out in turn: mesh (coarse, medium, fine give the same curves), a run cut short (decay threshold 1e-3 and 1e-5, and 20 000 against 60 000 steps, give the same), board size, radiation (the far-field efficiency was 0 % while the port "accepted" 52 %), dielectric loss (zero), the number of series cells across the dielectric (1, 2, 4, 8: identical), the via, the CPML (a closed lossless box shows it too). **Cause (shown by changing only the port width on the bare pad in a closed box):** port width 3.0 mm gives |S11| 0.980, 0.935, 0.886 at 1, 2, 3 GHz; 2.0 mm gives 0.985, 0.949, 0.905; 1.0 mm gives 1.0000, 0.9999, 0.9996; 0.5 mm gives 1.0000 at all three. The port is a row of parallel strips, each its own source and resistor; S11 = 2 V / Vs - 1 uses the mean strip voltage, but when the strips see different voltages (a port as wide as the pad, with fringing at its ends) the mean of unit-modulus reflection coefficients of different phase has modulus below 1. It is an artefact of the one-port definition, not dissipation: the true accepted power is the sum over strips of V_k I_k, which is zero for a lossless load. **Validation result with a 0.8 mm port on the same 3 mm line:** |S11| = 0.999 to 0.994 across 1.35 to 2.75 GHz (lossless, as required) and an effective permittivity from the unwrapped phase slope (39.3 mm between the port and the via centres) of 3.276, against 3.3255 from the Hammerstad and Jensen quasi-static formula (as coded in the script from memory of that paper, which has not been read; the formula and the 39.3 mm reference length are both open to a few percent): within 1.5 %. **Consequences:** (1) the preview and run notes now flag a port that is wider than 60 % of the copper it feeds (`board_test`: a 2.8 mm port on a 2.8 mm pad is flagged with both widths, a 0.8 mm one is not); (2) a scenario (`sim_test`, slow) pins both behaviours in a closed box: 1 mm port |S11| = 1 within 1e-3, 3 mm port below 0.95 and worse with frequency; (3) the far-field radiation efficiency, which divides by the accepted power P_avail (1 - |S11|^2), is overstated for wide ports and should be trusted only with a narrow port until a strip-resolved accepted power (the sum of V_k I_k per strip) replaces it; (4) every S11 magnitude in this file from a wide multi-strip port carries this error; the patch sample's port has width 0, a single strip, so it is not affected (checked: the far-field efficiency from the strip-resolved accepted power, 40 % at 2.04 GHz, equals the one from S11 to the digit). **Done after the diagnosis:** `RunResult` keeps every strip's voltage (`stripVoltage`, `stripResistance`) and `fdtd::acceptedPower` sums V_k I_k over the strips (V averaged over two steps to refer it to the half step of the source and the current); the far-field efficiency and gain use it. Scenarios: on the lossless pad in the closed box with the 3 mm port the strip-resolved accepted power is 0.05, 0.30 and 0.84 % of the available power at 1, 2 and 3 GHz where (1 - |S11|^2) claims 3.9, 12.6 and 21.5 %; on the 100 ohm load it is 0.8884 to 0.8868 of the available power against the exact 8/9 = 0.8889 (within 0.2 %). Not done: the strip-resolved S11 and accepted power; a convergence of the effective permittivity with cell size; the open-circuit and matched-line variants against closed forms (the matched line gave |S11| of 0.16 to 0.28 with a wide port, not yet repeated with a narrow one); the quarter-wave monopole.
- 2026-11-20, **Patch convergence (Next steps 1), measured with the new sweep driver** (`emfdtd out/samples/patch_2g45.json --gpu --sweep fine_cell_mm=1.0,0.5,0.35`, GPU, medium mesh otherwise; the sample is the project's own 36.9 x 28.5 mm patch on 1.6 mm FR-4 with epsilon_r 4.5 and loss tangent 0.02, probe-fed 4.2 mm off its centre line, 109 x 109 x 45 cells at the default): best match -5.35 dB at 2.1787 GHz (1.0 mm fine cells), -5.52 dB at 2.1787 GHz (0.5 mm), -5.36 dB at 2.1869 GHz (0.35 mm): the FDTD result is converged in the fine cell size to 0.4 % in frequency and 0.2 dB in depth. **It does not match the cavity-model prediction:** the transmission-line cavity model with the Hammerstad edge extension (formulas written from memory of the paper, which has not been read, so a few percent are open to doubt) gives epsilon_eff 4.10, an edge extension of 0.73 mm per side and resonances at 1.93 GHz along the 36.9 mm length and 2.45 GHz along the 28.5 mm width (the design target), whereas the S11 minimum sits at 2.18 GHz, 11 % below the 2.45 GHz mode, and the resistance and reactance plots show only a weak resonance (R up to about 25 ohm, X a nearly constant +20 to +30 ohm over 2.04 to 2.86 GHz). Further discriminators with the sweep driver (same sample, GPU): `dielectric_cells_min` 3 and 6 both give the best match at 2.1869 GHz (-5.28 and -5.27 dB), `boundary_distance_wavelengths` 0.25 gives 2.1869 GHz (-5.28 dB) and 0.5 gives 2.1461 GHz (-5.22 dB), so the dip moves by under 2 % with the domain size and not at all with the dielectric resolution: its position (2.15 to 2.19 GHz, against 2.45 GHz from the cavity model) is not a numerics effect of either. Plausibly the resistance peak is right (the feed is 10 mm from the near edge of the 28.5 mm resonant dimension, where the mode's field is about 0.45 of its edge value, so the input resistance is about 0.2 of the edge resistance, a few tens of ohm at a loaded Q near 20) and it is the frequency that is puzzling. Not explained, to investigate: the series inductance of the probe (a 1.6 mm feed pin is about 1 nH, 14 ohm at 2.2 GHz, which pulls a match down in frequency but cannot move a resonance), the substrate loss (tan d 0.02 broadens it; Q is low), the feed position, whether the resonance seen is the other mode, the 0.2 mm port gap. The earlier note that the dip sat near 2.4 GHz at 1 mm cells was made before the port rework and is not reproduced. The patch sample's absolute S11 is therefore not validated; only its convergence is. Next (needs a decision, not started): compare with a reference solver on the same patch (openEMS is GPL: run it for validation only, never read its source), or with a published patch whose measured S11 is available.

Slice tab at 1920x1080 (`--ui-bench 20`): slice 16.0 ms before, 3.2 ms now. Volume tab: 4.6 ms. Both views share one render `vkcore::Context` made on first use. **Two bugs found by the validation layer (`EMFDTD_VALIDATE=1` with `VK_LAYER_PATH=/usr/local/share/vulkan/explicit_layer.d`) and fixed:** (1) when the text size changes inside a frame the font atlas was cleared after the backend's `NewFrame`, so that frame drew with a null descriptor set: harmless-looking on KosmicKrisp, a segfault in MoltenVK's `bindDescriptorSets` (fix: `GpuUi::fontsChanged` recreates the font texture at once); (2) the swapchain signalled one render-finished semaphore for every image (fix: one per image). The window now runs clean under validation on both drivers (90 frames each); the GPU tests pass on both. **Decision on R2 (no readback): deferred.** With the UI on the GPU the CPU-side frame is 0.5 to 0.8 ms and a view costs 3 to 5 ms including its readback; removing the readback would save part of that, so it is only worth doing if a measurement shows the view cost matters (for example a 4K display or a larger display volume). **Still open:** the field images are still composed on the CPU and uploaded as one RGBA frame each frame (about 8 MB at 1080p); the Smith chart, plots and board overlays are ImGui draw lists and so are already GPU-drawn; the headless screenshot path is unchanged and CPU only.

Demir, "Formulations for Modeling Voltage Sources with RLC Impedances in the FDTD Method," ACES Journal 31(9):1020-1026, September 2016 (open access, journals.riverpublishers.com/index.php/ACES/article/view/9897; DOI not seen on the page text read). READ (sections II, III and the references, from the open PDF): the auxiliary-differential-equation update of a series or parallel R, L, C connected across one Yee edge (a second-order auxiliary variable Q, calculated at integer time steps from E^n and Q^n, Q^(n-1)), the multi-cell rule (equivalent voltage V/nz and equivalent R, L, C per edge), the numerical parasitic capacitance C_z = eps dx dy / dz per edge (their eq. 53) and validation against the expected impedance of series and parallel RLC, RL and RC over a wide band (figures only, no error numbers read). It treats a source with an RLC impedance; a passive load is the same with V_S = 0.

Citerne, "Correction procedures for the numerical parasitic elements associated with lumped elements in global electromagnetic simulators," IEEE Trans. Microw. Theory Tech. 46(9):1298-1306, 1998 (as cited in [29]; DOI not verified; not read). Cited for the original lumped-element formulation and for removing the parasitic capacitance.
