Exporter against the live KiCad (IFA copy open): the stackup entries carry F.Cu 35.56 um, In1.Cu and In2.Cu 36 um, B.Cu 35.56 um. The Antenna tab at 720 x 520 wraps and scrolls; its accessibility dump exits 0. Open: the generator's GND vias landing on other nets in the full IFA board, the one-sided-ground flag in `FeedReport`, the TI return-loss comparison.

**Should the feed estimate ignore bends and stubs? (2026-11-23, asked by the user; advisor consulted.)** Short answer: the number should not model them, but the row must not hide them, and on this project's own board it was hiding something larger than the effect the row was built to show. *Two different quantities were conflated.* The impedance printed is that of a uniform section, and a cross-section solve cannot contain a bend or a stub, which are local discontinuities between uniform lines whose Z0 they leave unchanged. What the antenna sees at its pad is the whole line, and there the row said nothing. *What the row was hiding (measured on the IFA test board, `snap.json`):* the feed is not uniform. Its width samples between 1 and 6 mm from the antenna pad were reduced to one median (0.76 mm, 58 ohm by the closed form); the samples show 0.48 mm from 1.0 to 1.4 mm (TI's feed neck; with the gap measured there, about 91 ohm), a ramp to 0.76 mm over about 0.2 mm, then 0.76 mm to 6 mm, and the copper turns or branches about 3.5 mm from the pad. The neck is a 30 ohm step at the antenna end, larger than the 3 to 4 ohm copper-thickness effect. Other parts' pads on the feed net also sat in the window and counted as line. *Sizing the effects (ideal lossless sections cascaded with textbook ABCD matrices, `scripts/antenna/cascade.py`, 2.44 GHz, effective permittivity 2.8, guided wavelength 73.4 mm):* a 0.4 mm neck at 91 ohm on a 50 ohm line moves the match from perfect to -33 dB (about 2 ohm of reactance) and on the IFA's 58 ohm line changes Zin from 66.8 -2.8j to 66.1 -4.9j ohm (return loss -16.7 to -16.8 dB, the line's own mismatch dominates); an open stub of 91 ohm across a 50 ohm line costs -32.6 dB at 1 mm, -22.9 dB at 3 mm, -16.3 dB at 6 mm, -10.4 dB at 10 mm, -3.3 dB at 15 mm, and shorts the line at a quarter wave (18.4 mm). So a corner or step much shorter than about a twentieth of a wavelength (about 3.7 mm) is second-order, a stub of that length or more is not. The cascade has no junction or corner capacitance and no dispersion, and was not checked against the FDTD solver (two GPU controls were proposed: the real feed against a straightened, un-tapered one; they were not run because the outcome would only have decided whether to model discontinuities, which this change does not do). *What was built (scenarios first, `antenna_test.cpp`):* `checkFeed` now returns `segments` (each stretch of steady width with its side gap and its closed-form Z0, short ramps under 0.3 mm omitted), `otherParts` (refs of other parts' pads touching the feed net in the window, whose copper no longer counts as line: they join the flood fill so the line beyond a series part is still found, but are labelled), and `turnsAtMm` (a sample whose copper runs far both ways, over 1.5 times the line width: a corner, a T, the start of a stub). The feed row prints a sentence for each: "uniform over the stretch measured (1 to 6 mm from the pad)" when nothing is found, else the width changes with each stretch's impedance, the parts named, the turns located, with the sizing above in the sentence. *Not done:* the length of a stub (it needs the dead-end length of a branch; the report names where the copper branches, not how long the branch is), modelling any discontinuity, and the FDTD controls. *Sources:* the physics is textbook transmission-line theory (D. M. Pozar, Microwave Engineering, chapters 2 and 4; a book, no DOI). For closed-form discontinuity models, two usual references, checked against Crossref but not read (IEEE, paywalled), so no formula from them is used: R. J. P. Douville and D. S. James, "Experimental Study of Symmetric Microstrip Bends and Their Compensation", IEEE Trans. Microwave Theory Tech. 26(3), 175-182 (1978), DOI 10.1109/TMTT.1978.1129340; M. Kirschning, R. H. Jansen and N. H. L. Koster, "Measurement and Computer-Aided Modeling of Microstrip Discontinuities by an Improved Resonator Method", IEEE MTT-S Int. Microwave Symp. Digest, 495-497 (1983), DOI 10.1109/MWSYM.1983.1130959. A third lookup (Hammerstad and Bekkadal's discontinuity handbook) was refused by Crossref with HTTP 429 and not retried.

**Correction (2026-11-23): the board has no via defect.** The entries above that call the "via ... lands on copper of net X: it short-circuits them" run notes on the full IFA snapshot "a layout defect in the generator" (the 3D-view check, the verification paragraph's "Open" list) were wrong. KiCad's own DRC on the board (`out/nrf52840-ifa/drc.rpt`) reports no shorting items, no via centre lies inside another net's copper in the snapshot, and the flagged vias (at 139.72/105.40, 145.70/101.30 and 150.55/101.20 mm) have pad edges 0.145 to 0.2 mm from the nearest foreign copper, legal under the board's 0.14 mm clearance. The notes came from the coarse mesh the run used (cells of 0.4 mm and more): a via column and a pad 0.15 mm apart land on the same grid nodes, the same sub-cell-gap effect as the coplanar feed. What stays true: the full snapshot's only port is at the chip, so that run was still not an antenna test. *Fixed in the voxelizer (scenario first):* the via note now measures the real gap between the via's edge and the foreign copper. Copper under the via is still "short-circuits them"; copper beside it that is apart on the board reads "on the board they are G mm apart; only the grid joins them (its cells are wider than that gap), so refine there (--refine) or treat the model as shorted".

**One-sided ground in the feed report: BUILT (2026-11-23).** `FeedReport::oneSided` (ground copper beside the line on one side only, found in at least half the samples) with that side's gap; the closed form still reads it as microstrip and the finding says so; `solveFeed` builds the cross-section with ground on one side (`groundedCoplanarSection(..., true, false)`), and the scenario shows the solve between the microstrip and the two-sided values. Before this, a line with a pour on one side was silently read as microstrip.

**First valid IFA antenna solve (2026-11-23): S11 does not show a resonance, cause not found.** Model: `out/nrf52840-ifa/valid/snapshot.json` (= `c_full.json`: the antenna present, the feed cut before it and a 0.3 mm port across the 1.2 mm gap at y 105.4 mm), `--profile bluetooth --nets /ANT_FEED --ground-nets GND --part R2=open --set mesh_preset=coarse --set decay_threshold=0.01 --refine 186.4 104.2 189.2 108.2 0.06`; 3.0 M cells, smallest cell 0.0232 mm, 116 272 steps in 424 s on the GPU (821 Mcells/s), run `run-20261123-093609`. Result: S11 between -2.8 and -4.5 dB over the whole reported band 2.04 to 2.856 GHz, never below -10 dB, no dip; impedance about 11 + 12j ohm at 2.45 GHz (resistance 8 to 14 ohm, reactance +8 to +13 ohm across the band). TI's note (SWRU120D, measured on its own CC2430DB reference board, not this board) states reflection below -15 dB and less than 10 percent reflected power over more than 300 MHz. So either the model or the layout is off, and one run at one mesh cannot say which. *Known facts about the model:* the run notes list the feed and the antenna's grounded leg as touching on F.Cu (868 grid edges) - expected for an inverted-F, whose short leg ties the arm to ground - and a 0.06 mm gap one cell wide near (184.13, 107.51); the fine box covers only the feed, so the arm (traces about 0.5 mm) is on the coarse grid; ground is the board's 80 x 37 mm pour, not TI's board; no halved-cell rerun; component bodies ignored; copper as zero-thickness sheets. *Next diagnostics, in order of cost:* (1) the same run with the antenna region refined to 0.2 mm cells (running; result below if it finished); (2) the control that decides whether the port sees the antenna at all: the same port and an open-ended stub in place of the antenna (the open and short controls already agree with theory, so a port or feed fault is unlikely); (3) compare the arm's modelled length and the leg's position with TI's Table 1-1 dimensions in the generator; (4) an antenna-only variant on a ground plane the size of TI's. Nothing here is a statement about the antenna's real performance.

**Diagnostic (1) of the IFA solve, result (2026-11-23):** the same model with the whole antenna region (175.5 to 192.5 mm by 99.5 to 110.5 mm) at 0.2 mm cells besides the 0.06 mm feed box (4.37 M cells, 114 160 steps in 509 s, run `run-20261123-094409` under `out/nrf52840-ifa/valid2/`) gives S11 -3.3 dB and 10.0 + 11.9j ohm at 2.448 GHz (before: -3.7 dB and 11.1 + 12.3j), still -2.6 to -4.0 dB over the whole band, no dip. So the missing resonance is not the arm's mesh. What remains: the model's topology or the layout (the shorted leg and feed tap, the arm length against TI's Table 1-1, ground size), and the port seeing the antenna through the feed neck. Next: diagnostics (2) to (4) above. Still no statement about the antenna's real performance.

## GPU farm: jobs on other machines' GPUs (planned and built 2026-11-25; requested by the user)

**Status at the end of 2026-11-25 (read this first; the paragraphs below are the history, in the order things happened).** *Built and tested:* `emfdtd --device` / `--list-devices`; device-resident buffers for discrete GPUs (24 to 1185 Mcells/s on the RTX, 49 times faster); the default device is the newest Vulkan driver; an MSVC build of the solver; the Python farm in `plugin/src/emfdtd_plugin/farm/` (TLS 1.3 only, private CA, one-time-code pairing with a pinned fingerprint, per-request policy, validated jobs, fair-share scheduler with leases, transfers, audit log, retention, a command line, a coordinator-held GPU turn with watchdog and conditional release); 134 plugin tests with a stub solver and 53 C++ assertions for the window client; the Farm tab in the emfdtd window (roles with a confirmation, devices, jobs with a progress bar and Cancel, invitations, members, Revoke) checked by `scripts/ui_farm_check.sh`; `emfdtd` prints a progress estimate. *Used for real:* the coordinator runs on this Mac (`the coordinator's LAN address:7420`, `gates.json` for `win-egpu`), the Windows eGPU box runs a worker (`--turn-from-coordinator`, 30 minute ceiling), and the Mac is also the client; three jobs have run on the RTX through the farm (two patch jobs, the TI board baseline `nd_med_01`, whose result equals the Mac run to 4.2e-7). *Not built:* "Where a run goes" on the run preview; the queue is not saved across a coordinator restart; no memory-fit check at placement; no service installers (a terminal or the user's own service manager); the Windows panel (`runCapture` is POSIX only); a control socket; discovery; preparing the model before taking the GPU turn (design in the idea paragraph below); a Linux NVIDIA worker. *Rules that came out of it:* TLS 1.3 required for everyone with no fallback; no shims in tests (a Python without TLS 1.3 fails, it does not skip); a worker never uses the GPU outside a turn; Windows GPU runs need the user's approval each time (the Windows-side session also gates them).

*Why:* the solver uses one GPU of the machine it runs on. The user has a Boot Camp Mac (Windows 10, Vulkan, RTX 4060 Ti eGPU, plus a Vulkan 1.1 Radeon that is filtered out) and may add another Apple Silicon Mac, a Linux box with an NVIDIA GPU or something else, so the design is platform and vendor neutral (Vulkan is the common layer). Work should be spread over one or more GPUs on this machine and over the network, coordinated by pushed events, secure on a local network. The M1 Pro is one GPU: local parallelism here is one job at a time; `--device` and per-device slots matter on multi-GPU hosts.

*Decisions made by the user (2026-11-25):* (1) **Off by default**: nothing imports, listens, dials or creates identity until the user enables a role (coordinator, worker), each separately, machine-wide. (2) **Configured in the emfdtd window** (a Farm screen; the KiCAD action stays minimal and just opens that window), with machine-wide state in the user config folder, never the board sidecar (a copied project must not enable networking). First-version contents: enable/disable per role, pair devices, devices and queue, choose where a run goes. UX is written into `docs/ux.md` and approved before the screen is coded. (3) Cross-platform: Apple Silicon Mac, Windows, Linux, any Vulkan 1.2 GPU.

*Zero-trust frame (NIST SP 800-207; the standard text was not fetched, only summaries):* network location is not trust; a policy decision and enforcement point authorizes each request; least privilege; continuous verification. Applied: mutual TLS with a private CA on the coordinator and device certificates (90-day, revocable); the coordinator is the decision point with a per-device policy file; workers dial out and never listen; the worker builds the solver command line itself from validated fields (no shell, no pass-through flags, fixed binary, per-job directory, size/time/step caps, sha256 on every file, audit log).

*Design:* a stdlib-only Python 3.9 package `plugin/src/emfdtd_plugin/farm/` and a CLI `plugin/tools/emfdtd_farm.py` (`enable`, `disable`, `reset`, `init`, `invite`, `enroll`, `serve`, `worker`, `status`, `devices`, `submit`, `cancel`, `results`, `revoke`; `--json` for the window). Plain TLS 1.3 sockets with length-prefixed JSON frames (WebSocket needs Python 3.11, TLS-PSK needs 3.13). Events pushed down the worker's connection: `offer`, `cancel`, `drain`, `config`; worker to coordinator: `hello` (devices, driver, solver version, data hash), `heartbeat`, `accept`, `progress`, `result`, `fail`. Bulk data uses a second dial-out connection per transfer (never the control stream). Pairing: coordinator-generated worker key with the system `openssl` (stdlib cannot make certificates; Windows has no openssl, so the worker never generates keys), delivered once through an enrolment endpoint that exists only while an invite is active (separate TLS context without client certificate, attempt and rate limits, one-time code proved by an HMAC over both fingerprints). The window never touches the network: it runs the CLI with `--json` and talks to the running farm process through a local control socket (Unix socket, mode 0600; Windows named pipe with a per-user ACL).

*Scheduling:* leases renewed by heartbeats, requeue on loss (max 2 attempts), late results discarded, client UUID job ids, per-device concurrency of one, placement by memory estimate (`--dry-run`) and measured Mcells/s; sweeps split into single-point jobs (`--set KEY=value`, `--geom NET.x=MM`) and the coordinator rebuilds `sweep.json` from the returned `s11.s1p` (a test compares it with the solver's own). Results record device and driver; a sweep stays on one device class unless the user allows mixing; a fixed cross-device check board is run when a device registers and a device that disagrees beyond the stated tolerance is marked unverified. The coordinator refuses a worker whose solver version or `data/` hash differs. Local jobs take the machine-wide GPU lock (CLAUDE.md rule).

*Solver change:* `--device SELECTOR` (index or UUID prefix, via `VkPhysicalDeviceIDProperties::deviceUUID` into `ContextOptions`; vendor/device id cannot tell two identical cards apart) and `--list-devices`; `EMFDTD_DEVICE` for workers; not a registry setting (machine-specific). Facts it rests on: `pickDevice` `src/vkcore/context.cpp:57-91`; no flag reaches `ContextOptions` today; one `Context` = one device, so one process per GPU; `--sweep` (`src/app/sweep.cpp:66-140`) exits 0 even when a point fails; results dir `<project>/em-fdtd/results/run-<time>/` has `s11.s1p`, `summary.txt`, `settings.json`, `board.json`.

*Phases (all but 5 and 6 done; see the status above):* 0 this section; 1 `--device`/`--list-devices`; 2 protocol, PKI, policy (loopback BDD with a stub solver); 3 coordinator, worker, scheduler, failure injection; 3b UX approval and control interface, then the Farm screen (a11y-checked at 720x520); 4 one real job on this Mac through the GPU lock, compared with a direct run (a second Apple Silicon Mac, if the user has one, is the cheapest first remote worker); 5 Windows and Linux builds of `emfdtd` (native Visual Studio versus the mongo project's mingw cross-build, `docs/PLAN.md` hardware section; the CLI links GLFW and ImGui, so even headless needs them or a split target); 6 Boot Camp/Linux workers (Python 3.9+ installed by the user; stub job first, then the cross-device check, then real jobs; Windows GPU runs need the user's approval); 7 `docs/farm.md`, CHANGELOG, CLAUDE.md commands. Licensing: stdlib only; `openssl` is a runtime tool on the coordinator only; `zeroconf` (LGPL) not used, discovery is manual host:port.

*Security tests that must pass:* unknown or revoked certificate rejected (also mid-session); wrong pinned fingerprint aborts enrolment; code expiry and attempt limit; forbidden flag, unknown setting, out-of-range value, path traversal, oversize frame or file, wrong sha256, replayed or expired offer, job to a device without the grant; cancel kills the process tree; with the farm disabled no socket is opened and `serve`/`worker` exit non-zero.

*Windows build (2026-11-25, done with the `powershell` Remote Control session; native Visual Studio route, nothing run on the GPU yet):* three rounds from a source tarball over the SMB share (`D:\\FileTransfers\\emfdtd`, sha256 checked each time; Visual Studio 2026 MSVC 14.52, CMake 4.3.1 with `-DCMAKE_POLICY_VERSION_MINIMUM=3.5`, Ninja, Vulkan SDK 1.4.363, `FDTD_SCREEN_READER=OFF` so AccessKit is not fetched). Fixes needed: an MSVC branch in `CMakeLists.txt` (GCC flags `-Wall -Wextra` broke `cl`; now `/W3 /utf-8 /permissive-`, `_USE_MATH_DEFINES NOMINMAX _CRT_SECURE_NO_WARNINGS`), `unistd.h`/`getpid`/`__attribute__` guards, a missing `#include <complex>` in `src/app/probes.hpp`, and `%s` with a `std::filesystem::path` (wchar_t on Windows) in `main.cpp`. Result: 248 of 248 steps link (`emfdtd.exe`, `bench.exe`, `tests_cpu.exe`, `tests_gpu.exe`) and `tests_cpu "~[slow]"` passes (40403 assertions in 130 test cases). `[slow]`, `tests_gpu`, `bench` and every Vulkan call are not run. The box: RTX 4060 Ti 16 GB (driver 621.24) plus a Radeon Pro 555X that reports Vulkan **1.2.162** on Windows (not 1.1), so a version filter does not exclude it and workers select the GPU by UUID or vendor allow-list; implicit layers present (Epic EOS overlay, AMD switchable graphics, NVIDIA Optimus); the GPU also drives a display; any run needs a turn on the shared Windows GPU lock and the user's approval; the machine had blue screens on 2026-11-17 so jobs must tolerate the worker disappearing. macOS note: the terminal needed the Network Volumes permission before this session could write to the share.

*First GPU runs on the Windows eGPU (2026-11-25, turn held on `win_gpu_lock.sh`, driven by the `powershell` session; approved by the user: `--list-devices`, `tests_gpu "[device]"`, one small job).* (1) The first `emfdtd.exe` call crashed (0xC0000409): `setvbuf(stdout, nullptr, _IOLBF, 0)` in `main` aborts the MSVC runtime (zero buffer size); fixed (`_IONBF` on Windows). (2) `--list-devices`: index 0 NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4060 Ti, uuid 99cbe8b64e12b2f55c408e988415f423, 15.7 GiB, Vulkan 1.4, driver 621.24; index 1 Radeon Pro 555X, Vulkan 1.2, AMD 20.45.40.15, uuid 00000000010000000000000000000000 (nearly all zero: do not use it as an identity, the vendor id is the safe key there). (3) `tests_gpu "[device]"` passes (9 assertions). (4) **Correctness:** the generated 2.4 GHz patch (`out/farm/job1/patch.json`, coarse, decay 0.01, 12 448 steps) on the RTX through `--gpu --device 99cbe8b6` gives best match -6.043 dB at 2.2195 GHz, the same as the Mac CPU backend (same step count), and the full 101-point S11 differs by at most 4.1e-8 (complex, at 2.856 GHz) from the Mac CPU reference: the Windows GPU and the CPU reference agree to float precision. (5) **Speed, a real finding:** 156.8 s for 12 448 steps = 24 Mcells/s, about 50 times slower than the Mac GPU (about 1200 Mcells/s on the antenna runs) and 17 times the 9 s estimate. Cause found in the code, not yet measured: `src/vkcore/buffer.cpp:17` allocates every buffer `HOST_VISIBLE | HOST_COHERENT` only. On Apple unified memory that is the fast path; on a discrete GPU it places the field arrays in system memory reached over PCIe (here Thunderbolt, x4 link, measured gen 1 when idle), so every kernel step streams the whole state across the link. The submit size is not the problem (the solver batches 64 steps per submit here: `gpu_solver.cpp:35-37`, 195 submits at about 0.8 s each). The fix is the usual one: `DEVICE_LOCAL` buffers for the field and coefficient arrays with a staging copy for the few host reads (probes, port log, DFT readback), chosen by memory-heap type at run time (unified-memory GPUs keep today's path). Until that is done the farm must not assume a discrete GPU is faster than the Mac GPU, and the worker's measured Mcells/s (placement input) will say so. The same limit applies to a Linux NVIDIA worker. Status: found, not fixed; needs the user's go-ahead as a solver change (the GPU tests and `check.sh --gpu` on both Apple drivers must still pass).

*Mac side of phase 1 (2026-11-25, under the GPU lock):* `tests_gpu "[device]"` passes (9 assertions) and `emfdtd --list-devices` prints two entries for the one Apple M1 Pro: index 0 through MoltenVK 1.4.2 (uuid 0000106b1b0002070000000000000000, 16.0 GiB, Vulkan 1.2) and index 1 through KosmicKrisp (uuid 190a0000010000000000000000000000, 11.8 GiB, Vulkan 1.4). The loader exposes the same physical GPU once per driver, and the two UUIDs differ, so the UUID cannot tell that they are one GPU. A worker must therefore never count them as two devices (it would run two jobs at once on one GPU): the local worker takes one slot per machine-wide GPU lock (as planned), and a device list should merge entries with the same vendor id, device name and device-local memory family or let the user pick one driver; decision pending, noted for phase 3.

*Staged device-resident buffers, result on the Windows eGPU (2026-11-25).* The fix for the finding above (large simulation buffers device-resident with a host staging copy on discrete GPUs: `Residency::Device` in `src/vkcore/buffer.*`, `Context::unifiedMemory()` and `copyBuffer`, used by `GpuSolver` for fields, material tables, CPML state, port buffer and phasor accumulators; unified-memory GPUs keep the old path; env `EMFDTD_STAGED_BUFFERS=1` or the context option forces staging for tests). Mac tests: `[staged]` (19 assertions: staged and unified paths give identical fields, port voltage and phasors with CPML, a pulsed port and a phasor list; and a staged buffer round trip) and the full `tests_gpu` (230 506 assertions in 17 test cases) pass. Windows RTX 4060 Ti (build 5, `[staged]` 19 assertions and `[device]` 9 assertions pass): the same patch job went from **156.8 s (24 Mcells/s) to 3.2 s (1185 Mcells/s), 49 times faster**, with an S11 file byte-identical to the first Windows run (sha256 89e30aa1...) and so equal to the Mac CPU reference to 4e-8; 1185 Mcells/s equals the Mac GPU's (about 1200 Mcells/s) on a 0.3 M cell grid. Default device rule changed to "newest Vulkan driver" (`defaultDevice`: discrete first, then newest API, then most memory; on this Mac KosmicKrisp 1.4 over MoltenVK 1.2): all antenna results before 2026-11-25 evening used MoltenVK; `nd_kk_01` gives the driver-to-driver difference. Not changed and still host-visible (slow on a discrete GPU, small effect for the farm jobs): the viewer buffers (`viz_vk`), the 2-D line solver (`gpu_line2d`).

*Farm phase 2 built (2026-11-25): pairing, certificates, policy, job validation; stdlib only, Python 3.9, 86 plugin tests pass, ruff clean.* `farm/config.py` (off-by-default switches, machine-wide folder), `farm/protocol.py` (length-prefixed JSON frames, message schema; enrolment messages added), `farm/pki.py` (private EC P-256 CA made with the system `openssl` on the coordinator, device certificates `role:name`, 90 days, fingerprints, revocation list, TLS contexts), `farm/enroll.py` (one active invitation: 12-character code from a 32-symbol alphabet, 60 bits, 10 minutes, 2 to 5 attempts, single use; the device pins the coordinator's fingerprint typed from its screen, then proves the code with an HMAC over both nonces and that fingerprint, so the code never crosses the network; wrong codes are serialised under a lock and delayed; the endpoint listens only while an invitation is active and closes when it is used up), `farm/policy.py` (per-device grants and limits, deny by default, checked per request; unknown actions refused), `farm/jobs.py` (a job is data: allow-listed settings by tier read from the worker's own `--list-settings`, `backend` denied, step cap, finite numbers only, no values starting with a dash, net names with no comma or control character, `--gpu`, `--device` and `--project-dir` set by the worker, sweep splitting including `geom.NET.x`). **Finding:** the system Python 3.9 on this Mac is linked to LibreSSL 2.8.3 and has no TLS 1.3, and KiCAD's bundled interpreter may differ, so `pki.min_tls()` requires TLS 1.3 where available and otherwise TLS 1.2 (still mutual, default ciphers); both paths were run (system Python: TLS 1.2 in the test suite; Homebrew Python 3.12 with OpenSSL 3.5: TLS 1.3, `out/farm/tls13_check.py`). Security scenarios covered: certificate from another farm refused; no client certificate refused; revoked certificate gives no identity; CA never overwritten; bad device names refused; wrong code costs an attempt and kills the invitation at zero; wrong fingerprint aborts before the proof; single use; expired invitation; endpoint closed after use; forbidden settings, unknown fields (`project_dir`, `argv`), dash and comma tricks, bad device selectors. Not yet built (phase 3): coordinator and worker processes, scheduler, transfers, process control, audit log, `emfdtd-farm` CLI.

*Farm phase 3 built (2026-11-25): coordinator, worker, scheduler, transfers, client, command line, docs for other users.* `farm/scheduler.py` (pure logic: fair share by jobs started per owner, fastest idle device, leases renewed by heartbeats, requeue up to 2 attempts, stale results refused, per-owner queue limit, a sweep pinned to one device class, retention; 16 scenarios), `farm/coordinator.py` (one mutual-TLS listener; per-request policy and revocation checks, also mid-session; audit log; offers pushed down the worker's connection; bulk data on its own connections with sizes capped before reading, hashes checked, names allow-listed, transfers bound to a job and an identity), `farm/worker.py` (dials out only; reconnects with backoff 1 to 30 s; offers only the chosen GPUs, one entry per physical GPU; builds argv from the validated job and the solver's own `--list-settings`, dry-runs it first, checks the GPU is still present, runs in a private folder with a time limit, a scrubbed environment and a killable process group, uploads s11.s1p, summary.txt, settings.json and the log, measures speed per device), `farm/client.py`, `farm/procs.py`, `farm/store.py`, `farm/audit.py`, `farm/cli.py` (`enable|disable|status|init|invite|enroll|serve|worker|devices|members|revoke|submit|queue|cancel|results`), `plugin/tools/emfdtd_farm.py`, `docs/farm.md` (set-up, security, limits, troubleshooting: written for people other than me; clients are separate identities, jobs and results private per person). The farm needs TLS 1.3 and refuses to enable otherwise. Tests (a stub solver, `plugin/tests/farm_stub/stub_solver.py`): 12 end-to-end scenarios over loopback (round trip with results; two devices in parallel; forbidden setting refused; solver rejection and crash fail without retry; time limit kills the solver; losing a worker mid-job requeues it to another worker; cancel stops the process; another user cannot see, cancel or fetch a job; a worker cannot submit and a client cannot act as a worker; a revoked worker is dropped and gets no more jobs; oversized input refused) and one command-line scenario with three machines (enable, init, invite, enroll for worker and client, serve, worker, a two-point sweep with `--wait`, members, revoke); 95 farm tests pass under Python 3.12 (TLS 1.3), **Correction (2026-11-25, user): no shims.** The first version skipped the TLS scenarios under the venv's TLS-less Xcode Python and ran them under another Python with the venv's packages through `scripts/check.sh`; both were removed: TLS 1.3 is required for everyone, the farm refuses to run without it, and the tests run in the plugin's own interpreter or fail. `plugin/.venv` was rebuilt (2026-11-25) on Homebrew `python@3.12` (OpenSSL 3.5.9, TLS 1.3) with `uv pip install` of kicad-python, pytest, pytest-bdd and ruff from PyPI, replacing the Xcode Python 3.9.6 venv (LibreSSL 2.8.3, kept in `out/old-venvs/`); all 121 plugin tests pass there with no skips. KiCad's bundled Python is 3.9.13 with OpenSSL 1.1.1n (TLS 1.3 too). Running the tests on 3.12 no longer exercises 3.9 itself, which found one 3.9 hazard that was fixed: asyncio locks and events created in `__init__` outside the running loop (Python 3.9 binds them to the wrong loop), now created lazily inside the loop. Not done: persistence of the queue across coordinator restarts, a memory-fit check at placement, run-as-service installers, the Farm screen in the window (UX not yet approved), the run on the real Windows worker through the farm (the Windows build exists; a farm job through the stub is verified, a real job through the farm is not), and a Windows job object (taskkill is used).

*Farm review fixes (2026-11-25, from the Windows-side review before enabling the worker there).* (1) **GPU turn that cannot be skipped:** `emfdtd --dry-run` still creates a Vulkan device (the context is made before the dry-run exit in `main.cpp`; it needs the device memory for the estimate), so a dry run, the device listing and the solve all take the turn; the worker refuses to start unless given exactly one of `--lock-command` (wraps every Vulkan call), `--turn-from-coordinator` (the coordinator holds the turn per job and the offer must carry `turn: true`; the worker offers devices recorded earlier during a turn with `devices --save`) or an explicit `--no-lock`. (2) **No Vulkan outside a turn:** the heartbeat no longer enumerates devices; device health is what the pre-job check last found. (3) **Worker ceilings:** `clamp_limits`: the coordinator can only lower the worker's own caps (time, steps, input size, result size, `max_tier`); the worker also refuses to upload a result over its own cap. (4) **Windows file permissions:** `config.restrict` removes ACL inheritance and grants only the current user (`icacls`) on the farm folder, the CA directory and the identity files (POSIX modes do not restrict Windows ACLs). (5) **How a Windows worker takes the turn that lives on the Mac:** the coordinator holds it (`gates.json`: a `hold` command that prints `HOLDING` and keeps the turn, and a `release` command; for the eGPU these are the existing `win_gpu_lock.sh hold|release`, not modified), offers only while holding, releases when the job ends, fails, is cancelled or the worker is lost, and requeues without counting an attempt if the turn never comes. A lock on the Windows box itself (owned by pg-strom's script) remains a separate user decision and is not needed for this. **Second Windows review (same day): a solver must not outlive its turn.** In coordinator-held mode the coordinator used to release the turn as soon as it stopped treating the job as running, but on a partition or a sleeping Mac the Windows solver keeps using the GPU until the worker notices, while the lock says free. Fixed: (a) the coordinator pings every worker (new `ping` message) and the worker has a watchdog (`--watchdog`, default 20 s, reported in its hello): after that much silence it kills the solver tree, refuses to start a dry run or solve, and drops the connection; (b) the coordinator releases a turn only when the worker confirmed the solver is dead (a `result`, a `fail`, or the new `stopped` message after a cancel) or when the worker's watchdog plus a 10 s margin has passed since the job ended on its side (`_release_ended`, also run from the expiry loop); (c) a hold command that ends mid-job cancels and requeues the job; (d) `gates.json` takes `max_minutes`, which lowers the job's limit and the hold time for that worker; (e) shutdown no longer waits on half-open connections. **The board job found a second flaw: the dry run (2026-11-25).** The first board job (`nd_med_01` on the RTX through the farm) never reached the solve: the worker's pre-solve `--dry-run` (fixed 120 s timeout) was killed after 121 s, because `--dry-run` does the whole model preparation (voxelising 9.46 M cells; 50 s of one core on the M1 Pro, over 110 s and still running on the Windows box) before it makes the Vulkan device, and the solve then repeats it. The timeout also reported an empty reason (a `TimeoutError` has no text), was retried, and re-took the turn. Fixes: **the dry run is gone** (the worker validates the job itself and the solver validates its own command line again at the start of the real run, exiting non-zero with its own message, which fails the job without a retry); one Vulkan call less per job; the worker reports each phase to the coordinator (fetching the input, checking the GPU, starting the solver) so `running` is never silent; worker errors name their exception type; the turn label gets the lease (`{lease}` in `gates.json`) so a retry never reuses an earlier attempt's label. The cancel-before-solve test now cancels a worker that is waiting for its lock command (a busy GPU), which is the same code path: the waiting command dies within 3 s and the solve never starts. The cancelled job, its re-offer and its turn were cleaned up (status free). **First real farm job (2026-11-25) and a bug it found.** Coordinator on the Mac (the coordinator's LAN address:7420, `gates.json` for `win-egpu`, 10 minute cap), worker `win-egpu` enrolled on the Windows box with `--turn-from-coordinator` (devices recorded under a held turn), client `mac-main` enrolled on the Mac. The generated patch job went submit, turn held (audit `turn_held`), offer, solve on the RTX 4060 Ti, result back, turn released in 4 s; 12 448 steps in 3.2 s (1191 Mcells/s), best match -6.043 dB at 2.2195 GHz, and all 101 S11 points differ by at most 4.1e-8 from the Mac CPU reference. The bug: the coordinator killed the hold command's whole process group before running the release command, so `win_gpu_lock.sh` could not run its cleanup trap and left a stale `/tmp/win_egpu.lock` (owner dead; the next taker takes it over, but `status` showed it held). Fixed: release first, then wait for the hold command to exit by itself (`release_wait_s`, default 20), kill only after that; the test stub now records its own exit and the scenario asserts hold, release, hold-exit in that order. **Third Windows review: early cancel and orphan children.** A cancel used to kill only a running solve, so one arriving during the input fetch, the device check or the dry run was ignored and the solve started outside the turn. Fixed: the worker records the cancelled job, cancels its task, checks it again right before the dry run and before the solve (`JobCancelled`), sends `stopped` only after `_reap` has killed and waited for every process of that job, and runs the device listing and the dry run as registered children (`_child`) that are killed on any exit (cancel, watchdog, timeout) and by `_kill_everything`; `procs.start` also kills a child whose creation was cancelled before the caller got its handle. `--watchdog` is now a real worker option. Tests: a cancel during an 8 s dry run kills the dry-run process within 3 s, the solve never starts, and the coordinator gets the turn back within 5 s through `stopped` (not after the 30 s grace); disabling the cancel handling makes it fail (mutation checked). Test: a relay that goes silent without closing (a one-way-until-timeout partition): the solver is killed within the watchdog, and the gate log shows the release only after the solver was dead; with the watchdog disabled the same test fails (checked by mutation). Tests: 133 plugin tests pass (new: turn modes, lock command wraps device check, dry run and solve but not the settings listing, heartbeat makes no GPU call, coordinator-held turn held before and released after, released on cancel, an offer without a turn refused, limits clamped, Windows `icacls` command, CLI refusal without a turn mechanism).

*Lock-command workers connect without the GPU (2026-11-25, for the Mac as a second worker).* A worker in `--lock-command` mode used to list its devices at connect time through the lock (`_capture`, 60 s timeout), so while a 40 minute antenna run held the shared Mac lock every hello timed out and the worker looped on backoff. Now there is one path for every turn mode: `devices_for_hello` reads the devices recorded once with `devices --save` (under a free lock, a held turn or `--no-lock`; `devices` takes `--work` for a non-default job folder) and the worker makes no Vulkan call to connect; the live check that the GPU is still there stays before each job, inside the turn (a disappeared device is `DeviceGone`, retried as before). Without a recorded list the worker (and `worker` on the command line) stops with the command to run. Tests: the lock-command scenario now asserts the lock log is empty while connecting and shows exactly one device check and the solve per job; a worker whose lock command always waits 8 s connects within 3 s and then completes a job; a worker with no recorded device list names `emfdtd-farm devices ... --save`. The Mac is still not enrolled as a worker (that needs the antenna queue to empty, then `devices --save --lock-command with_gpu_lock.sh nice -n 10`, then the enrolment).

*Farm agent lifecycle built (2026-11-25).* `plugin/src/emfdtd_plugin/farm/agent.py` and `autostart.py`; commands `agent configure|start|stop|restart|status|logs|doctor|autostart` and `setup` (docs/farm.md, "Running the worker as a managed agent"). The worker takes all its settings from `agent.json` (`worker --from-agent`), handles SIGTERM by ending its solver, and writes `agent.status.json` every heartbeat (pid, connected, job, phase, percent, solver pids), which is how `status` recognises its own process (a recycled pid is never signalled) and how `stop` finds a solver left behind by a killed worker. `start` runs the doctor first and names the first failing check with its fix; the `certificate` check separates "coordinator not reachable" from "certificate rejected". Autostart is explicit and off: launchd plist (RunAtLoad, KeepAlive false so a stop stays a stop), systemd user unit, Windows scheduled task, all running the same `agent start`; every external command goes through one helper that tests replace, and no test installs anything. The JSON shapes are fixed for the agent window. A bug found by the tests: an agent started and stopped inside one process (the window's controller, the tests) left a zombie that `kill(pid, 0)` called alive, so `stop` never saw it end; started agents are now tracked and reaped. 46 new scenarios (configure validation and privacy, doctor problems, start/stop/restart against a live coordinator, stale and recycled pids, stopping kills the solver, a frozen agent is killed, setup by flags and by typed answers, autostart text and commands for macOS, Linux and Windows).

*Open:* per-device local locks (only for a multi-GPU host; keep the machine-wide mutex for now); whether another Apple Silicon Mac exists; the Windows build route; the cross-device tolerance (to be measured, not assumed).

## Multi-port S-parameters (planned 2026-11-23; requested by the user, for later; nothing built)

*Why:* every result so far is a one-port S11. The questions a radio board raises are mostly two-port or more: isolation between two antennas (Wi-Fi and Bluetooth on one board, diversity or MIMO pairs), coupling from an antenna into a neighbouring trace or connector, the insertion loss of a matching network or a filter between two pads, a feed line measured as a through (S21 of a known line is a control that needs no antenna), and a chip's RF pin to its antenna pad. *What exists:* the snapshot already carries a list of ports (`ports`, each with its own reference impedance, layers, width) and the solver already has lumped ports with strips and source resistance, but `sim::runOnePort` drives the first one and returns `fdtd::SParam1` (S11 only, plus the aggregated port voltage); the Touchstone writer and `scripts/antenna/touchstone.py` handle one-port files.

*Method (to be written down with a cited source before any code, as the project rule says):* drive each port in turn with the band pulse while the others are terminated in their reference impedance (a lumped port with its source off is already a resistor), record the voltage and current at every port, and form S_ij = b_i / a_j from the incident and reflected waves at each port's reference plane, one column of the matrix per excitation. N ports cost N runs of the same grid (the same model and mesh, sources switched), so a 2-port is twice the time of today's run and a sweep over geometry multiplies it again; the GPU runs go through the shared lock as always. Shortcuts (assume reciprocity and skip half the runs) are an option the user turns on, never the default. No paper is needed: the extraction follows from the existing port model (a source in series with R) and the power-wave definition, and is derived in `docs/methods/multiport-sparameters.md` (an in-house methods note: S_ij = 2 V_i sqrt(R_j) / (sqrt(R_i) Vs_j) from the voltage across each port alone, reference-plane shift, checks and a validation plan). It is stated as derived, not as cited; the scenarios below are what test it.

*Pieces to build, in order (each scenario first, with the tolerance in the THEN):* (1) a CPU reference for N driven ports on the existing grid, `RunResult` carrying an S matrix per frequency (`SParamN`), S11 unchanged for one port; (2) Touchstone `.sNp` writing and reading (the Python helper reads `.s1p` only), with the port order and reference impedances stated in the header; (3) the GPU path, columns run one after another on the same engine (no new kernel is expected: the port update is already per port); (4) command line (`--ports A,B` to choose which ports are in the model and `--drive all|NAME` to choose the columns; a dry run lists the ports and the run count); (5) the window: a port list in the Preview (which ports are modelled, each one's impedance, already settings-driven), an S-parameter selector on the S11 plot tab (Sij in dB, phase, a Smith chart per Sii, isolation as a number at the band centre), the run estimate multiplied by the column count, every widget through the accessibility helpers, and the plugin flow unchanged until the user has approved any plugin UI; (6) the net selection (`--nets`, `--ground-nets`) per port, so two ports on different nets keep their own reference ground.

*Checks that must pass before a multi-port number is believed (known loads, in the spirit of `fdtd-control-check`):* reciprocity, |S12 - S21| small for a passive reciprocal board (and a failure is a finding: it points at an unequal port or a mesh asymmetry); passivity, the largest singular value of S at most 1 and the power sum |S_1j|^2 + |S_2j|^2 at most 1 for each driven port (lossless boards sum to 1); a through line of known length and impedance (S21 magnitude near 1 for a lossless line, phase slope 360 times the electrical length per wavelength, which the existing `phase_fit.py` slope check already measures for one port; S11 of a matched through small); a matched load and an open or short at the second port, which must give the one-port S11 the cascade of the first port's line and that termination predicts (`scripts/antenna/cascade.py`); two ports far apart on an unconnected pair of lines, S21 below about -60 dB and falling with distance. Reference-plane shift (de-embedding the port-to-pad line) is the one thing that cannot be skipped: with two ports the phase of S21 depends on both port positions, so the pad plane has to be named, not guessed; the wide-port finding (strips seeing different voltages) applies to each port.

*Open decisions for the user:* whether the plugin or only the standalone window offers it first; the default port set when a board has several (all of them, or the one the Antenna tab's part uses); whether isolation between two antenna parts should get its own row in the Antenna tab (two chosen parts, one number, no solve until the user asks); and how much run time N columns may cost before the preview must say so in the warning colour. Not started until the single-port antenna result in "Diagnostic (1) of the IFA solve" and the controls after it are understood, because a multi-port model inherits every port fault the single-port one has.

**Policy for methods whose papers cannot be obtained (2026-11-23, from the user).** The user may not be able to get paywalled papers. When a method is needed and its source is out of reach: (1) derive it from first principles or textbook identities and write it up as an in-house methods note under `docs/methods/` that says so on its first line, with the derivation checkable by hand and a validation plan; (2) where the quantity can be measured with this project's own solvers (an excess capacitance or inductance of a step, a bend or a stub from FDTD or the 2D solve, a de-embedding length from the field solve), produce our own table or fit with the runs recorded and the settings named, instead of quoting a paper's formula; (3) cite only what has been read in full and whose DOI was checked against Crossref, and list the unread ones as unread (as now); (4) never present an in-house result as a literature result. First note written: `docs/methods/multiport-sparameters.md`. A second candidate, if closed-form discontinuity corrections are wanted: fit excess C and L of a step, a mitred bend and an open end from FDTD runs of known lines, against the cascade in `scripts/antenna/cascade.py`.

**Correction (2026-11-23): the "valid" IFA runs were not valid; the controlled run resonates.** The runs recorded above as `valid` (`run-20261123-093609`) and `valid2` (`run-20261123-094409`) used `c_full.json`, whose port is a horizontal 0.3 mm port from the GND pour at (186.8, 105.4) mm to ANT_FEED copper at (188.04, 105.4) mm, in the middle of a feed that runs on to R2: the 22 mm of line toward R2 (R2 set open) was an open stub in parallel with the antenna, the "port mid-line" failure the controls had already shown once. `c_full` was never run through the known-load controls (the passing controls, `w_open` and `w_short`, used a different, vertical port at (188.04, 105.0) after cutting the line), so calling it valid was a label, not a verified fact. Their S11 (-2.6 to -4.5 dB, about 11 + 12j ohm, no dip) is void, and so is the conclusion that the arm's mesh was not the cause. *The run that uses the controlled port:* `out/nrf52840-ifa/anti/a_open.json` = `c_full.json` with the two ANT_FEED shapes on the R2 side of the cut removed and the `w_open` port, i.e. the `w_open` geometry with the antenna restored; same flags as the controls (`--nets /ANT_FEED --ground-nets GND --part R2=open --set mesh_preset=coarse --set decay_threshold=0.01 --refine 186.4 104.2 189.2 108.2 0.06`); 114 608 steps, 740 s, run `run-20261123-100122`. Run notes: R2 open, C24 do-not-populate left open, C23 left out (no stray shunt); the feed and ground are joined by copper through the IFA's short leg (expected, and the port note says so). *Result:* a resonance. The reactance crosses zero at about 2.14 GHz (Z = 15.6 - 7.2j at 2.105 GHz, 16.0 + 9.2j at 2.171 GHz), best S11 -5.7 dB at 2.138 GHz; at 2.45 GHz Z is about 26 + 76j ohm (S11 about -2.6 dB); the resistance at resonance is 16 ohm, not 50. *Reading:* the antenna in the model resonates about 12 percent below 2.44 GHz and is badly matched at resonance (R 16 ohm). It does not reproduce TI's statement (reflection below -15 dB; tuned to 50 ohm on its 1 mm board with its ground plane), and nothing here says which of the model and the layout differs. *Free checks done:* the footprint's topology matches TI's Figure 1-1 (arm, notched left column, stub with chamfered feed, two grounded legs, feed between ground sections; legs and the right ground edge were read off the figure, so a percent-level comparison is not possible); no copper on In1, In2 or B.Cu lies under the arm (samples at x 178 to 202 mm, y 108.5 to 113.5 mm), ground returns about 2 to 7 mm beyond the arm's right end. *Next, in order:* (1) a halved-cell rerun of `a_open` (the arm is on the coarse grid) to see whether 2.14 GHz moves; (2) a variant on TI's stackup (1.0 mm FR-4, two layers, ground plane like TI's) since thicker dielectric under the arm lowers the resonance and TI says the PCB thickness and ground shape move it; (3) only then the matching question (R 16 ohm at resonance). Each is a model change, not a claim about the board.

**IFA antenna, mesh dependence (2026-11-23).** Same controlled model (`a_open.json`, flags as above) with the whole antenna (box 175 to 205 mm by 106 to 116 mm) at 0.2 mm cells besides the 0.06 mm feed box: 5.96 M cells, 114 896 steps, 1321 s, run `run-20261123-102016`. Series resonance at 2.226 GHz with R = 19.7 ohm (coarse grid: 2.134 GHz, 15.7 ohm), best S11 -7.2 dB at 2.236 GHz; at 2.448 GHz Z = 24.3 + 52.4j ohm, S11 -3.9 dB; best in the 2.4 to 2.4835 GHz band -4.5 dB. Refining the arm moved the resonance up 4.3 percent and the resistance up 4 ohm, so the coarse result was not converged and this one is not shown to be either: a 0.1 mm rerun (queued) says which way it settles. (My earlier "valid2" refine box stopped at x 192.5 and y 110.5 and missed most of the arm, which spans x 177.2 to 202.8 and y 107.6 to 114.5; this box covers it.) Tool: `match.py summary` now prints where the reactance crosses zero and the resistance there.

**IFA antenna on TI's board thickness (2026-11-23).** Same model and 0.2 mm antenna box as the previous entry with the middle dielectric set to 0.86 mm (`--set diel2_thickness_mm=0.86`: total 1.0 mm, the thickness SWRU120D's results use; the four-layer stack's inner layers are empty under the arm, so the total is what the arm sees): 5.05 M cells, 115 584 steps, 837 s, run `run-20261123-104217`. Series resonance 2.333 GHz with R = 22.2 ohm (1.44 mm board, same mesh: 2.226 GHz, 19.7 ohm), best S11 -8.3 dB at 2.342 GHz, in the 2.4 to 2.4835 GHz band between -5.8 and -7.5 dB, Z = 24.9 + 27.9j ohm at 2.448 GHz. Thinner dielectric raises the resonance by 4.8 percent, as TI says PCB thickness shifts it; it is still about 4 percent below 2.44 GHz with the resistance at 22 ohm, not 50, and the mesh is not converged (the 0.1 mm run on the 1.44 mm board is queued; it should be repeated on this thickness if the mesh moves the answer again). Everything here is the model's own answer, with TI's ground plane and PCB shape still not reproduced (our ground is the 80 x 37 mm four-layer pour), so it is not yet a comparison with TI's measurement.

**IFA antenna mesh convergence (2026-11-23).** The antenna box at 0.1 mm cells (11.2 M cells, 114 512 steps, 2545 s, run `run-20261123-105616`; 1.44 mm board, controlled port): series resonance 2.2355 GHz with R = 20.3 ohm, best S11 -7.5 dB at 2.244 GHz, Z = 24.3 + 50.8j ohm at 2.448 GHz, in-band best -4.6 dB. Sequence of the antenna region's cell: coarse preset (about 0.4 to 0.5 mm over the arm) 2.1336 GHz, R 15.7 ohm; 0.2 mm 2.2261 GHz, R 19.7 ohm; 0.1 mm 2.2355 GHz, R 20.3 ohm. So the 0.2 to 0.1 mm step moves the resonance 0.4 percent and the resistance 0.6 ohm: converged for practical purposes at 0.2 mm (six cells across the 1.21 mm arm), and **the default coarse mesh puts this antenna's resonance 4.5 percent low and its resistance 4.6 ohm low**. Consequences: (1) the earlier statement that the 1.0 mm board shifts the resonance by 4.8 percent stands (same 0.2 mm mesh: 2.226 to 2.333 GHz); (2) a chip or trace antenna on the default mesh is mis-tuned by about its own bandwidth, so a fine box over the antenna should be on by default: the model's refinement already adds boxes around ports and for narrow gaps (`--refine-gaps`), but not over a narrow copper feature such as an arm, so the recommendation is a suggestion in the Preview tab ("fine cells over the antenna part: arm 1.21 mm, 0.2 mm cells, N more cells") built like the gap suggestion, with the same switch; it is a UX addition and needs the user's go-ahead before it is built; (3) what the model says about TI's antenna on this board is now stable: it resonates near 2.24 GHz at 20 ohm on the 1.44 mm board and about 2.34 GHz at 22 ohm on a 1.0 mm board, not at 2.44 GHz and 50 ohm. The remaining differences from TI's measurement are the ground plane (TI's reference board shape and size are not in SWRU120D; ours is an 80 x 37 mm four-layer pour), the exact dimensions the footprint took from Figure 1-1 by eye (leg positions, the right ground edge), copper as zero-thickness sheets (the arm is 35 um thick, which makes it electrically a little wider), and the missing solder mask. Not run: a variant with thickness, a TI-like small ground, or the leg and ground-edge dimensions varied by a few tenths of a millimetre.

**Copper in the model, fab copper and stackup options, and the IFA variants (2026-11-23, asked by the user).** *Premise.* Copper is a **zero-thickness** PEC sheet in the 3D model (`src/board/voxelize.cpp` writes the note "copper is modelled as zero-thickness PEC sheets; dielectric thickness is used as given"; `docs/snapshot.md` says so). What was added this session (snapshot `thickness`, the exporter, `copper_outer_um` / `copper_inner_um`) reaches only the Antenna tab's feed-line field solve; the FDTD solve never sees it. *What thickness would do (sized with the 2D solver, `[.report]` in `feed_solve_test.cpp`, microstrip over 1.44 mm of er 4.5):* the 1.21 mm IFA arm line's effective permittivity falls from 3.117 (sheet) to 3.073 (35 um), impedance 74.8 to 73.5 ohm, which raises a quarter-wave resonance by about 0.7 percent (sqrt of the ratio); the 0.46 mm feed neck 3.003 to 2.932, 108.9 to 105.8 ohm, about 1.2 percent. That is above the mesh error found for this antenna (0.4 percent at 0.2 mm cells) but far below what separates the model from TI (the resonance 2.24 against 2.44 GHz, the resistance 20 against 50 ohm), so it is a correction, not the explanation. An equivalent-width trick does not reproduce it (widening a sheet raises, not lowers, the effective permittivity); real thickness needs a slab model in the voxelizer: z grid lines at the interface and interface plus t, PEC on every plane within the slab, via and port spans from copper surface to copper surface, a smaller time step when the cell is the copper thickness (a 17.5 um cell cuts dt by about a quarter against today's 23.2 um minimum), opt-in as `copper_model = sheet | slab` (Advanced, default sheet, so every earlier number stays reproducible); acceptance: a 50 ohm microstrip with 35 um copper within 2 percent of the 2D solve's thick-copper value and the sheet default identical to today. A staircase slab is plain Yee, not the conformal scheme the patent rule guards. *Not built; a decision for the user.*

*TI's figure, measured instead of read by eye.* From `out/antennas/ti_p2.png` (1401 x 2140 px), the black-pixel extents with the arm length L1 = 25.58 mm as scale (41.6 px/mm; the arm's thickness then reads 1.20 mm against TI's W1 = 1.21 mm, a check of the scale): leg A 6.85 to 8.17 mm from the arm's left end (footprint 6.83 to 8.20), leg B 8.99 to 10.17 mm (footprint 8.99 to 10.18), feed line 10.67 to 11.09 mm (10.63 to 11.09), right ground edge 12.02 mm (12.02). Agreement within about 0.03 mm, which is one pixel, so the "read off the figure" caveat on the footprint is closed and a leg-position variant would test nothing.

*Fabs (read 2026-11-23 by fetch agents; pages quoted, nothing assumed).* **OSH Park 4-layer** (`docs.oshpark.com/services/four-layer`): fixed stackup, 63 mil nominal: outer copper 1 oz "clad+plated" 1.7 mil (43.2 um), 7.87 mil FR408HR 2113 prepreg (Dk 3.61 at 1 GHz), inner copper 0.5 oz 0.68 mil (17.5 um), 39 mil FR408HR core (the page gives no core Dk; the profile uses the Isola datasheet's 3.69 at 1 GHz); the page's rows sum to 61.9 mil against 63 mil nominal; a changelog line (28-JAN-2026) mentions a temporary alternate stackup for some 4-layer orders that the page body does not describe; the page text has no controlled-impedance statement and links an "Impedance Constraint Table" image that was not read; all 4-layer and 6-layer services are 63 mil. 2-layer: 63 mil, 1.4 mil (35.6 um) copper, 60 mil core, Dk 4.5 at 10 MHz; a 0.8 mm, 2 oz two-layer service exists; no impedance control or copper-weight choice is described on the pages read. (An earlier search summary that called the OSH Park copper weights inconsistent was wrong: the table and the spec line agree.) **PCBWay** (`multi-layer-laminated-structure.html`, `capabilities.html`): 4-layer stackups are published as tables, outer copper given as base and finished ("Outer Base Copper 0.5 oz, 0.0175 mm, plating to 1 oz"; finished outer 1 to 5 oz in the stackup tool, 1 to 8 oz on the capabilities page, 35 um per oz), inner 1 to 4 oz (0.5 oz appears in the tool's list), inner layers not labelled base or finished; materials and Dk (7628 prepreg Dk 4.74, 2116 4.45, 3313 4.45, 1080 4.21, core 4.6, no frequency stated); a 1.0 mm four-layer design is listed (core 0.43 mm, 7628 prepreg 0.1785 mm after lamination for 50 percent residual copper, finished 0.99 mm +-0.1 mm; the 1.6 mm design 1 is `pcbway_4layer`); the page says "if you need custom stackup or control impedance, we will manufacture according to your requirement" and that after the order they calculate whether it meets the requirement from stackup, material and impedance data and confirm; the capabilities table gives an impedance tolerance of +-10 percent (50 ohm and below: +-5 ohm); whether they adjust trace widths is not stated; the 6 to 18 layer designs (from design 77 on) were not reached. Neither fab lists a copper "pour" option: the only copper-distribution input is PCBWay's inner-layer residual copper ratio (30 to 90 percent), a lamination choice that moves the prepreg thickness by up to about 0.014 mm. *Profile data:* `oshpark_4layer` outer copper corrected from 35 to 43.2 um with the dated source; `pcbway_4layer` already carried design 1's numbers exactly.

*Advice for an antenna board.* (1) Take the fab's published stackup into the project (profiles do this); expect the fab's thickness tolerance (PCBWay +-10 percent on the finished board, OSH Park +-10 percent on the core) to spread an IFA's resonance by roughly 1.5 percent, estimated by scaling this model's 4.8 percent shift for a 30 percent thickness change (1.44 to 1.0 mm), a linear guess, not a run. (2) Controlled impedance is a PCBWay service (custom stackup or impedance control in the order form, +-10 percent, +-5 ohm at 50 ohm and below); OSH Park's pages read do not offer it, so a feed that must be 50 ohm there has to be designed on the published stackup with a field solver and the tolerances above, and the unread Impedance Constraint Table should be read first. (3) Ask for 1 oz finished outer copper (35 um at PCBWay, 43 um at OSH Park: the wider the copper, the lower a coplanar line's impedance by about 6 to 9 percent, the feed row's finding). (4) The board thickness is a bigger lever for an IFA than copper: 1.0 mm raised this antenna's resonance 4.8 percent (variant below); PCBWay lists 1.0 mm four-layer; OSH Park's four-layer is 1.6 mm only. (5) Keep the inner layers empty under any printed antenna whatever the stackup.

*Variants queued (each on the converged 0.2 mm antenna mesh, controlled port, `a_open.json`; baseline 1.44 mm FR4-like board: 2.2261 GHz, R 19.7 ohm, run `run-20261123-102016`):* OSH Park 4-layer profile; PCBWay 4-layer profile; ground cropped to x >= 157 mm (ground 20 mm left of the arm) and x >= 167 mm (10 mm left), both labelled sensitivity, not "TI's board" (the CC2430DB outline is in TI's reference-design package, swrr034.zip, not in SWRU120D; one search did not find its size). Results follow below as they finish.

**IFA variants, results (2026-11-23).** All on the controlled model (`a_open.json`: `w_open` port and cut, antenna present) with the antenna at 0.2 mm cells (converged to 0.4 percent) and the same flags; baseline (the board's own 0.07 / 1.3 / 0.07 mm stackup, er 4.8 / 4.5 / 4.5, 80 mm of ground): series resonance 2.2261 GHz, R 19.7 ohm, S11 -7.2 dB at best, run `run-20261123-102016`. (Steps and time differ between rows because thicker dielectrics allow a larger time step; the antenna mesh is the same.)

| variant | series resonance | R at resonance | best S11 | Z at 2.448 GHz | in-band (2.4 to 2.4835 GHz) | run |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| baseline, 1.44 mm | 2.2261 GHz | 19.7 ohm | -7.2 dB | 24.3 + 52.4j | -4.5 to -3.5 dB | 102016 |
| 1.0 mm total dielectric (middle layer 0.86 mm) | 2.3331 GHz | 22.2 ohm | -8.3 dB | 24.9 + 27.9j | -7.5 to -5.8 dB | 104217 |
| OSH Park 4-layer profile (FR408HR, Dk 3.61 / 3.69, 0.200 / 0.991 / 0.200 mm) | 2.3342 GHz | 21.1 ohm | -7.8 dB | 22.9 + 28.0j | -6.9 to -5.4 dB | 115013 |
| PCBWay 4-layer profile (7628 prepreg Dk 4.74, core Dk 4.6, 0.1855 / 1.03 / 0.1855 mm) | 2.1903 GHz | 18.0 ohm | -6.6 dB | 24.2 + 62.1j | -3.6 to -2.9 dB | 115515 |
| ground cropped to x >= 157 mm (20 mm left of the arm) | 2.2649 GHz | 24.2 ohm | -9.2 dB | 23.9 + 39.9j | -5.9 to -4.2 dB | 120103 |
| ground cropped to x >= 167 mm (10 mm left of the arm) | 2.2692 GHz | 35.5 ohm | -15.5 dB | 33.8 + 35.8j | -8.9 to -6.3 dB | 122414 |

*Reading.* (1) **Dielectric constant and thickness set the resonance.** The OSH Park stack (Dk 3.6 to 3.7, 1.39 mm in all) resonates 4.9 percent above the baseline and the PCBWay stack (Dk 4.6 to 4.7, 1.40 mm) 1.6 percent below it, the same ordering as the permittivity, and a 1.0 mm board raises it 4.8 percent; a fab's stackup is therefore a first-order antenna parameter, not a detail. (2) **Ground length sets the resistance, not the resonance.** Shortening the ground behind the arm from 40 mm to 20 mm to 10 mm moves R from 19.7 to 24.2 to 35.5 ohm and the resonance by under 2 percent; at 10 mm the model reaches -15.5 dB with a 237 MHz band below -10 dB (2.138 to 2.375 GHz), the shape TI describes (reflection below -15 dB, more than 300 MHz) but 7 percent low in frequency. TI says the match depends on the ground; this is that statement measured. (3) **The model is now qualitatively where TI's antenna is** (a resonance with a few-tens-of-ohm resistance that moves with ground and board), and still quantitatively off: nothing yet combines a short ground with TI's thickness. The effects roughly add: the short ground and the 1.0 mm board together would put the resonance near 2.37 GHz, and thick copper (+0.7 percent, sized above) near 2.39 GHz; a run of the first pair is queued. *Caveats:* the ground crops are sensitivity variants, not TI's board (its outline is in swrr034.zip, not in SWRU120D); one run each, one mesh; copper is still a sheet; the stackup rows follow each fab's published numbers, with the loss tangents the profiles mark as assumed.

**IFA variant that combines the two levers (2026-11-23).** Ground cropped to x >= 167 mm (10 mm left of the arm) on TI's 1.0 mm board thickness (`--set diel2_thickness_mm=0.86`), otherwise the converged setup above: 5.8 M cells, 115 600 steps, 855 s, run `run-20261123-124812`. Series resonance 2.396 GHz with R = 37.0 ohm; best S11 -16.5 dB at 2.391 GHz; -10 dB band 2.260 to 2.522 GHz (261 MHz); in the 2.4 to 2.4835 GHz band between -16.3 and -12.4 dB; at 2.44 GHz S11 -14.9 dB and Z = 36.9 + 8.7j ohm. SWRU120D states reflection below -15 dB and more than 300 MHz below 10 percent reflected power (S11 < -10 dB) on TI's reference board: this model, with a short ground and TI's board thickness, is at -14.9 dB and 261 MHz, 2.4 percent below 2.44 GHz at resonance. *How far to trust that agreement:* the two levers were chosen after the table above showed which way each one moves the result and after knowing TI's target, so this is a fitted agreement of a plausible configuration, not an independent validation; the ground length (10 mm behind the arm) is a guess at TI's board, whose outline is not in the app note; copper is still a sheet (sized at +0.7 percent in frequency, which would move the resonance to about 2.41 GHz); one run, one mesh. What it does show: the solver, the controlled port and the mesh produce a TI-like match (tens of ohms at resonance, a 250 to 300 MHz band) for a configuration inside the range of boards TI's antenna could have been measured on, which makes "the model is wrong" a less likely explanation of the earlier 20 ohm result than "the board's ground and stackup differ from TI's". A test that would not be fitted: obtain the CC2430DB outline (swrr034.zip) and run it unchanged. **Consequence for the product:** the Antenna tab's static ground check (ground reach against the vendor's test board, `antenna_testboard_*`) is the right instrument for this effect: here a 40 mm to 10 mm change of ground behind the arm moved R from 19.7 to 35.5 ohm.

**Copper slab model: BUILT (2026-11-23, requested by the user; opt-in).** *Setting:* `copper_model` (PCB group, Advanced): `sheet` (default; every earlier result is reproduced exactly: the fast suite, 127 cases, is unchanged) or `slab`. *What slab does* (`src/board/voxelize.cpp`, `buildZ`): a copper layer with a thickness (the snapshot's, else the profile's, else `copper_outer_um` / `copper_inner_um`; a layer without one stays a sheet and a note says so) fills z cells of its own thickness: the outer top layer above its dielectric interface, the outer bottom layer below it, an inner layer in the first cell of the dielectric over it (so no dielectric thickness changes; that cell is at most half the dielectric's). The grid gets a line at each slab face and the air above and below the board grows from the slab's cell size. Tangential E is PEC on every plane from a slab's lower to its upper face inside the copper footprint, and Ez is PEC along z between them at the nodes inside the footprint; vias and vertical ports still run between the dielectric interfaces and meet the slab's face. The run notes say which model is in use and with which thicknesses. *Scenarios (written red first, `board_test.cpp`, `settings_test.cpp`):* with `sheet` the thickness changes nothing (same grid, same PEC edges, same note); with `slab` the top copper fills one cell of 35 um above its interface, the ground one below it, the pad's Ex and Ey are PEC in both planes and Ez between them (zero for a sheet), a layer without a thickness stays a sheet with a note; the setting defaults to sheet, refuses a typo, reaches the plan, is found by searching "slab", and a prepared model of a board with thickness carries the slab note. *Physics acceptance (differential, because the open-ended-line fit has port and end errors that cancel between two runs on the same mesh; `validate_lines.py --thickness-um 200 --copper-model sheet|slab`):* a 1.0 mm line on 1.6 mm of er 4.4 with 0.2 mm copper (one cell thick at this mesh; the 2D solver of `line2d` with the same section predicts Z0 x 0.9284, eps_eff x 0.9440, k x 0.9716 against the sheet): FDTD at 0.1 mm lateral cells, sheet fit Z0 86.9 ohm, slab fit 82.1 ohm (x 0.9448), propagation constant x 0.9775, i.e. the right sign and 77 percent (Z0) and 79 percent (k) of the predicted size; runs `run-20261123-133103` (sheet) and `run-20261123-140014` (slab). The shortfall is probably the lateral mesh under-resolving the strip's side wall; a pair at 0.05 mm cells is queued and decides whether it closes. *On the antenna:* the controlled IFA model (`a_open.json`, 0.2 mm antenna mesh, thicknesses 35.56 um outer and 36 um inner as exported, 5.96 M cells, smallest cell 0.0175 mm, time step 0.0503 ps against 0.0626 ps, 143 024 steps, 1633 s, run `run-20261123-133257`): series resonance 2.2377 GHz, R 19.6 ohm, against the sheet model's 2.2261 GHz and 19.7 ohm: **+0.52 percent in frequency, no change in resistance**, inside the +0.7 percent (arm) to +1.2 percent (neck) the 2D solver predicted and consistent with the slab model delivering about three quarters of a predicted effect at this mesh. *Costs and limits:* the slab cells (the inner layers' half-cells down to 17.5 um) shrink the time step by about 20 percent here and add air cells above and below; the staircase slab is plain Yee, not the conformal subcell scheme the patent rule guards; the cell next to a slab can be several times larger than the slab (the smooth-mesh rule is not enforced inside the dielectric); the solder mask is still not modelled (OSH Park's own prediction of 49.8 ohm for its 15 mil line includes it: closed form 54.7 ohm, 2D solve with 45.7 um copper 51.0 ohm, so the mask is about 1.2 ohm of that line). Thickness is therefore a correction of about half a percent in an antenna's frequency, now measurable instead of assumed.

**OSH Park's two unread images, read (2026-11-23; saved as `out/antennas/oshpark_four_layer_stackup.png` and `oshpark_four_layer_impedance_table.png`).** *Stackup diagram* (its own figure, not the page text): soldermask 0.60 mil (Taiyo-SM purple, Dk 3.90, Df 0.033); Lyr1 "0.5 oz / Std" 1.80 mil; prepreg 7.87 mil (FR408HR 2x2113, 57 percent, Dk 3.61, Df 0.009); Lyr2 0.5 oz 0.60 mil; core 39.00 mil (FR408HR, **Dk 3.87**, Df 0.009); Lyr3 0.5 oz 0.60 mil; prepreg 7.86 mil (Dk 3.61); Lyr4 "0.5 oz / Std" 1.80 mil; soldermask 0.60 mil. Against the page table: outer copper 1.7 mil there against 1.80 mil here (43.2 against 45.7 um; both are "clad+plated"), inner 0.68 against 0.60 mil (17.3 against 15.2 um), and the core's Dk, which the page text does not give, is 3.87, not the 3.69 the profile took from the Isola datasheet. `oshpark_4layer` now carries 3.87 with the diagram as its source (and its test); the OSH Park variant run above used 3.69, so its 2.334 GHz would sit a little lower with 3.87, not rerun. *Impedance constraints* (OSH Park's calculator output for the four-layer stack): single-ended, Lyr1, design line 15.0 mil, reference layers 0 / 2 (the plane on layer 2), target 50.0 ohm, tolerance 5.0, **predicted 49.8 ohm**; differential 9.0 mil lines at 14.0 mil pitch, target 90 ohm, tolerance 9, predicted 91.5; differential 6.9 mil at 12.0 mil pitch, target 100 ohm, tolerance 10, predicted 100.3. So OSH Park publishes controlled geometries for its fixed stackup (as design data with a predicted value and a tolerance, not as an impedance-test service on the pages read). *A free cross-check of this project's line solvers on that 50 ohm line (15 mil over 7.87 mil, Dk 3.61):* closed form (zero-thickness) 54.7 ohm; 2D solve as a sheet 54.1 ohm; 2D solve with 1.8 mil (45.7 um) of copper 51.0 ohm; OSH Park 49.8 ohm. The thick-copper solve is within 2.4 percent and the 1.2 ohm left is about what the 0.6 mil solder mask (Dk 3.9) they include would remove, a layer this project does not model; the zero-thickness form is 10 percent high. That supports the feed row's thickness correction (6 to 9 percent) against an independent calculator.

**Copper slab model: correction and final validation (2026-11-23).** The first acceptance numbers in the entry above (the line at 77 and 79 percent of the predicted shift, the IFA at +0.52 percent) came from a model with a defect found while chasing the shortfall: with grid lines snapped onto a copper edge (the default), the even-odd ray test classified a node exactly on the edge by rounding, so a slab's left wall was claimed and its right wall was not (the pad of the scenario claimed columns 3.6, 4.53, 5.47 mm and not 6.4 mm). A sheet barely notices that missing edge node, a slab's side wall is the thing being modelled. Fixed for slab layers only (`insideShapeOrOn`: a node within 0.1 um of the shape's edge counts as inside, on every side; sheets keep the old test, so defaults are unchanged), with a scenario that requires the Ez and Ey columns at both edges. *Why the 2D prediction was trusted:* plain unextrapolated solves of the validation line converge to the same ratios as the extrapolated ones (Z0 x 0.9379, 0.9337, 0.9311, 0.9295 and k x 0.9743, 0.9730, 0.9723, 0.9719 at cells of 0.08, 0.04, 0.02, 0.01 mm), so the 2D shift (Z0 x 0.929, k x 0.972) is converged, and a finer lateral mesh did not move the first slab result (Z0 ratio 0.9448 at 0.1 mm, 0.9450 at 0.05 mm), which pointed at the model and not the mesh. *Acceptance, corrected slab* (1.0 mm line on 1.6 mm of er 4.4, 0.2 mm copper, FDTD open-line fit against the sheet run on the same mesh; runs `run-20261123-142205` and `142354`): at 0.1 mm lateral cells Z0 x 0.9137 and k x 0.9472 (overshoot, mesh-limited); **at 0.05 mm cells Z0 x 0.9298 against the 2D solver's 0.9284 (within 0.2 percent of the ratio) and k x 0.9633 against 0.9716**; the k ratio carries the open-end fit's own error (the end extension changes with a thick conductor), so the impedance ratio is the acceptance and the k ratio a consistency check. The slab is meant for a mesh that resolves its side wall (at least about two cells across the thickness laterally); the 0.1 mm run at 0.2 mm thickness is the coarse limit. *Corrected IFA result* (`a_open.json`, 0.2 mm antenna mesh, 35.56 um outer and 36 um inner copper, 143 024 steps, 1543 s, run `run-20261123-143241`): series resonance **2.2406 GHz, R 19.5 ohm**, against the sheet model's 2.2261 GHz and 19.7 ohm: **+0.65 percent in frequency, no change in resistance**, inside the +0.7 (arm) to +1.2 percent (neck) sized earlier. The superseded run `run-20261123-133257` (2.2377 GHz) is kept only as the record. `scripts/check.sh` passes (fast suite 128 cases, slow suite, formatting, ruff, pytest, scripts).

**TI's CC2430DB package read (2026-11-23; `out/antennas/swrr034c.zip` from `https://www.ti.com/lit/zip/SWRR034`, a link embedded in SWRU120D's reference [1]; private, git-ignored, used only to develop and check this project's model of TI's antenna; nothing from it is reproduced in the repository).** Contents (1.1 MB zip, extracted into its own folder and read as data): `readme_PCB.txt`, Gerbers `l1.spl` to `l4.spl`, solder mask, silk and paste, Excellon drill files, the PCB as CADSTAR files, schematic PDF, BOM and part lists, and `Inverted_F_Antenna.dxf` / `.spl`. *What it says that this plan had wrong:* (1) the board TI measured the antenna on is a **four-layer 1.112 mm board** (the app note says "1 mm FR-4"): layers 1-2 0.321 mm, 2-3 0.34 mm, 3-4 0.321 mm of FR4 with Dk 4.5 (no loss tangent given), copper "0.35um" per layer (a typo for 35 um; the model assumes 35 um), 8 mil minimum trace and space. The "1.0 mm, empty inner layers" model used for the thickness variants above was not TI's board. (2) The outline (the loop in `drill.spl`) is **48.06 x 98.17 mm** (1.6230 to 3.5150 inch by 1.3150 to 5.1800 inch), not the 80 x 37 mm board of the IFA test design, and the antenna sits along its top edge toward the left: the radiator polygon in `l1.spl` is 25.4 mm wide and 7.0 mm tall (x 44.17 to 69.57 mm, y 123.85 to 130.86 mm in the Gerber frame, TI's box is 25.58 x 7.5), the feed line leaves it near x = 55.0 mm and passes through a gap in the layer-1 ground. (3) **The ground under the antenna**: the layer-2 ground plane (positive copper) starts 32 mm below the board's top edge, so there is no inner plane under or near the antenna; the layer-4 (solder side) pour starts 7.5 mm below the top edge, so under the ground edge and the feed point the reference is layer 4, 1.112 mm away; layer 3 is a partial VCC plane. The "short ground behind the arm" guess (10 mm) that produced -14.9 dB was therefore not TI's configuration. *What was built:* a small Gerber rasteriser and a raster-to-polygon tracer (private, `out/antennas/swrr034c/tools/`, no external libraries) and a snapshot builder: outline from `drill.spl`; layers 1, 2 and 4 rasterised (0.05 mm, 0.1 mm, 0.1 mm), traced to polygons (shapes over 0.3 mm2 on layer 1, over 1 mm2 elsewhere, holes over 0.25 mm2: 299, 72 and 133 shapes, 38 700 vertices), copper as sheets (35 um recorded), no net names (Gerbers have none), layer 3 left out, and only the vias that land in the ground copper of both outer layers (58 of 337 on this board), because Gerbers cannot tell signal vias from ground vias. The antenna is in the model as TI drew it. The feed line is cut at y = 120.45 mm (the pads below it erased) and a vertical 0.3 mm port runs from the line at (55.27, 120.9) mm on layer 1 to layer 4, the style of the controlled IFA model; the layer-4 pour lies under the port and layer 2 has no copper there. *Controls on this port* (antenna erased, line open; and the same with a 0.4 mm tie from the line to the layer-1 ground, 0.5 mm above the port), then the antenna run, are queued (3.26 M cells, smallest cell 0.0565 mm, about 14 minutes each); results follow below. *Limits to keep in mind:* the model leaves out layer 3, the chip, the components and the balun (the port is at the antenna end, as TI's figure puts the 50 ohm feed point); signal-via barrels are dropped; the tracer simplifies copper to within 0.03 mm; loss tangent 0.02 is assumed.

**TI's CC2430DB antenna, modelled unchanged from TI's Gerbers: the independent test (2026-11-23).** Model: `out/antennas/swrr034c/ti_cc2430db.json` (built as described above; stackup from TI's readme, FR4 Dk 4.5 as stated, loss tangent 0.02 assumed, copper as sheets), flags `--profile bluetooth --set mesh_preset=coarse --set decay_threshold=0.01 --refine 53.5 119.0 56.5 124.0 0.06 --refine 43.5 121 71 132.5 0.2`, 3.26 M cells, smallest cell 0.0565 mm. *Controls on this port first (as `fdtd-control-check` requires):* antenna erased and line open, `run-20261123-155147`: phase at f = 0 0.8 degrees, reads as open; the same with a 0.4 mm tie to the layer-1 ground 0.5 mm above the port, `run-20261123-155546`: 162.1 degrees, reads as short (inside the 45 degree tolerance; the line between port and tie accounts for the rest); both reflect essentially everything (-0.06 and -0.04 dB). *Result with the antenna* (`run-20261123-160002`; nothing fitted to TI's figures): series resonance **2.2445 GHz with R = 55.5 ohm**, best S11 **-26.9 dB at 2.236 GHz** (Z = 54.2 - 2.2j ohm), S11 below -10 dB from 2.130 to 2.391 GHz (**261 MHz**, 11.7 percent of the centre) and below -15 dB from 2.179 to 2.301 GHz (122 MHz); at 2.44 GHz S11 -9.1 dB, Z = 95.8 + 24.5j ohm; a parallel resonance at 2.683 GHz. *Reading against SWRU120D* (tuned to 50 ohm on the reference board, reflection below -15 dB, more than 300 MHz below 10 percent reflected power): the resistance at resonance (55.5 against 50 ohm) and the bandwidth (261 against more than 300 MHz) are what TI's description implies; the **resonance is 8 percent low (2.24 against about 2.44 GHz)**, which is why the match at 2.44 GHz is only -9.1 dB.
