Uniform finer cells without refinement are not enough (40 cells per wavelength: 2.2986/2.2971/2.2948, shifts +2.26, +3.22, +3.47 %; 60: 2.3264/2.3125/2.3060, +3.26, +2.99, +3.29 %). The cavity with 0.25 mm cells over the whole cavity: f(4.5) = 2.8790, 2.8777, 2.8801 GHz for boxes 0.25, 0.5, 1.0 (0.08 % spread, 0.12 to 0.17 % below the exact 2.8826), shifts +3.53, +3.56, +3.48 % (exact +3.51). Slab copper (the robust edge test, 35 um) on the patch at the default cells, boxes 0.25 to 1.0: f(4.5) = 2.3093, 2.3061, 2.2962, 2.2960 GHz (a smooth 0.6 % drift instead of the 6 % jumps of sheet copper) and f(4.2) = 2.3766, 2.3904, 2.3761, 2.3769 GHz, shifts +2.92, +3.66, +3.48, +3.53 % (expected +3.38): the robust edge test removes the discrete jumps, and what is left (0.5 %) is the coarse mesh. *Answers:* (a) the domain-size dependence is mesh and rasterization, not the absorbing boundary: it appears on a closed cavity and goes to 0.1 % when the structure is resolved; (b) a Dk change shifts the resonance by the right fraction (to 0.1 %) once the copper edges and the dielectric are resolved, and by -3 to +4 % scatter otherwise: this explains the board's +2..3 %, +0.35 %, -2.96 % sequence; (c) a resolved cavity is within 0.15 % of the exact answer; the patch converges to about 2.37 GHz (the cavity model's 2.45 is a design formula, not a reference). *What it means for the board:* its runs use slab copper (the robust test) and fine boxes at the antenna, but the ground plane and feed line sit on coarse cells whose size varies by 15 % with the box, so the +-1.6 % and the Dk sign flip are expected to be the same mesh effect, weaker; the test was a whole-board refinement at 1.0 mm (`fb_ref10_bd25/50/75/100`, the same flags as the Dk 4.5 board runs plus `--refine 40 32 90 133 1.0`; 174 400, 123 056, 156 432 and 157 744 steps, 1065 s to 4945 s). **Result: the series resonance is 2.3055, 2.2811, 2.3094, 2.2727 GHz for boxes 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0, against 2.3714, 2.3241, 2.4005, 2.3725 GHz at the default mesh: the box-to-box spread falls from 3.3 % to 1.6 % (+-0.8 %) and the whole curve moves down 2 to 5 %, so the board is not mesh-converged either, and the residual keeps the two-level signature (0.25 and 0.75 near 2.307, 0.5 and 1.0 near 2.277).** A 1.0 mm cell is the equivalent of the patch's 0.8 mm step (box spread 0.7 %), so a finer board refinement is the next test (it was done: see the 0.5 mm series below; the cost estimate of about 8 times the cells here was wrong); until the board is converged its absolute resonance and the domain-size term cannot be quoted better than about +-1 %. *Fix candidates (solver, not applied here):* use `insideShapeOrOn` for sheet layers too (src/board/voxelize.cpp), and a mesh policy that refines around every snapped copper feature (or warns when a copper edge sits on a cell larger than a few dielectric thicknesses). Next experiments if the board test is inconclusive: refine around the feed line and the ground-plane edge only, `lateral_growth_ratio` 1.05, and the ledger term for "mesh around features" per run.


**Reactance slope, defined once, and the real finding: Q depends on the box (2026-11-26, overnight task I; `scripts/antenna/slope.py`, selftest in `selftest.py`).** *Definition:* series resonance = reactance zero crossing nearest the |S11| minimum; slope = least-squares dX/df in ohm per 50 MHz over (fixed) 2.30 to 2.35 GHz and over (own) +-25 MHz around the run's own series resonance; `q_eff = f0 (dX/df) / (2 R)`. *The "13 against 31" in the earlier paragraphs was a definition mismatch, not a factor of two in Q:* measured at the same fixed window our `nd_med_01` has 20.2 (the Z values printed above give 19.9), and openEMS's resonance is at 2.235 GHz so the fixed window sits above it and reads about 0 (r05 -6.7, r04 -46.7). Measured at each run's own resonance: ours at the default box (0.25 wavelengths) **20 to 21** (`nd_med_01`, decay 3e-4 and 1e-4 runs), openEMS **20 to 26** (r04 26.4, r04b 19.8, r05 22.8, r05p 21.3), q_eff 10 against 7 to 10. So at the default box the two codes agree on the slope. *What the table shows instead:* every run at the default box, whatever else changes (mask eps 1.0, copper 17 or 70 um, thickness +-10 %, grading 1.05 to 1.2, PML 4, 16 or 24 cells), has slope 17 to 21, R 46 to 50 ohm, q_eff 8.5 to 10.5; every run at 0.5 wavelengths or more has slope 7 to 14, R 59 to 73 ohm, q_eff 2.2 to 6. Series resonance, R, dip depth and slope by box (all `ti_nol3_nodrop`, medium, 0.1 mm antenna box, Dk 4.5, decay 1e-3): 0.25: 2.3714 GHz, 48.2 ohm, -32.8 dB, slope 20.9, q_eff 10.3; 0.5: 2.3241, 61.2, -20.5, 10.1, 3.9 (PML 16 cells: 2.3261, 60.3, 11.2, 4.3); 0.75: 2.4005, 59.3, -21.4, 13.2, 5.4; 1.0: 2.3725, 73.1, -18.2, 6.8, 2.2. The PML thickness does not move anything (4, 16 and 24 cells: slope 20.9, 20.9; R 47.0, 48.2), so the box distance, not the absorber, is the variable. Reading: the match depth and the Q that the box-0.25 runs report (and with them every 'TI-like' number, R 48 and -33 dB) belong to a box that is too small; the radiation resistance and the Q change by a factor of 2 to 4 between 0.25 and 0.5 wavelengths and are not settled at 1.0 (R 73, slope 6.8 against 59 and 13 at 0.75: the 1.0 run does not continue the trend). Both codes were run with about 0.25 wavelengths of air by default (our default, openEMS `--margin 32` mm), which is why they agree there. Fine and medium runs differ in frequency by 4 to 6 % from openEMS (2.32 to 2.37 against 2.235 to 2.239 GHz) at converged cells: that inter-code frequency gap is still open and is not touched by this finding. The `fd_*` runs at 0.75 and 0.5 in the Dk/thickness/mask set have slope 10.5 to 14.1 and q_eff 3.9 to 6.0 whatever the change, so their small differences are not Q effects. **openEMS margin pair (2026-11-26, same snapshot `ti_nol3_nodrop`, 0.4 mm antenna-box cell, feed box 0.12 mm, coarse 3 mm, `out/openems-case/recheck/m32`, `m94`, 3.18 M and 6.79 M cells, 7 416 s and 11 564 s):** margin 32 mm (0.26 wavelengths) and 94 mm (0.75 wavelengths) give the **same answer**: series resonance 2.2231 GHz both, R 55.0 ohm both, dip -27.07 and -27.08 dB at 2.2175 GHz, slope (own window) 14.4 and 14.3, q_eff 5.8 both, -10 dB band 2.115 to 2.360 and 2.115 to 2.357 GHz. The margin was applied (the cell count doubled). **openEMS has no box dependence between 0.26 and 0.75 wavelengths; our solver does** (R 48 to 59, slope 20.9 to 13.2, resonance +1.2 %, dip -33 to -21 dB over the same change). So the box effect in our results is not near-field physics: it is an artifact of our mesh, our boundary or our port, and the board-level numbers at every box are suspect until it is found. (The openEMS pair is at a coarser cell than our medium runs, so the absolute resonance differs; the point is the constancy.) Next experiments, cheapest first: (1) diff the grids of our 0.25 and 0.5 runs near the board (cell sizes along each axis within 30 mm of the antenna: the graded air mesh may place different cells there, since the number of z cells changed 79 to 89 to 105 between runs); (2) our solver on a simple structure (known answer, task H) at several boxes; (3) our box 0.25 with a much thicker PML (done: 4, 16 and 24 cells change nothing) and with the CPML conductivity profile order or reflection target changed; (4) the lumped port's reference: a port whose two terminals sit in different cells at different box sizes may see a different gap voltage; (5) the time-step: the Courant limit changes with the smallest cell only, which is the same in all boxes, so it is not a candidate.
**Checkpoint and resume: solver side built (2026-11-26; the farm half is not).** `emfdtd --checkpoint-dir DIR [--checkpoint-every SEC] [--resume]`, a `DIR/stop` file or SIGTERM (checkpoint, then exit 75, not an error), `--json` fields `paused` / `resumed_from_step`; format, flags and refusals are in `docs/checkpoint.md`. Built as planned: `Engine::saveState/restoreState` on the CPU and GPU engines (`Solver<T>::saveState`, `GpuSolver::saveState`), the driver's own state in `runOnePort` (`RunOptions::resume/checkpointDue/pauseRequested/onCheckpoint`, `RunResult::paused/resumedFromStep`), `src/app/checkpoint.{hpp,cpp}` (CRC-32C sections, two generations, flushed temporary file then rename, run hash over the post-voxelizer model and the resolved options), a refusal for any phasor list (flags that need one are exit 2). **Bit-identity holds:** a run checkpointed at step 320 of 640 and resumed on a fresh engine gives the identical port voltage, source, every strip, S11 and (CPU and GPU) all six field arrays, compared byte for byte: CPU (`tests/cpu/checkpoint_test.cpp`), GPU on KosmicKrisp and on MoltenVK, 31 exact comparisons each (`tests/gpu/checkpoint_gpu_test.cpp`, `[checkpoint]`, under the lock), and through the command line (`scripts/checkpoint_check.sh`: paused by the stop file at step 64 of 800, resumed, the s11.s1p files are identical, one results folder, `resumed at step` in the summary, the refusals say why). *Sizes and times:* a 0.3 M cell CPU run writes 23 MB in 0.1 s; the real board on the Mac GPU (KosmicKrisp, 8.29 M cells in this configuration, 1200 steps) wrote one 236 MB checkpoint in 1.0 s including the read-back from the device, about 28 bytes per cell, so the 19.7 M cell grid would be about 560 MB and about 2.4 s (extrapolated, not measured); the default 300 s interval costs under 1 % of a run. *Not measured / not built:* a resume of a real-size GPU run through the command line (the unit scenario covers the engines), another device or machine, the farm side (paused job state, `resume`, automatic resume after a worker restart, retention of the checkpoint folder, disk check in `agent doctor`).

**Checkpoint and resume: plan (2026-11-25, user request; same setup only, portability later).** *Why:* `fd_dk42_bd10` ran 45 minutes on the Mac and was killed by the worker's ceiling; the work was lost. *Scope v1:* a headless solve (the farm path) can write checkpoints and be resumed on the same machine, the same device and driver and the same binary; a resume on anything else is refused with the reason. Runs that keep phasor lists (the window's animation views, a headless run with frames or `--view`) are not checkpointed in v1 (they are 60 MB to 1 GB more state and are not farmed); the solver says so and runs without. *State to save (research, file:line in the agent's report):* GPU: the six field buffers (about 480 MB for 19.7 M cells), the CPML psi buffer (about 66 MB), the lumped-port state (inductor current and previous E, a few words per port), the step counter. CPU: the same six arrays, 12 full psi arrays (about 1 GB), port state. Host state in `runOnePort`: steps, progress, peak, the decay window (last 12 chunk maxima), and the whole histories `portVoltage`, `portSource`, `stripVoltage[strip][step]` plus the engine-side per-edge series (`GpuSolver::portV_/portVs_`, `CpuEngine::series_`), which S11 is computed from at the end (there are no incremental S11 sums). Not saved, rebuilt from snapshot and settings: materials, coefficient tables, CPML tables, PEC edges, port constants, source waveforms (a pure function of the step). *Where:* the end of a loop iteration in `runOnePort`, after `onProgress` and the stop checks: the GPU is idle between submits there and the step count is a multiple of 16. New methods `Engine::saveState/restoreState` on the GPU and CPU engines; a versioned file format (magic, version, run hash, backend, device uuid/name/driver, binary version, grid size, steps, dt; sections each with a CRC); written to a temporary file, flushed, renamed, two generations alternating so a crash while writing leaves the older one. *Run identity:* a hash over the prepared model (grid edges, cell materials, material table, PEC edges, port plans), the resolved run options (frequency range, CPML cells, Courant factor, max steps, decay, sample interval) and dt, plus device and binary version; a resume with a different hash is refused. *CLI:* `--checkpoint-dir DIR`, `--checkpoint-every SECONDS` (default 300 when a directory is given), `--resume`, and a control file `DIR/stop`: when it appears the solver writes a checkpoint and exits with status 75 and the line `paused: checkpoint at step N`. A resumed run continues in the same results folder and the summary says "resumed at step N". *Farm:* the worker gives each job a checkpoint folder inside its job folder and passes `--checkpoint-dir`. At the time or step ceiling the worker no longer kills the solver: it writes the stop file, waits up to a minute, and a solver that exits 75 makes the job `paused` (not failed) with its step shown; `resume JOB [--max-minutes N]` queues it again pinned to the same worker (a checkpoint lives only on that machine) with the resume flag; an agent restart or a lost connection resumes automatically from the newest checkpoint when the same worker comes back, and `restart JOB` discards the checkpoint. *Tests:* a CPU scenario (run to N steps, checkpoint, resume; compare to an uninterrupted run: the voltage and source histories and S11 identical), the same on the GPU under the lock (expected identical on the same device; this is an assumption until measured), refusal scenarios (different hash, different device, damaged file, truncated file, older generation used), and farm scenarios with the stub solver (pause at the ceiling, resume, automatic resume after an agent restart, restart discards). *Not in v1:* moving a checkpoint to another machine (needs the backend-neutral layout: the CPML psi layouts differ between CPU and GPU, and fp32 results differ slightly between GPUs), phasor lists, the window's own runs, sweeps (each point would need its own folder).
**Checkpoint and resume, the farm half (2026-11-26, built and tested with the stub solver; not yet deployed: the coordinator and the Mac agent run the earlier code because a job was running, and the Windows worker needs a new bundle).** Wire (backward compatible: an old worker gets none of it): the worker hello carries `features: ["checkpoint"]` and `checkpoints: [{job, step, device}]`; the offer carries `checkpoint: {every, resume}` only for such a worker; new messages `checkpointed` and `paused` (worker to coordinator; `paused` has `why`: `ceiling` or `stopped`), `discard` (coordinator to worker), `resume` and `restart` (client to coordinator, granted like `cancel`). Scheduler: state `paused` (not offered by itself), `hold_worker/hold_until/resume/run_limit/ckpt` per job (saved in `jobs.json`); a lost running job with a reported checkpoint waits for its worker for `hold_s` (default 3600) instead of requeuing anywhere; the worker's next hello decides (checkpoint listed: resume pinned to that worker and GPU; not listed: start from step 0 anywhere); a polite stop is not a failed attempt; a solver that refuses the checkpoint (`checkpoint refused: ...`) makes the job start again from step 0 without costing an attempt; cancel and restart queue a `discard` for the worker (an offline worker is told at its next hello). Worker: a job folder keeps `in/`, `run/`, `ckpt/` and `checkpoint.json` (the step, parsed from the solver's `checkpoint: step N written` line); at the ceiling `ckpt/stop` is written and the solver gets 60 s to exit 75; `graceful_stop` (agent stop, SIGTERM) first sends a heartbeat marking its GPUs not ok (found by a test: the coordinator re-offered the paused job to the stopping worker), then pauses; folders are removed after 7 days or on `discard`, and kept on a lost connection when a checkpoint exists. Decisions recorded: the offer's checkpoint interval is the coordinator's (`serve --checkpoint-every`, default 300 s, clamped 10 to 3600 on the worker); resume after a ceiling is never automatic; `resume --max-minutes` can lower a run's ceiling, never raise the worker's; `agent doctor` wants 2 GiB plus 2 GiB for checkpoints; `agent stop` waits up to 90 s. 13 scheduler tests and 12 end-to-end scenarios (`tests/features/farm_checkpoint.feature`), 317 plugin tests in all. Not built: a Farm tab control for resume (the window shows the reason text and `checkpoint_step` is in `queue --json`; resume is `emfdtd-farm resume JOB`), checkpoints across machines, sweeps.


**Jobs that would not fit (2026-11-26).** `fd_dk42_bd10` (Dk 4.2 at the 1.0 wavelength box, 26.4 M cells) ran 45 minutes on the Mac and was killed by the worker's 45-minute ceiling, which I had set too low: the 1.0 box baseline had taken 2942 s there and Dk 4.2 needs about 1.4 times the steps. The user chose avoiding the failure over checkpoints (there is no checkpoint or resume anywhere; a failed job restarts from step 0). Built: `submit` runs the solver's CPU dry run and sends `estimate: {cells, steps}`; the dry run's step count is an upper bound (3 pulse lengths) and real runs take 0.34 to 0.47 of it at decay 1e-3, 0.66 at 3e-4 and 0.70 at 1e-4, so the estimate is bound x 0.5 / 0.7 / 1.0 (`farm/estimate.py`); for this job it gives 139 318 steps, 63 min on the Mac at 1217 Mcells/s and 47 min on the RTX at 1619, both over a 45-minute ceiling (the Mac really overran). Workers send `limits.max_minutes` in `hello` (older workers do not: their ceiling is the policy's and gates', plus whatever a time-limit failure teaches), the scheduler skips devices where cells x steps / speed x 1.25 exceeds the ceiling and keeps the job queued with a reason that updates as workers come and go, `submit --device WORKER[:GPU]` pins a job, and a time-limit failure now names the worker and the fix. The estimate and pin are scheduler hints and are removed before the offer so an older worker (the Windows bundle) never sees an unknown field. Not built: checkpoint and resume, holding a job for a much faster busy device, and a per-job ceiling override.

**Coordinator hardening, tested (2026-11-25).** `jobs.json` in the farm folder is written atomically whenever the queue changes (`Scheduler.snapshot()`, restored by `Scheduler.restore()` when the coordinator starts: waiting jobs stay queued, done jobs keep their results, a job that was running is queued again with the attempt counted or fails with "gave up", a `receiving` job older than an hour fails with "the input never arrived", a damaged file or row is skipped, never fatal); `gates.json` is re-read before every dispatch through `gates_loader` (a broken file keeps the last good gates); `serve` handles SIGINT and SIGTERM by awaiting `Coordinator.stop()` (turns given back, listeners closed). The tests found two bugs, both fixed: the gates loader raised `SystemExit` for a broken file, which is not an `Exception`, so a running coordinator would have died instead of keeping the last good gates (now `GatesError`, with `SystemExit` only at start-up); and `restore()` crashed on a saved row that was not an object. 9 restart scenarios (queue survives a restart, running job requeued, attempt limit, done jobs and results survive, input timeout, gate added live, broken gate file, damaged jobs.json, file contents), 5 scheduler snapshot scenarios and a three-machine CLI scenario (SIGTERM gives back a held turn, exits 0 within 10 s) were added; 153 plugin tests pass. One unexplained transient: the three-machine sweep scenario timed out once at 120 s while other agents were building and testing on the same machine, and passed 11 times in a row afterwards. Still deploy only when no turn is held.

**Cross-device control on the real board, through the farm (2026-11-25): the RTX 4060 Ti reproduces the Mac.** `nd_med_01` flags submitted as a farm job to the Windows worker (RTX 4060 Ti, driver 621.24, Vulkan 1.4, solver built from the same source tree): the same 117 952 steps, series resonance 2.3714 GHz, parabola 2.3733 GHz, dip -32.82 dB at 2.3746 GHz, and all 101 S11 points differ from the Mac run by at most 4.2e-7 (the patch job gave 4.1e-8; float noise grows with the number of steps). Speed: 609 s, 1832 Mcells/s, **1.5 times the Mac GPU** (about 1200 Mcells/s) now that the buffers are in VRAM. So a result from the RTX is interchangeable with a Mac result at this precision. Further antenna runs that need no particular driver (decay-threshold 3e-4 and 1e-4 at the baseline, to check the stop criterion) are queued on the Mac GPU instead (`nd_dec00003_01`, `nd_dec00001_01`, KosmicKrisp, a frozen copy of the solver binary in `out/antennas/bin/emfdtd_antenna`): the Windows GPU is shared and the user's approval covered the baseline run only, so nothing more goes to it without an explicit go-ahead.

**Driver-to-driver control `nd_kk_01` (2026-11-25): no difference.** The baseline `nd_med_01` flags on KosmicKrisp (`--device 190a0000`; this ran on the `build/release` binary, which was last built before the device-resident buffers and the default-device rule, both of which went into `build/farm`, so it tests the driver switch only; the staged-path equivalence rests on the `[staged]` tests) against the original MoltenVK run: the same 117 952 steps, series resonance 2.3714 GHz in both, dip -32.82 dB at 2.3746 GHz in both, and all 101 S11 points differ by at most 2.7e-8. So the switch to the newest Vulkan driver did not move any result on the real board (and the staged-buffer change, which the unified path does not use, is covered by the `[staged]` tests); the MoltenVK-era series and the KosmicKrisp default are comparable. (The first launch of this run failed silently for an hour because the launcher passed `--device 190a0000` as one word under zsh; fixed with `${=...}`.) 

**Results of the four controls (2026-11-25; all on MoltenVK, the old default driver; `scripts/antenna/subbin.py` series resonance, baseline `nd_med_01` 2.3714 GHz, grid 426 x 281 x 79).** (1) **Mask grid control `nd_mask10_01` (eps 1.0): the grid is identical to the baseline (426 x 281 x 79, same time step) and the series resonance is 2.3711 GHz, -0.3 MHz.** So the mask runs carry no grid artefact (the mask box edges coincide with the refine box), and the mask effect is physics: **eps 1.8 -8.4 MHz (-0.35 %), eps 3.5 at 3 times the thickness -21.4 MHz (-0.90 %)**. Sized estimate for the real mask (25 um at eps 3.5 is the eps 1.8 case; 10 um is 0.4 of that): **-0.14 to -0.35 %, about -0.25 % +- 0.1 %**, deterministic at a fixed grid; the sign is lower frequency, so the model without a mask reads high. (2) **Lateral growth ratio: converged.** 1.05 (grid 502 x 281 x 79) 2.3658 GHz (-0.24 %), -40.8 dB, Z 49.1+0.2j; 1.1 baseline 2.3714; 1.2 (387 x 281 x 79) 2.3760 GHz (+0.19 %), -36.4 dB, Z 48.6-0.5j. Monotonic and within +-0.25 % over a factor of 1.5 in cell growth, R unchanged at 48 to 49 ohm: the growth ratio explains neither the R drift nor the boundary effect. (3) **Boundary distance is not converged, and the two readings I wrote down both fail.** Reactance zero crossing, R at the dip, dip depth: 0.25 wavelength 2.372 GHz, 48 ohm, -32.8 dB (9.5 M cells); 0.5 wavelength 2.324 GHz (-2.0 %), 59.9 ohm, -20.5 dB (14.5 M cells); 0.75 wavelength about 2.40 GHz (+1.2 %), 59.3 ohm, -21.4 dB (20.3 M cells). The resistance converges (about 60 ohm for 0.5 and 0.75 wavelength; the 48 ohm of the default box is the outlier, so the cpw60 R drift was probably the same effect), but the resonance does not: it moves down by 2.0 % and then up by 1.2 % from 0.25 to 0.5 to 0.75 wavelength (2.371, 2.324, 2.400 GHz), non-monotonic, 3 % apart between the two larger boxes. The reactance slope near resonance falls from about 0.4 ohm/MHz in the default box to 0.20 (0.5 wavelength) and 0.25 (0.75 wavelength): the Q roughly halves, R rises from 48 to 60, and the independent `cpw60` run also gave R 62, so two ways of enlarging the model agree: **the default 0.25 wavelength box under-radiates, R about 60 ohm is the converged value, and the baseline's -32.8 dB depth is an artefact of R = 48 sitting near 50 ohm (R = 60 alone gives -20.8 dB, which is what both larger boxes show).** So reading A (0.25 is too close, 0.5 is the truth) is false (0.75 disagrees with 0.5 by 3 %), and reading B holds in the plain sense: **the solver issue to name is open-boundary / domain-size convergence not reached on this board at the medium preset, +-1.5 % in the resonance, non-monotonic in the box size (2.371, 2.324, 2.400 GHz).** Mechanism candidates, untested: weakly absorbed grazing waves travelling along the large ground plane and the board edge (CPML reflection at oblique incidence grows with the distance travelled), and a box resonance between the board and the absorber. (The mesh transition is struck as a candidate: the growth-ratio runs changed it by 1.5 times at a fixed box and moved the resonance by only +-0.25 %.) A weak hint, a noisy proxy and not a finding: steps to reach the 0.001 decay were 117 952 (0.25 wavelength), 120 352 (0.5) and 93 568 (0.75), so the 0.5 wavelength box, which has a low Q by its slope, nevertheless held its energy longest, which points at a resonance of that box size more than at plain absorber reflection. **Predictions written before the runs (2026-11-25):** `nd_bd50_pml16_01` (0.5 wavelength, 16 CPML cells, same everything else): if the series resonance moves by more than 0.5 % from 2.324 GHz the cause is absorber reflection at grazing incidence and the fix is a thicker CPML at large boxes; if it stays within 0.5 % the box itself is resonant (the "8 cells is converged" statement was measured only at 0.25 wavelength where the absorber is in the near field). `nd_bd100_01` (1.0 wavelength): if the resonance returns toward 2.37 GHz the dependence is periodic in about half a wavelength (a standing wave off the boundary); if it sits near 2.40 GHz, 0.75 wavelength is converged and 0.5 was the resonant outlier. `nd_kk_01` (the baseline on KosmicKrisp, `--device 190a0000`): the driver-to-driver difference on the real board; all runs so far used MoltenVK. Until then the mesh-convergence statement for this board is "+-1.5 % in domain size, not the +-0.5 % target" (the open-boundary convergence is the largest solver term in the ledger if these runs do not resolve it), and the band a designer can trust from this solver on this board is wider than the +-0.5 % that the mesh alone would suggest. **Dk caveat (advisor, 2026-11-25):** every statement in this document that the Dk assumption dominates the gap to TI was made with the 0.25 wavelength box, which is not converged (+-1.5 % in the resonance); `nd_bd75_01` at 2.400 GHz is within 1 % of TI's 2.42 GHz at Dk 4.5, which proves nothing about Dk until the domain size is converged. The mask also goes the other way for that story: the model without a mask reads high, and adding it (-0.25 %) widens the gap.

*What TI's reflection figure is (2026-11-25; text of SWRU120D extracted with pdftotext from the copy fetched from ti.com, kept as `out/antennas/swru120d.txt`, private).* Section 2.2 says only that 'the IFA ensures less than 10% reflection of the available power for a bandwidth of more than 300 MHz' and titles Figure 2-8 'Measured Reflection at Feed Point of Antenna'. The note names no instrument, calibration or de-embedding, does not say whether the chip or match was removed, states no stackup, and gives no minimum or frequency in the text (the -24 dB, 2.42 GHz and 295 MHz in this ledger are read from the figure, so their uncertainty is the plot's reading, not stated). So the reference plane is the feed point, which is where our port is, but how TI got a feed-point reading is not documented: R, Z and depth of TI's curve cannot be treated as a known-good value below about 1 to 2 dB of depth or 20 MHz of frequency. A dip of -24 dB or deeper in a model is therefore 'consistent with', not 'equal to', the reference.

*Body runs finished, dropped-trace test, and the comparison metric (2026-11-25).* **Bodies with eps 1 (air) at 0.05 mm** (`f_epsb1_005`, `settings.json` confirms 1.0; full model, Dk 4.2, slab): series resonance 2.4678 GHz, R 77.5 ohm, dip -13.3 dB at 2.4562. The body permittivity series at 0.05 mm is eps 1: 2.4678, eps 4: 2.4508, eps 10: 2.4465 (finer 0.05 mm air cell at eps 10: 2.4439), against 2.3922 with no bodies. Air bodies cannot load the antenna, so the +3.2 percent between no bodies and eps 1 is not dielectric loading. Evidence that the grid differs: all four runs print the same cell counts (708 x 360 x 83) but the time step is 0.0729 ps without bodies and 0.0692 ps with them, whatever their permittivity, and permittivity of 1 or more cannot change a vacuum-limited step, so the line positions differ (body corners are snapped into the grid, `voxelize.cpp` line 273; body cells only replace air cells, so no copper is overwritten). Not verified: which lines moved, and that nothing else in the body voxelization (the 'bodyOwner' bookkeeping, the near-board air) changes the model; the real dielectric effect of the bodies is the small decrease with eps (about -0.9 percent from eps 1 to eps 10). Consequence (provisional until the line positions are compared): the full model's drift with cell size looks like a mesh-topology effect, and the grid line placement alone would move the resonance by about 3 percent at 0.05 mm, the same size as the inter-code gap. The no-bodies model (2.3836, 2.3870, 2.3922 GHz) is the converged estimate of the base physics; the bodies' physical term is small and not resolved separately from this artefact. Not yet done: find which snap lines move the result (the lines are global along x and y, so lines at the body corners change cells around the radiator or the feed), and make the voxelizer's snapping not alter the antenna region. **Dropped-trace test (`nd_02`).** openEMS's log lists 170 and 172 'Unused primitive' lines for `r04`/`r05`, which is 85 and 86 primitives printed twice, so the 85 predicted by the script is right. Removing the 87 matching shapes (39 F.Cu, 34 B.Cu, 14 In1.Cu; no net; at most 12 mm2 each, 20 to 28 vertices; 87 against 85 because a few shapes share a bounding box) from `ti_nol3.json` and rerunning our base slab model at 0.2 mm (same grid 301 x 218 x 73, same time step, decay 0.001): dip -16.07 dB at 2.2277 GHz (Z 64.4 - 10.9j), series resonance 2.4666 GHz, R 73.2 ohm; with the traces (`sd_02`): dip -21.15 dB at 2.2766, series resonance 2.3034, R 61.9. The two metrics disagree on direction: the dip moved to openEMS's value (2.2325) but the zero crossing moved up 7 percent, because with the traces removed the reactance stays small and negative across the band and the crossing lands wherever it falls. Z(f) at 2.2/2.3/2.4/2.5 GHz: `sd_02` 49.7-25.0j, 61.4-0.4j, 80.2+9.7j, 83.5+14.0j; `nd_02` 59.7-16.5j, 82.1-4.7j, 81.3-12.7j, 73.3+9.3j; openEMS 0.1 mm (9.5 ns series) 52.4-12.8j, 73.9+33.9j, 126.2-40.8j, 75.0-9.7j; openEMS 0.2 mm 51.0-21.4j, 102.1+13.0j, 77.2-25.5j, 64.8+0.8j. openEMS's reactance changes sign three times between 2.2 and 2.5 GHz (the ring-down is cut at -15 to -19 dB), so its zero crossings above the dip are not reliable and the series resonance is not a safe comparator; compare dip frequency and Z at fixed frequencies instead, and only below about 2.3 GHz for the truncated openEMS series. A test of whether the removed shapes touch a pour (bounding boxes against the large pours) was uninformative: all 87 overlap a board-sized pour's bounding box. So: 87 small unnetted shapes up to 60 mm from the port move our S11 by 2 percent at the dip and change the reactance curve, so they are not irrelevant. They are on the real board, so the model that includes them is the faithful one; openEMS's full-geometry number is the answer for a geometry the board does not have, and the matched run (`nd_02`) is the one that compares the solvers. The matched comparison gives agreement on the dip frequency and a disagreement of about 10 ohm in R. **`nd_01` (matched geometry, 0.1 mm, 8.3 M cells, 139 744 steps, 928 s):** deepest dip -17.0 dB at 2.4888 GHz (Z 66.4 - 0.9j), series resonance 2.4909 GHz, R 66.6 ohm; at fixed frequencies Z = 52.8-56.4j (2.10), 61.0-16.7j (2.20), 72.1-8.9j (2.25), 82.9-3.7j (2.30 GHz), |S11| -15.0 dB at 2.20 and -12.1 at 2.30. openEMS 0.1 mm full series: 46.3-40.9j, 53.9-13.0j, 54.0+5.7j, 73.6+36.8j at the same frequencies, |S11| -17.8, -23.4 at 2.20 and 2.25. So at matched geometry our reactance rises by about 5 ohm from 2.25 to 2.30 GHz where openEMS's rises by about 31 ohm: the two codes disagree on the slope of Z(f) near resonance (a loaded Q), and ours has no clear series resonance below 2.49 GHz (the model's 0.2 mm run, `nd_02`, had its dip at 2.2277 and its crossing at 2.4666; at 0.1 mm the dip moved to 2.4888, so the matched-geometry model is not mesh-stable in our solver either). This is an open item, not a finding about the antenna. Untested candidates, cheapest first on our side: the coarse cell (about 4 to 5 mm derived from 10 cells per wavelength, against 2.0 mm in openEMS; `mesh_preset=medium` halves it), dielectric loss (our per-layer loss tangent against openEMS's conductivity at 2.4 GHz; loss tangent 0 in both), the absorbing boundary distance (0.25 wavelength in both) and the CPML thickness, and the port (a 0.3 mm wide vertical lumped port through the whole stack in both, no inner copper at its position).

Patent check, first pass (2026-11-24; Google Patents driven by Playwright with the installed Chrome, scripts and hit lists in git-ignored `out/patent-search/`; priority after 2006-01-01, four queries on FDTD with conformal, subcell, PEC, non-uniform grid, circuit board and permittivity averaging; a secondary source, so not a legal-status record and not a freedom-to-operate opinion). Hits that matter: (a) US20250086347A1, X Development, 'Efficient and accurate subpixel smoothing for FDTD', a pending application (filed 2023-09-08, nothing granted shown); claim 1 requires determining each voxel's permittivity from feature parameters while its field values load from main memory, which our voxelizer (permittivity precomputed on the grid) does not do as read, but any later interface-averaging scheme must be compared with it before implementation. (b) Chinese filings, claim 1 read from Google's machine translation: CN121920161B (UESTC, granted 2026) a non-uniform conformal main grid with a refined subgrid and main/sub interface; CN117892585A (application) a PEC conformal update by relative area and side-length ratio with a time-step reduction rule and a minimum-step limit, which is the Dey-Mittra family we would otherwise implement; CN115221760A (application) a smoothing of conductivity and permittivity of stepped cells at a tissue/air interface by volume ratio over a 3x3x3 neighbourhood; CN119337675A text not retrievable. All are territorial to China (no family member seen elsewhere), so they do not constrain a US or EU release but would matter for a Chinese one; the conformal ones would need a read of the full claims before any such implementation. (c) EP2287757A1, Fujitsu, multilevel-multigrid, status ceased. (d) US20180113971A1 (Mentor) is a layout-cropping and boundary-element method with a termination structure, not FDTD: not relevant. TWI737741B (Coventor family) claims were not retrievable and remain unread. Second pass, five broader queries (conformal/cut-cell/fractional PEC, subcell/thin wire/strip, staircase correction, graded mesh with antenna or PCB, GPU FDTD with PCB or antenna; no country filter, the first attempt to add one failed): the hits were dominated by optics, plasmonics, biomedical and Chinese filings; the only EM-solver-adjacent US hits were Cadence US8631381B2 (power delivery network analysis, not read) and the Coventor family (unread). Still no US or EP grant claiming a conformal PEC cell-fill scheme surfaced, but Google's full-text ranking is imprecise and this is not a clearance. Third pass: Coventor's TWI737741B/CN109564702B claim 1 is virtual semiconductor manufacturing with an implicit geometric representation (not an FDTD solver, not relevant); CN119337675A claim 1 is a CDI-FDTD update on a non-uniform grid with a smooth fine-to-coarse transition (China only). Espacenet returned a blank page to headless Chrome, so the legal-status check there was not done and I did not try to get round it; it needs a person in a normal browser. Still open: full claim sets of the Chinese conformal hits, status in USPTO Patent Public Search and Espacenet, a qualified review. The rule in CLAUDE.md (no conformal or subcell PEC scheme before the check and review) stays. 

**System UI font (2026-11-25, the user's request).** The window now draws with the operating system's UI font instead of Dear ImGui's built-in bitmap font. `src/viz/system_font.hpp` (`systemUiFont()`, one implementation file per platform: CoreText on macOS, the Windows Fonts folder, fontconfig and `gsettings` on Linux) finds a font file at run time; nothing is bundled, so the Licensing table is unchanged. The atlas (`buildFonts` in `apps/emfdtd/ui.cpp`) loads it at the existing text-size pixel size and the physical-pixel rasterizer density, with the built-in font merged behind it for missing characters, and falls back to the built-in font alone. Setting `ui_font` (View: system or builtin) and `EMFDTD_UI_FONT` choose; the old screenshot check pins the built-in font. On this Mac the system font is San Francisco (`/System/Library/Fonts/SFNS.ttf`, variable font: stb_truetype reads the default regular instance). Screenshots at 720x520 and 720x900, 100 % and 150 % text size, show every line wrapping inside the window (proportional text is narrower than the monospace font, so there is more room, not less). Windows and Linux lookups are untested.

## Investigations queued (2026-11-26; the user may or may not start them today; nothing here is built)

**A. GPU memory-traffic audit (bandwidth is what limits us).** Fields already stay in device memory: discrete GPUs use `Residency::Device` with a host staging copy (156.8 s to 3.2 s on the RTX, 2026-11-25); unified-memory GPUs keep host-visible buffers on purpose. What is not measured: (1) bytes that cross the host/GPU boundary per step (port voltage, decay check, progress counters; the solver submits 64 steps at a time, so one read per submit is cheap and one per step is not); instrument one run on both devices and report bytes per step. (2) Bytes moved per cell-step against the measured copy bandwidth: we reach about 5 Mcells/s per GB/s on the M1 Pro (about 1000 Mcells/s on 200 GB/s) and about 6.8 on the RTX 4060 Ti (1950 on 288); count what each kernel reads and writes (material tables, CPML state on cells that need none) before trying tiling (CLAUDE.md: only if measured to help). (3) Still host-visible and slow on a discrete GPU: viewer buffers (`viz_vk`) and `gpu_line2d`; small effect on farm jobs. Done when: a table of bytes per cell-step per kernel, the host traffic per step, and a decision on whether anything is worth changing.

**B. Mesh policy for copper edges (the "shortcut" for convergence).** Result so far: with the whole board refined, the series resonance at boxes 0.25/0.5 is 2.3714/2.3241 GHz (default mesh), 2.3055/2.2811 (1.0 mm), 2.2829/2.2856 (0.5 mm); Dk 4.5 at 0.5 mm: 2.2829, 2.2856, 2.2838 GHz at boxes 0.25, 0.5, 0.75 (spread 0.12 %; box 1.0 still queued); Dk 4.2 at 0.5 mm: 2.3515, 2.3358, 2.3348, 2.3387 GHz at boxes 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1.0, so the Dk shift is +3.00, +2.20, +2.23, +2.33 % (the sign flip of the default mesh is gone; boxes 0.5 to 1.0 agree to 0.17 %, box 0.25 is the outlier, 0.7 % higher: provisional box term about +-0.1 % at boxes of 0.5 wavelengths and up, and a smaller box is not trusted; the Dk 4.5 series is 2.2829, 2.2856, 2.2838, 2.2855 GHz, spread 0.12 %). The grid-offset runs (0.5 mm cells, box 0.5, Dk 4.5; `scripts/antenna/grid_offset.py`): board shifted by 0, 0.10, 0.17 and 0.33 mm gives 2.2856, 2.3073, 2.3027 and 2.3007 GHz: the three shifted runs agree to 0.3 % and the unshifted one is 0.8 % lower, a range of 0.95 %, so the global grid phase is worth about +-0.5 % at 0.5 mm cells and the box study (one grid phase) could not see it. A first pause and resume on a real job: the RTX job `fb05_dk42_bd75w` paused at its 45-minute ceiling (step 134 656), was resumed with `resume JOB --max-minutes 45`, and finished with a result 0.04 % from its neighbor; the Mac job `fb05_dk42_bd100` paused at its 120-minute ceiling and resumed the same way. The board runs use slab copper, so the sheet-copper fix (872d69b) did not change them: the gain is refinement. Options: (1) fine cells only in a band around each snapped copper edge (ground-plane edge, feed line, antenna), graded out with `lateral_growth_ratio`, as a dry-run suggestion like `--refine-gaps`; estimate the cell count with `--dry-run` on `--refine` boxes around the feed and ground edge only, before claiming a saving (measured by dry runs 2026-11-26, box 0.5: whole-board refinement at 0.5 mm 21.7 M cells, at 0.25 mm 32.9 M, with the 0.25 mm band y 100 to 133 only 25.5 M; the board is 105 cells thick and the 35 um copper slab sets the time step, 7.7e-14 s at 0.5 mm and 7.2e-14 s at 0.25 mm, so a finer in-plane cell costs about 1.5 times the cells and few extra steps: the estimate of 8 times in the first draft of this paragraph was wrong; edge-band refinement saves only about a quarter at 0.25 mm); (2) openEMS's "thirds rule" (mesh lines 1/3 inside and 2/3 outside a metal edge instead of on it; behavior read from the openEMS docs, not its source, per the clean-room rule; the docs report it was both more accurate and cheaper in a notch-filter tutorial): our solver (`snapToGeometry`) and our openEMS cross-check (`scripts/antenna/openems_case.py`, snapped edges merged into the lines) both put a line exactly on the edge, so a shared edge treatment cannot show up as a gap between them. Test: our solver and openEMS on the patch and cavity known-answer cases with lines on the edge versus thirds, at matched cells. Thresholds must be data (settings registry), never a hard-coded cell size; no conformal or subcell PEC (patent check, CLAUDE.md).

**C. The 2.7 % gap to openEMS.** Our settled series resonance is about 2.28 GHz, openEMS (coarser cell, 0.4 mm, margin-independent: 2.2231 GHz at margins 32 and 94 mm) 2.22. Suspects in order of cost: thirds-versus-on-edge (B), the lumped port and its reference gap, openEMS's own mesh error at 0.4 mm (rerun it at 0.2 mm), port impedance and feed placement. The known-answer cases (patch, closed cavity) with exact answers are the discriminator: run both codes at matched cells and compare each to the exact value.

**D. Hardware, if a new Mac is bought (researched 2026-11-26; speed figures are bandwidth scaling from our M1 Pro, plus or minus 30 %, nothing measured on these chips).** Memory bandwidth, not GPU core count or generation, predicts our speed. Bandwidth from Apple's pages: M4 120, M4 Pro 273, M4 Max 410 (binned, Wikipedia only) or 546; M5 153, M5 Pro up to 307, M5 Max 460 or 614; M6 (base only, no Pro or Max announced) 170 (aggregator sites, dates conflict); our M1 Pro 200. Estimated speed: M4 600-660 Mcells/s, M4 Pro 1350-1500, M5 Pro 1500-1700, M5 Max 2300-3400, M6 850-950, M1 Pro 1000 (measured). Pick: M5 Pro with 48-64 GB (M4 Pro as the safer choice because M4 is in LunarG's own CI); two Pros on the farm beat one Max for independent convergence runs, a Max only if one run must exceed 64 GB. KosmicKrisp: Apple silicon with macOS 26 or later (Metal 4); LunarG's XDC 2026 post says Vulkan-conformant on M1-M5 and A18 Pro, CI on M1, M2, M4; M6 and Pro/Max variants are not mentioned; the Mesa docs say M1/M2 on macOS 26 show the most issues. Ports: M5 Pro and Max MacBook Pros have Thunderbolt 5; base M4/M5 have Thunderbolt 4 (Apple's spec pages); not checked for the M4 Pro. After buying: `check.sh --gpu` under both Apple drivers, then one 0.5 mm board run to compare with the estimate.

**E. TinyGPU (tinygrad's eGPU driver for Apple silicon) does not help the plugin.** Its docs (docs.tinygrad.org/tinygpu) require macOS 13+, a USB4/Thunderbolt port and an AMD RDNA3+ or NVIDIA Ampere+ GPU, and show use only through tinygrad (`DEV=AMD|NV`); no Vulkan, Metal or OpenCL is mentioned, so our Vulkan solver cannot use an eGPU behind it. Using it for FDTD would mean a second solver backend written in tinygrad. The RTX 4060 Ti (Ada, after Ampere) is inside the stated NVIDIA family but unverified, and a news guide says practical support is narrow. Not planned; revisit if a Vulkan route appears.

**TI CC2430DB as a KiCad project (2026-11-26, the user's request).** Directory `/Volumes/CrucialX9/Projects/ti-cc2430db` (outside the repository, private: TI's package is not licensed for redistribution here). (1) `kicad-cli pcb import --format cadstar` on TI's `CC2430DB_1_3.cpa` (KiCad 10.0.7) gives `cc2430db.kicad_pcb`: 115 footprints, 533 pads, 496 nets, but no tracks, vias or zones (the archive has parts and netlist, not routed copper; the VCC plane layer has no net, spacing classes and fonts not carried over); parts sit about (-499, 498) mm from the origin (CADSTAR offset). (2) `scripts/gerber/snapshot_to_kicad.py` writes a copper-only board from the Gerber-built snapshot (`out/antennas/swrr034c/ti_nol3_nodrop.json`): 419 filled zones, 58 vias, the outline and the stackup; `cc2430db_copper.kicad_pcb` loads in KiCad 10 (DRC: 644 violations, expected with no nets) and reads back through KiCad's Python with copper area per layer equal to the snapshot (ratio 1.00000 on F.Cu, In1.Cu and B.Cu), 58 vias, the outline. It is the same model, not an independent one: its use is the plugin's export on a complicated antenna (open it in KiCad on a copy, export, diff against the snapshot) and a board to look at.\n\n**IFA validation coupons for OSH Park (2026-11-26; designed, files in `/Volumes/CrucialX9/Projects/ti-cc2430db/coupon/`; generator `boards/ifa-coupon/generate.py`; the user places the order; the TI-derived antenna dimensions are SWRU120D's, see the licence caveat below).** *Purpose:* a measured S11 of an antenna whose every input is known, to test the solver against reality (it tests the solver against OSH Park's board, not against TI's 2.42 GHz: the stackup differs). *Boards (OSH Park 4-layer, ENIG, sets of 3):* `antenna` 80 x 37 mm (edge-launch SMA at x = 0, a 70 mm 50 ohm line, TI's inverted-F with its ground edge at x = 70 mm); `cal_pads` 72 x 14 mm (same launch and line, ending in a 0603 pad pair: unpopulated = OPEN, 49.9 ohm = LOAD, 0 ohm = SHORT); `cal_short` 72 x 14 mm (same launch and line ending in a copper strip into the pour: a SHORT without a resistor's inductance). Three designs, not four, because open and load share one design (saves about $15 of area). *Why these:* the cal boards are the one-port calibration standards at the antenna's feed plane (x = 69.5 mm): measure open, short and load through the same SMA and line, calibrate (SOL), then the antenna's S11 refers to the feed plane the solver's port models; the same boards are the real-hardware known-load control of the port model. *Choices:* stackup from `data/fab_profiles.json` `oshpark_4layer` (re-read 2026-11-26 on docs.oshpark.com/services/four-layer: 1.7 mil outer and 0.68 mil inner copper, 7.87 mil 2113 prepreg Dk 3.61 at 1 GHz, 39 mil core Dk 3.87 from OSH Park's stackup diagram, 63 mil nominal); feed line TI's 0.46 mm with 0.45 mm gaps, which gives 49.9 ohm on the 0.2 mm prepreg (grounded coplanar waveguide, Simons closed form; microstrip alone 48.8); ground on all four layers up to the antenna's ground edge (stitched by 0.3 mm vias), none beyond it (TI's strip), In1 cleared under the SMA center pad (the reference plane there is In2, 1.2 mm down, so the 1.5 mm pad is near 50 ohm instead of 17); copper kept 0.4 mm from every board edge (OSH Park's 15 mil edge keepout); solder mask open over the feed line and the antenna so the modeled copper is the fabricated copper (a silk bracket as long as the arm, 25.58 mm, on the masked board end for calipering); a 10 mm silk ruler; ground size 80 x 37 mm with the SMA 70 mm from the antenna (the cable is then not the counterpoise; cable interaction is an error term the solver does not model: use a ferrite choke and a long cable, keep the cable away from the antenna, and compare with the cable moved). *Checks done:* `kicad-cli pcb drc` against OSH Park's 4-layer rules (5 mil trace and space, 10 mil drill, 4 mil annular ring, 15 mil edge keepout): 0 errors, 0 unconnected, warnings only (the deliberate solder-mask aperture over the line and pour edges, one silk-on-opening note); Gerbers exported with `kicad-cli pcb export gerbers` and read back with `scripts/gerber/gerber.py`: the copper of every layer equals the solver snapshot's to 0.06 % (`boards/ifa-coupon/verify_gerbers.py`). OSH Park accepts a `.kicad_pcb` directly but processes it with KiCad 9.x and does not refill zones, and these files are KiCad 10: **upload the Gerber zips** (the names KiCad writes are detected automatically). Cost (OSH Park 4-layer: $10 per square inch per set of 3, 9 to 14 days, verified on the service page): antenna 4.59 in2 = $45.88, each cal board 1.56 in2 = $15.62, total about $77 for the three designs (9 boards). Parts to buy: edge-launch SMA jack for 1.6 mm boards, Amphenol 132289 (pad pattern from KiCad's footprint library, **not yet checked against Amphenol's drawing**: compare before ordering); 0603 resistors 49.9 ohm and 0 ohm. *PREDICTIONS, written before any antenna run (2026-11-26):* (1) the solver, with the port at the feed plane at converged settings (slab copper, medium mesh, whole board refined to 0.5 mm, boxes 0.5 and 0.75, decay 1e-3, plus one grid offset), will put the antenna's series resonance at **2.35 GHz, +-0.10 GHz (+-4 %)**, a reasoned first guess: the same antenna on the TI board model gave 2.28 GHz (box 0.5, Dk 4.5), and OSH Park's lower Dk (3.6 to 3.9) raises the frequency, partly offset by the thicker board and the absent ground under the strip; grid placement and mesh contribute +-0.5 %, the published Dk +-0.1 (about +-1.5 % in frequency), the unmodeled solder mask (open over the antenna) 0, ENIG and copper roughness a little lower. (2) The known-load controls in the solver (port at the SMA pad, coarse mesh): open near 0 degrees and short near 180 degrees extrapolated to zero frequency (within 45), the two shorts (resistor and strip) agreeing, the 49.9 ohm load better than -15 dB over 2.0 to 2.8 GHz; failing any of these blocks the order. (3) Measured against the solver after SOL calibration, I expect the resonance within +-3 % and the dip depth 3 to 6 dB shallower than the simulation (conductor loss and the cable are not modeled); a difference larger than 5 % in frequency points at the port or feed model, not at the mesh. *Licence caveat:* the antenna's dimensions are TI SWRU120D's (a public application note; TI allows its antenna files only for developing an application that uses TI products): the generator and files stay private until that is settled.

**F. Farm and release items still open.** `check.sh --gpu` on both Apple drivers (overdue); pause and resume has not run on a real job (the RTX smoke job finished under the 300 s checkpoint interval; a Mac job over 10 minutes with `agent stop` would test it); Windows r7 agent and build7 solver are installed and online (the Windows session closed 2026-11-26); the error ledger (sign, size, uncertainty) is written after the 0.5 mm series completes; the American-spelling conversion stays deferred until the model is reliable.

**G. Live job statistics from the coordinator (planned 2026-11-26 at the user's request; later, after the science queue; nothing built).** Today `queue` shows a label, a state, a percent and the last progress line, and `agent status` shows only the worker's one current job; there is no steps-per-second, ETA, run time or history, and finished jobs keep their speed only as text. Facts the plan builds on: the solver prints `progress: STEPS steps, about P%, SECONDS s` at least every 5 s (`apps/emfdtd/main.cpp`, `onProgress`); the worker forwards each line as a `progress` message (`worker.py`); the coordinator keeps only the last line and the parsed percent (`Scheduler.progress`); `Job.public()` (`scheduler.py`) is what `queue --json` returns; the job spec already carries the dry-run `estimate` (cells, steps, which is an upper bound: finished runs took about half of it) and the lease and resume history is in the scheduler. **No solver or wire change is needed for version 1:** the coordinator parses the whole progress line, not only the percent. **Compatibility is not a constraint (the user, 2026-11-26: nothing has been released publicly, so older plugin and agent versions need no support):** change the farm protocol, `queue --json` and the job store freely and in step on every machine, drop compatibility shims such as the worker's `--checkpoint-dir` probe once all machines run the new build, and bump the farm's schema number instead of keeping old keys. *Per-job fields to add to `public()`:* `steps` (latest), `elapsed_s` (solver clock of the current run segment), `rate_steps_s` (smoothed over the last minute from the last samples, not the lifetime average, so a slowed GPU shows), `mcells_s` (rate times the estimate's cell count), `eta_s` (remaining steps from the solver's own percent, `steps / percent`, once the percent is at least 5; before that from the estimate, marked as such; "about", because the percent is how far the signal has decayed and is not linear), `segments` (run segments so far: first run plus resumes), `queued_s`, `run_s` (wall clock, all segments), `finished` (time), `ckpt_age_s` (since the newest checkpoint was reported), and for finished jobs the final steps, run time and Mcells/s kept in `jobs.json` so history survives a coordinator restart. *Per-device fields in the device listing:* current job, its rate against the device's measured Mcells/s (a job far below it points at a throttling or shared GPU), time left on the ceiling of this segment (so a pause is predicted before it happens). *New commands:* `emfdtd-farm stats [JOB]` (one job in detail: the fields above plus the recent rate history), `emfdtd-farm jobs [--since HOURS] [--state S]` (one row per job: label, state, device, segments, steps, run time, Mcells/s, exit reason; the history report asked for 2026-11-26), `queue --watch` (redraws every few seconds until the active jobs end; polls, no new connection type). *Later, only if polling proves too slow for the window:* a `subscribe` message on the client connection that pushes job events (progress sample, state change, pause) so the Farm tab updates without polling; the Farm tab then gets columns for rate, ETA and a progress bar, through the `apps/emfdtd/ui.cpp` helpers (named nodes, `px()` sizes, no overflow at 720x520, screenshot check with `scripts/ui_farm_check.sh`). *Policy and safety:* a client sees only its own jobs and the device list (as now); statistics add no file access and no new port; a stats read never touches the scheduler's state (no effect on placement). *Tests (BDD, written first):* the stub solver prints progress lines at a scripted pace, and the scenarios assert a rate within 10 %, an ETA within 15 % once the percent is at least 5, `segments` 2 after a pause and resume with the elapsed clock continuing, `ckpt_age_s` growing between checkpoint messages, a restarted coordinator keeping finished jobs' final statistics, the fields being absent (not wrong) for a job that has not started, and `queue --json` parsing with the new keys; pure functions (smoothing, ETA) get unit tests in `plugin/tests/`. *Cost:* about a day: scheduler fields and parsing (small), CLI commands (small), tests, `docs/farm.md` and `docs/cli-json.md` entries; the Farm tab columns add about half a day. *Open questions:* how long to keep the rate history per job (suggest the last 120 samples, in memory only); whether `jobs` should read the worker's `work/jobs` folders for disk use (not in version 1).
